46. nce-s-eth-046 · Research and Program Evaluation · informed consent and its required elements · easy · In review

A counselor researcher is preparing a consent form for a study of coping in caregivers of people with dementia. Which statement is a required element of informed consent for research participants?
A. Participants may decline or withdraw at any time without penalty ✔ KEY
G.2.a lists the freedom to withdraw without penalty as a required element.
B. Participants must finish the study once they have signed the form
Participation is voluntary throughout; withdrawal must be allowed.
C. Participants give up the right to see results in exchange for pay
Payment does not remove participant rights, and results may be shared.
D. Participants are told the hypotheses only after data have been used
Purposes and procedures are explained up front, except in justified deception.
Rationale: G.2.a requires researchers to explain the purpose and procedures, risks, benefits, alternatives, limits of confidentiality, the format and audience for results, and that participants are free to withdraw at any time without penalty. Consent is an ongoing process, not a one-time signature.
References: ACA Code of Ethics — G.2.a Informed Consent in Research · Erford (Research and Evaluation in Counseling) — Informed consent and voluntary participation
☐ Approve☐ Revise☐ RetireNotes:

47. nce-s-eth-047 · Research and Program Evaluation · confidentiality and its limits · medium · In review

A counselor in a community agency plans to survey clients about service satisfaction and later publish the findings. The agency has access to a university institutional review board (IRB). Which step is MOST appropriate before collecting data?
A. Begin the survey now, since satisfaction surveys of clients carry no risk to them
Research with clients involves risks, including pressure to participate and privacy.
B. Ask clients to put their names on surveys so their answers can be checked later
Collecting names without need raises risk; data should be de-identified when possible.
C. Collect the data first and seek a review only if the results turn out to be publishable
Review occurs before data collection, not after results are known.
D. Seek IRB review and plan how consent is obtained and how data will be kept private ✔ KEY
Section G calls for review of research and for protecting participants' consent and privacy.
Rationale: Section G directs counselors to follow applicable law and institutional review requirements, to obtain informed consent, and to protect the confidentiality of participant information. Planned publication makes this research rather than routine program evaluation, so IRB review is sought before data collection, and data are de-identified and stored securely.
References: ACA Code of Ethics — Section G, Research and Publication (institutional review and confidentiality of information) · Erford (Research and Evaluation in Counseling) — Institutional review boards and protection of participants
⚑ Check: Check whether a specific G subsection (e.g., G.1.b or G.2.d) should be cited for IRB review and data confidentiality.
☐ Approve☐ Revise☐ RetireNotes:

48. nce-s-eth-048 · Research and Program Evaluation · ethical decision-making models · medium · In review

A counselor's outcome study finds that a group program her agency promotes did not reduce symptoms more than a waitlist. The agency director suggests leaving these results out of the annual report. What should the counselor do?
A. Omit the findings, since agency staff must support the programs their employer runs
Loyalty to an employer does not permit withholding results of professional value.
B. Adjust the analysis until the program shows a positive effect worth reporting on
Manipulating analyses to produce a result is a form of data distortion (G.4.a).
C. Report the results accurately, since unfavorable findings may not be withheld ✔ KEY
G.4.b states that results reflecting unfavorably on programs are not withheld.
D. Report only the subgroups in which scores improved and describe them as overall gains
Selective reporting misrepresents results and violates G.4.a.
Rationale: G.4.a requires counselors to plan, conduct, and report research accurately without distorting data or biasing results. G.4.b states that results of professional value are reported, and results that reflect unfavorably on institutions, programs, services, or vested interests are not withheld.
References: ACA Code of Ethics — G.4.b Obligation to Report Unfavorable Results · ACA Code of Ethics — G.4.a Accurate Results
☐ Approve☐ Revise☐ RetireNotes:

Intake, Assessment, and Diagnosis

48 items · NCE weight 12% (19 of 160 scored items)

49. nce-s-ass-001 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · intake interview structure · easy · In review

A counselor at a community agency begins a first session with a 34-year-old client who has been referred for "stress." Before asking about presenting concerns or history, what should the counselor address?
A. A full family genogram covering at least three generations
A genogram can be useful later, but it is not the opening task and does not secure informed consent.
B. Informed consent, including the limits of confidentiality ✔ KEY
Clients need to know their rights, the nature of services, and the limits of confidentiality before disclosing sensitive information.
C. A provisional DSM-5-TR diagnosis for the referral form
A diagnosis requires assessment data that have not yet been gathered; it cannot come first.
D. Long-term treatment goals the client hopes to reach
Goal setting follows assessment of the presenting problem; it is premature at the very start.
Rationale: Intake interviews typically open with informed consent and an explanation of confidentiality and its limits, then move to presenting problem, history, mental status, risk, and goals. Discussing limits first lets the client make an informed choice about what to disclose.
References: ACA Code of Ethics — A.2.a Informed Consent; B.1.d Explanation of Limitations
☐ Approve☐ Revise☐ RetireNotes:

50. nce-s-ass-002 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · mental status examination · hard · In review

When asked why he came in, a 61-year-old client describes his commute, the parking garage, a conversation with a neighbor, and the weather in great detail before finally explaining that he has been sleeping poorly. How should the counselor record this in the mental status examination?
A. Tangentiality, noted under thought process
Tangential speech drifts away and does not return to the original question; this client does return to it.
B. Flight of ideas, noted under thought content
Flight of ideas involves rapid, accelerated shifts, usually with pressured speech, and is a thought process finding, not content.
C. Circumstantiality, noted under thought process ✔ KEY
Excessive, irrelevant detail that eventually reaches the point is circumstantiality, a disturbance of thought process.
D. Perseveration, noted under perceptual disturbance
Perseveration is repetition of the same response, and it is not a perceptual finding.
Rationale: The MSE separates thought process (how ideas connect) from thought content (what the person thinks about). Circumstantial speech is roundabout but goal-directed in the end; tangential speech never gets back to the goal. Both are recorded under thought process.
References: Hays (Assessment) — Mental status examination: thought process
☐ Approve☐ Revise☐ RetireNotes:

51. nce-s-ass-003 · Assessment and Testing · suicide and violence risk assessment · medium · In review

During intake, a 19-year-old college student says, "Sometimes I wish I could just go to sleep and not wake up." What should the counselor do FIRST?
A. Ask directly about thoughts of suicide, plans, intent, and means ✔ KEY
Passive death wishes call for direct, specific questions about ideation, plan, intent, means, and past behavior to estimate risk.
B. Avoid the subject so as not to put the idea of suicide in her mind
Asking about suicide does not increase risk; avoiding it leaves risk unassessed.
C. Arrange an involuntary hospitalization before the session ends
Level of care is decided after assessment; a passive wish alone does not establish need for involuntary admission.
D. Refer her to a psychiatrist and end the intake for the day
Referral without assessment abandons the risk question and may leave the client without support.
Rationale: A wish to be dead is the lowest rung of suicidal ideation on structured tools such as the C-SSRS, but it requires follow-up. The counselor asks plainly about active thoughts, method, intent, plan, preparatory behavior, and prior attempts, then decides on next steps based on that picture.
References: C-SSRS — Ideation severity: wish to be dead through active ideation with plan and intent
☐ Approve☐ Revise☐ RetireNotes:

52. nce-s-ass-004 · Assessment and Testing · suicide and violence risk assessment · medium · In review

A 47-year-old veteran reports recurring suicidal thoughts without a current plan or intent. He mentions that he keeps a loaded handgun in his nightstand. Which step is MOST important to include in his safety plan?
A. A written promise that he will not harm himself before next week
No-harm contracts lack evidence of protective value and do not address lethal means.
B. A list of reasons for living to read when he feels hopeless
Reasons for living can help, but they do not reduce access to a highly lethal method.
C. A plan to increase his exercise and sleep routine this month
Wellness activities are reasonable adjuncts but leave the most urgent risk factor untouched.
D. Lethal means counseling to store the gun outside the home ✔ KEY
Firearm access sharply raises lethality; collaborative means restriction is a core safety-plan step.
Rationale: Firearms are the most lethal common method of suicide, and reducing access during high-risk periods saves lives. The Stanley-Brown Safety Planning Intervention includes making the environment safe, such as having a trusted person hold a firearm or storing it locked away from home.
References: Stanley-Brown SPI — Step 6 (final step): Making the environment safe, including lethal means
☐ Approve☐ Revise☐ RetireNotes:

53. nce-s-ass-005 · Assessment and Testing · DSM-5-TR diagnostic structure and specifiers · easy · In review

A counselor trained years ago asks a colleague where to record the Axis V Global Assessment of Functioning (GAF) score when using DSM-5-TR. What is the BEST answer?
A. On Axis V, as in earlier versions of the manual
DSM-5-TR has no Axis V; the multiaxial system is gone.
B. On Axis IV, together with psychosocial stressors
There are no axes in DSM-5-TR, so Axis IV does not exist either.
C. Nowhere; DSM-5-TR uses nonaxial documentation ✔ KEY
DSM-5 dropped the multiaxial system and the GAF; diagnoses are listed together without axes.
D. In a required appendix scored from zero to 100
No GAF appendix exists; the GAF was removed from the manual.
Rationale: DSM-5 and DSM-5-TR replaced the five-axis system with nonaxial documentation. Psychosocial and contextual factors are noted with codes from "Other Conditions That May Be a Focus of Clinical Attention," and the GAF was dropped; the WHODAS 2.0 is offered as a measure of disability.
References: DSM-5-TR — Section I: Use of the Manual (nonaxial documentation)
☐ Approve☐ Revise☐ RetireNotes:

54. nce-s-ass-006 · Assessment and Testing · differential diagnosis · hard · In review

A 28-year-old client currently meets criteria for a major depressive episode. Two years ago she had a 5-day period of elevated mood, decreased need for sleep, and rapid speech that friends noticed, with no marked impairment, hospitalization, or psychotic features. She has had no other mood episodes. Which diagnosis BEST fits?
A. Major depressive disorder, recurrent
A past hypomanic episode rules out major depressive disorder.
B. Bipolar II disorder ✔ KEY
At least one hypomanic episode plus at least one major depressive episode, with no manic episode, defines bipolar II.
C. Bipolar I disorder
Bipolar I requires a manic episode, which lasts at least one week (or any duration if hospitalized) and causes marked impairment, hospitalization, or psychosis.
D. Cyclothymic disorder
Cyclothymia requires two years of subthreshold symptoms and is excluded once a major depressive episode occurs.
Rationale: Hypomania lasts at least four consecutive days, is observable by others, and does not cause marked impairment or require hospitalization. Mania lasts at least a week (or any duration if hospitalized) and is severe enough to cause marked impairment, require hospitalization, or include psychotic features. A hypomanic episode plus a major depressive episode equals bipolar II.
References: DSM-5-TR — Bipolar II Disorder criteria; Hypomanic Episode Criterion A
☐ Approve☐ Revise☐ RetireNotes:

55. nce-s-ass-007 · Assessment and Testing · reliability types · easy · In review

A test developer gives the same anxiety inventory to the same group of adults twice, two weeks apart, and correlates the two sets of scores. Which type of reliability is being estimated?
A. Test-retest reliability ✔ KEY
Correlating scores from the same test given twice to the same people estimates stability over time.
B. Alternate-form reliability
Alternate-form reliability uses two different but equivalent versions of the test.
C. Split-half reliability
Split-half reliability comes from one administration divided into two halves.
D. Interrater reliability
Interrater reliability is agreement between two or more scorers or observers.
Rationale: Test-retest reliability (the coefficient of stability) reflects how consistent scores are across time. The interval matters: too short invites practice and memory effects, too long lets real change lower the coefficient.
References: Hays (Assessment) — Reliability: test-retest (coefficient of stability)
☐ Approve☐ Revise☐ RetireNotes:

56. nce-s-ass-008 · Research and Program Evaluation · reliability types · medium · In review

A school counselor has a single administration of a 40-item vocabulary test in which every item is scored simply right or wrong. Which statistic is BEST suited to estimate the test's internal consistency?
A. A test-retest correlation
Test-retest requires two administrations and measures stability, not internal consistency.
B. Cohen's kappa coefficient
Kappa indexes agreement between raters, corrected for chance.
C. An alternate-form coefficient
Alternate-form reliability needs a second, equivalent version of the test.
D. Kuder-Richardson Formula 20 ✔ KEY
KR-20 estimates internal consistency from one administration of dichotomously scored items.
Rationale: Internal consistency estimates come from one administration. KR-20 is used with dichotomous (right/wrong) items; coefficient (Cronbach's) alpha generalizes it to items with more than two score points. Split-half reliability, corrected with the Spearman-Brown formula, is another single-administration option.
References: Erford (Research and Evaluation in Counseling) — Internal consistency: KR-20 and coefficient alpha
☐ Approve☐ Revise☐ RetireNotes:

57. nce-s-ass-009 · Assessment and Testing · validity types · medium · In review

Developers of a new mechanical aptitude test give it to newly hired technicians and correlate those scores with supervisor performance ratings collected 18 months later. What type of validity evidence does this provide?
A. Concurrent validity
Concurrent validity uses a criterion measured at about the same time as the test.
B. Predictive validity ✔ KEY
The criterion is measured well after testing, so this is criterion-related predictive validity.
C. Content validity
Content validity concerns how well items sample the domain, judged by experts, not a criterion correlation.
D. Discriminant validity
Discriminant validity is construct evidence showing low correlation with unrelated constructs.
Rationale: Criterion-related validity has two forms that differ by timing: concurrent (criterion gathered at the same time) and predictive (criterion gathered later). Correlating test scores with later job ratings shows how well the test forecasts future performance.
References: Hays (Assessment) — Validity: criterion-related (concurrent and predictive)
☐ Approve☐ Revise☐ RetireNotes:

58. nce-s-ass-010 · Research and Program Evaluation · validity types · medium · In review

A new depression scale correlates .78 with an established depression inventory and .06 with a measure of musical aptitude. The low correlation with musical aptitude is BEST described as evidence of which type of validity?
A. Convergent validity
Convergent evidence is the high correlation with the established depression inventory, not the low one.
B. Predictive validity
Predictive validity concerns forecasting a later criterion, which is not described here.
C. Discriminant validity ✔ KEY
A low correlation with a theoretically unrelated construct supports discriminant (divergent) validity.
D. Face validity
Face validity is whether a test looks like it measures its target; it is not shown by correlations.
Rationale: Construct validity is supported by convergent evidence (strong relations with measures of the same construct) and discriminant evidence (weak relations with measures of different constructs). The multitrait-multimethod matrix examines both together.
References: Erford (Research and Evaluation in Counseling) — Construct validity: convergent and discriminant evidence
☐ Approve☐ Revise☐ RetireNotes:

59. nce-s-ass-011 · Assessment and Testing · norm- vs criterion-referenced tests · easy · In review

A district reading test reports, for each fourth grader, whether the student has mastered at least 80% of the grade-level reading objectives, regardless of how classmates performed. What kind of test is this?
A. A norm-referenced test
Norm-referenced tests compare a score with a norm group, which this report does not do.
B. An ipsative measure
Ipsative measures compare traits within one person, not mastery of a standard.
C. A projective technique
Projective techniques use ambiguous stimuli to explore personality.
D. A criterion-referenced test ✔ KEY
Comparing performance with a fixed standard of mastery is criterion-referenced.
Rationale: Criterion-referenced tests interpret a score against a set standard or body of content (what the person can do). Norm-referenced tests interpret a score against the performance of a comparison group (how the person ranks).
References: Hays (Assessment) — Norm-referenced vs criterion-referenced interpretation
☐ Approve☐ Revise☐ RetireNotes:

60. nce-s-ass-012 · Assessment and Testing · standard scores, percentiles and the normal curve · medium · In review

Scores on a test are normally distributed. Approximately what percentage of test takers score between one standard deviation below the mean and two standard deviations above the mean?
A. About 82% ✔ KEY
About 34% fall between -1 SD and the mean, and about 47.5% between the mean and +2 SD, for roughly 82%.
B. About 68%
68% is the share within plus or minus one SD of the mean.
C. About 95%
95% is the share within plus or minus two SD of the mean.
D. About 48%
About 47.5% lies between the mean and +2 SD alone; the band below the mean is left out.
Rationale: On the normal curve about 68% of scores fall within 1 SD of the mean, about 95% within 2 SD, and about 99.7% within 3 SD. Halving those figures gives the share on one side: about 34% from the mean to 1 SD and about 47.5% from the mean to 2 SD.
References: Erford (Research and Evaluation in Counseling) — The normal curve: 68-95-99.7 rule
☐ Approve☐ Revise☐ RetireNotes:

61. nce-s-ass-013 · Assessment and Testing · standard scores, percentiles and the normal curve · hard · In review

A counselor compares four results from different normally distributed tests. Which result reflects the HIGHEST standing relative to its norm group?
A. A z score of +1.2
A z of +1.2 is below the T score of 65, which equals z = +1.5.
B. A deviation IQ of 118
With mean 100 and SD 15, an IQ of 118 equals z = +1.2.
C. A T score of 65 ✔ KEY
With mean 50 and SD 10, a T of 65 is 1.5 SD above the mean, the highest of the four.
D. A percentile rank of 88
The 88th percentile corresponds to a z of about +1.17.
Rationale: Converting each score to z allows comparison: z = (score - mean) / SD. T scores have a mean of 50 and SD of 10; deviation IQs have a mean of 100 and SD of 15; the 84th percentile is about z = +1.0 and the 88th about z = +1.17.
References: Hays (Assessment) — Standard scores: z, T and deviation IQ conversions
☐ Approve☐ Revise☐ RetireNotes:

62. nce-s-ass-014 · Assessment and Testing · standard scores, percentiles and the normal curve · hard · In review

On a normally distributed test, one student's percentile rank rose from 50 to 60 and another student's rose from 89 to 99. Which statement about their raw-score gains is accurate?
A. Both students gained about the same number of raw-score points
Percentile ranks are not equal-interval units, so equal percentile gains do not mean equal raw gains.
B. The second student needed a much larger raw-score gain ✔ KEY
Scores thin out in the upper tail, so moving from the 89th to 99th percentile takes far more raw-score distance.
C. The first student needed a much larger raw-score gain
Scores cluster near the mean, so a 10-rank gain there takes a small raw-score change.
D. The gains cannot be compared without first converting to stanines
Stanines are not needed; converting percentiles to z scores shows the difference directly.
Rationale: Percentile ranks are ordinal. Because most scores pile up near the mean, a small raw change in the middle moves the percentile a lot, while the same percentile change in the tail requires a large raw change. Here 50 to 60 is about 0.25 SD; 89 to 99 is about 1.1 SD.
References: Hays (Assessment) — Percentile ranks: ordinal scale and interpretation limits
☐ Approve☐ Revise☐ RetireNotes:

63. nce-s-ass-015 · Assessment and Testing · standard error of measurement · medium · In review

A client obtains a score of 92 on a test with a standard error of measurement (SEM) of 4. Which range is the approximate 95% confidence band for the client's true score?
A. 88 to 96
Plus or minus 1 SEM gives a band of about 68% confidence.
B. 90 to 94
Plus or minus half an SEM is far narrower than any standard band.
C. 80 to 104
Plus or minus 3 SEM gives about 99.7% confidence, wider than asked.
D. 84 to 100 ✔ KEY
Plus or minus about 2 SEM (1.96 x 4, roughly 8 points) gives the 95% band.
Rationale: The SEM is the standard deviation of a person's hypothetical scores around the true score. Bands of plus or minus 1 SEM, about 1.96 SEM (often rounded to 2), and 3 SEM around the obtained score give roughly 68%, 95%, and 99.7% confidence that the true score lies inside. Here 1.96 x 4 = 7.84, so the 95% band is about 84 to 100.
References: Hays (Assessment) — Standard error of measurement and confidence intervals
☐ Approve☐ Revise☐ RetireNotes:

64. nce-s-ass-016 · Research and Program Evaluation · standard error of measurement · hard · In review

A test has a standard deviation of 15 and a reliability coefficient of .91. What is its standard error of measurement?
A. 4.5 points ✔ KEY
SEM = 15 x the square root of (1 - .91) = 15 x 0.3 = 4.5.
B. 1.35 points
This multiplies the SD by .09 and omits the square root.
C. 13.65 points
This multiplies the SD by the reliability coefficient itself.
D. 0.3 points
This is only the square root of (1 - .91), not multiplied by the SD.
Rationale: SEM = SD x the square root of (1 - r). Higher reliability means a smaller SEM and narrower confidence bands; a perfectly reliable test would have an SEM of zero.
References: Erford (Research and Evaluation in Counseling) — Standard error of measurement formula
☐ Approve☐ Revise☐ RetireNotes:

65. nce-s-ass-017 · Assessment and Testing · intelligence and achievement tests · easy · In review

A school-based counselor is part of a team evaluating the intellectual functioning of a 10-year-old. Which instrument is designed for this child's age?
A. WAIS-IV
The WAIS-IV is normed for adults and older adolescents (16 and up).
B. WPPSI-IV
The WPPSI-IV is for preschool and early primary children, below age 8.
C. WISC-V ✔ KEY
The WISC-V covers ages 6 through 16, including a 10-year-old.
D. MMPI-A
The MMPI-A is an adolescent personality inventory, not an intelligence test.
Rationale: The Wechsler scales are split by age: WPPSI-IV for young children (about 2½ to 7½), WISC-V for ages 6 to 16, and WAIS-IV for ages 16 to 90. Each yields a Full Scale IQ with a mean of 100 and SD of 15.
References: Hays (Assessment) — Intelligence testing: Wechsler scales by age range
⚑ Check: Check the Wechsler age ranges used in the rationale (WPPSI-IV, WISC-V, WAIS-IV).

66. nce-s-ass-018 · Assessment and Testing · personality inventories (MMPI-3, NEO-PI) · easy · In review

A counselor wants a personality inventory built on the five-factor model (neuroticism, extraversion, openness, agreeableness, and conscientiousness). Which instrument fits this purpose?
A. Rorschach Inkblot Test
The Rorschach is a projective technique, not a five-factor inventory.
B. NEO Personality Inventory-3 ✔ KEY
The NEO-PI-3 measures the five broad domains of the five-factor model and their facets.
C. Myers-Briggs Type Indicator
The MBTI sorts people into Jungian types rather than measuring the five factors.
D. MMPI-3
The MMPI-3 focuses on psychopathology and validity scales, not the five-factor model.
Rationale: The NEO-PI-3 (Costa and McCrae) assesses neuroticism, extraversion, openness, agreeableness, and conscientiousness, each with six facets. The MMPI family is used mainly to assess psychopathology and includes validity scales for over- and underreporting.
References: Hays (Assessment) — Personality inventories: NEO-PI-3 and the five-factor model
☐ Approve☐ Revise☐ RetireNotes:

67. nce-s-ass-019 · Assessment and Testing · projective tests · easy · In review

A counselor shows an adolescent a series of ambiguous drawings of people in everyday situations and asks him to tell a story about each one, with a beginning, middle, and end. Which instrument is being used?
A. Rorschach Inkblot Test
The Rorschach uses inkblots, not pictures of people, and asks what the person sees.
B. House-Tree-Person
In House-Tree-Person the client makes the drawings rather than responding to them.
C. Rotter Incomplete Sentences
The Rotter uses sentence stems for the client to finish, not pictures.
D. Thematic Apperception Test ✔ KEY
The TAT presents ambiguous picture cards and asks for a story about each.
Rationale: The Thematic Apperception Test, developed by Morgan and Murray, is a projective technique in which stories told about ambiguous scenes are thought to reveal needs, motives, and conflicts. Projective tests rely on ambiguous stimuli and generally have weaker psychometric support than objective inventories.
References: Hays (Assessment) — Projective techniques: Thematic Apperception Test
☐ Approve☐ Revise☐ RetireNotes:

68. nce-s-ass-020 · Assessment and Testing · screening instruments (PHQ-9, GAD-7, AUDIT, CAGE) · medium · In review

On the PHQ-9, a client's total score is 6, in the mild range, but she marks "several days" on the last item, which asks about thoughts that she would be better off dead or of hurting herself. What should the counselor do next?
A. Follow up at once with a direct suicide risk assessment ✔ KEY
Any endorsement of the self-harm item calls for direct risk assessment whatever the total score.
B. Note mild depression and recheck the PHQ-9 next month
Waiting ignores a positive response on the item that flags possible suicidal thinking.
C. Give the GAD-7 to see whether anxiety explains the item
An anxiety screen does not assess or rule out suicide risk.
D. Treat the item as invalid because the total score is low
A low total does not cancel a positive item 9; risk can exist with few other symptoms.
Rationale: The PHQ-9 has nine items scored 0 to 3 (total 0 to 27). Item 9 screens for passive or active thoughts of death or self-harm; any positive answer should be followed by a clinician's direct suicide risk assessment, since a screening total alone does not measure risk.
References: Hays (Assessment) — Screening instruments: Patient Health Questionnaire-9 (PHQ-9)
☐ Approve☐ Revise☐ RetireNotes:

69. nce-s-ass-021 · Assessment and Testing · substance use assessment · medium · In review

A 52-year-old client says that when he goes a day without drinking he gets shaky, sweaty, and cannot sleep. Using the ASAM Criteria (3rd edition), under which dimension should the counselor rate these symptoms?
A. Readiness to change
This dimension rates motivation and engagement, not physical withdrawal.
B. Recovery environment
This dimension rates the client's living situation and supports.
C. Intoxication and withdrawal ✔ KEY
Tremor, sweating, and insomnia on stopping alcohol are withdrawal signs, rated in Dimension 1.
D. Relapse potential
This dimension rates risk of continued use, not current withdrawal.
Rationale: The ASAM Criteria rate six dimensions to match level of care. Dimension 1 covers intoxication and withdrawal; in the widely taught third edition the others address biomedical conditions, emotional/behavioral/cognitive conditions, readiness to change, relapse or continued-use potential, and the recovery environment. The 2023 fourth edition renamed and reorganized several later dimensions, but intoxication and withdrawal remains part of Dimension 1. Alcohol withdrawal can be dangerous and may call for medically managed care.
References: ASAM Criteria — 3rd edition, Dimension 1: acute intoxication and/or withdrawal potential

70. nce-s-ass-022 · Social and Cultural Diversity · culturally fair assessment · medium · In review

A counselor is asked to assess the cognitive abilities of a 13-year-old who arrived from Guatemala six months ago and is still learning English. Which approach is MOST appropriate?
A. Give a verbal English IQ test and report the score as usual
The score would mostly reflect English proficiency, so reporting it without caveats is invalid.
B. Use a nonverbal or native-language measure, interpreted with care ✔ KEY
Reducing language load and noting norm limits gives a fairer, more valid picture of ability.
C. Delay all testing until he has lived in the country for five years
A rigid wait is arbitrary and may delay needed services; fairer methods exist now.
D. Add 15 points to his score to adjust for the language barrier
Arbitrary score adjustment has no psychometric basis and distorts results.
Rationale: Counselors select instruments whose norms and language suit the client and interpret results in light of culture, language, and acculturation. When a test was not normed on the client's population, the report should say so. Nonverbal measures or measures in the client's dominant language reduce bias from limited English.
References: ACA Code of Ethics — E.8 Multicultural Issues/Diversity in Assessment · Sue & Sue (Counseling the Culturally Diverse) — Culturally biased assessment and test interpretation
☐ Approve☐ Revise☐ RetireNotes:

71. nce-s-ass-023 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · biopsychosocial history · medium · In review

During a biopsychosocial intake, a 55-year-old client with no prior mental health history describes new anxiety, a racing heart, weight loss despite a good appetite, and feeling hot all the time. What is the counselor's BEST next step?
A. Diagnose generalized anxiety disorder and begin relaxation training
GAD requires that symptoms not be due to a medical condition, which has not been ruled out.
B. Explore childhood experiences that may underlie the new anxiety
This skips the possibility that a medical problem is driving the symptoms.
C. Reassure the client that midlife anxiety is common and expected
Reassurance without evaluation may miss a treatable physical cause.
D. Recommend a medical evaluation to rule out a physical cause ✔ KEY
Late onset with weight loss and heat intolerance suggests a possible medical cause, such as thyroid disease.
Rationale: The biological part of a biopsychosocial history covers health, medications, and physical symptoms. New-onset anxiety in midlife with physical signs such as weight loss and heat intolerance can reflect conditions like hyperthyroidism, so a medical evaluation comes before a mental disorder diagnosis.
References: DSM-5-TR — Anxiety Disorder Due to Another Medical Condition; GAD Criterion E
☐ Approve☐ Revise☐ RetireNotes:

72. nce-s-ass-024 · Assessment and Testing · collateral information and records · medium · In review

A client being assessed for alcohol use says he "only has a couple of beers on weekends." With his written consent, the counselor also interviews his spouse. What is the PRIMARY value of this collateral information?
A. It can confirm or add to self-report that may be minimized ✔ KEY
Collateral sources help check self-report, which can be limited by minimization or poor insight.
B. It removes the need for a structured diagnostic interview
Collateral data supplement, not replace, a thorough clinical interview.
C. It lets the spouse set the goals of the client's treatment
Treatment goals are set collaboratively with the client, who directs his own care.
D. It supplies the evidence that state law requires to diagnose
Diagnosis does not legally require collateral reports; they are clinically useful, not mandatory.
Rationale: Collateral information from family, prior records, or other providers broadens the assessment, especially when the client may underreport, lack insight, or have memory problems. It is gathered with the client's consent and weighed alongside the client's own account.
References: Hays (Assessment) — Collateral sources of assessment information

73. nce-s-ass-025 · Career Development · career assessment: interest inventories · easy · In review

A 23-year-old graduate student wants to know how her likes and dislikes compare with those of people who are employed in, and satisfied with, a wide range of occupations. Which instrument is designed to provide this comparison?
A. Strong Interest Inventory ✔ KEY
Its Occupational Scales compare the client's responses with those of satisfied workers in many occupations.
B. Myers-Briggs Type Indicator
The MBTI sorts people into psychological types; it does not compare interests with occupational groups.
C. Differential Aptitude Tests
The DAT measures aptitudes (abilities), not interests or occupational fit.
D. Minnesota Importance Questionnaire
The MIQ measures work needs and values, not interests compared with workers in occupations.
Rationale: The Strong Interest Inventory reports General Occupational Themes (Holland's RIASEC types), Basic Interest Scales, Occupational Scales, and Personal Style Scales. The Occupational Scales are built empirically from people who are employed in an occupation and report liking it, so a high score means the client's interests resemble theirs. Interest similarity predicts satisfaction, not ability or success.
References: Sharf (Career Development Theory) — Interest inventories: Strong Interest Inventory scales · Hays (Assessment) — Career assessment: interest inventories

74. nce-s-ass-026 · Career Development · career assessment: interest inventories · easy · In review

A rural high school with limited counseling staff wants a career interest tool that students can take, score, and begin interpreting on their own, producing a three-letter summary code they can use to look up matching occupations. Which instrument BEST fits this need?
A. Strong Interest Inventory
The Strong is scored by the publisher and is usually interpreted with a trained counselor, not self-scored.
B. ASVAB career aptitude battery
The ASVAB measures aptitudes and is not a self-scored interest inventory.
C. Self-Directed Search ✔ KEY
Holland designed the SDS to be self-administered and self-scored, yielding a summary code linked to an occupations finder.
D. Thematic Apperception Test
The TAT is a projective personality technique, not a career interest measure.
Rationale: John Holland's Self-Directed Search asks about activities, competencies, occupations, and self-estimates, and the person totals the scores into a three-letter RIASEC summary code. The code is matched to occupations with the same code. Its self-guided design makes it practical where counselor time is limited, although counselor follow-up still improves interpretation.
References: Sharf (Career Development Theory) — Holland's theory: the Self-Directed Search

75. nce-s-ass-027 · Career Development · career assessment: qualitative methods · medium · In review

A counselor working with a 46-year-old who lost a long-held factory job asks him to sort cards naming occupations into "would choose," "would not choose," and "undecided" piles and then talk through why he placed each card where he did. What is the MAIN advantage of this method over a standardized interest inventory?
A. It yields norm-referenced scores that compare clients fairly
Card sorts are qualitative and generally unnormed; they do not produce norm-referenced scores.
B. It brings out the client's own reasons and personal meanings ✔ KEY
The discussion of each placement reveals the client's constructs, values, and reasoning in his own words.
C. It offers strong predictive validity for later job success
Card sorts are not designed or validated to predict job success.
D. It removes the need for counselor interpretation of results
Card sorts rely heavily on counselor-client dialogue; interpretation is the heart of the method.
Rationale: Card sorts (occupational, values, or skills sorts) are qualitative, interactive assessments. Their strength is flexibility and insight into how the client thinks about work, which is useful for adults in transition. The tradeoff is the lack of norms and standardized psychometric evidence.
References: Sharf (Career Development Theory) — Qualitative career assessment: card sorts

76. nce-s-ass-028 · Career Development · career assessment: work values · medium · In review

A 31-year-old client's interest profile fits her field well, yet she has left three jobs, each time saying the work offered little recognition or independence. Her counselor gives her the Minnesota Importance Questionnaire. What does this instrument primarily measure?
A. Her verbal, numerical, and spatial aptitudes for job training
Aptitudes are measured by ability batteries, not the MIQ.
B. Her personality type according to the five-factor model
Five-factor traits are measured by instruments such as the NEO-PI-3, not the MIQ.
C. How closely her likes resemble those of satisfied workers
That describes the Occupational Scales of an interest inventory such as the Strong.
D. Her work needs and values, compared with what jobs reward ✔ KEY
The MIQ assesses vocational needs and values so they can be matched with the reinforcers that occupations provide.
Rationale: The Minnesota Importance Questionnaire comes from Dawis and Lofquist's Theory of Work Adjustment, which holds that satisfaction depends on the match between a worker's needs and values and the reinforcers a job supplies. Interests alone may fit, but a values mismatch (such as low recognition or autonomy) can still lead to dissatisfaction and turnover.
References: Sharf (Career Development Theory) — Theory of Work Adjustment: Minnesota Importance Questionnaire
⚑ Check: Confirm the MIQ is still described in Sharf as the TWA values/needs measure (it is now largely available through O*NET-related tools).

77. nce-s-ass-029 · Career Development · intelligence and achievement tests · medium · In review

A high school junior is exploring careers. Her counselor wants an estimate of her potential to learn in several separate areas, such as mechanical comprehension, arithmetic reasoning, and word knowledge, rather than a record of what she has already mastered in class. Which type of instrument BEST fits?
A. A multiaptitude battery such as the ASVAB ✔ KEY
Multiaptitude batteries estimate potential in several distinct ability areas and are widely used in career exploration.
B. An achievement battery covering school subjects
Achievement tests measure what has already been learned, which the counselor explicitly does not want.
C. A general intelligence test with one overall IQ
A single global score does not profile separate occupational ability areas.
D. An interest inventory such as the Strong
Interest inventories measure likes and dislikes, not ability or potential.
Rationale: Aptitude tests predict future learning or performance; achievement tests measure past learning from instruction; intelligence tests estimate general cognitive ability. A multiaptitude battery such as the ASVAB or the Differential Aptitude Tests yields a profile of specific abilities, which is useful alongside interests and values in career planning.
References: Hays (Assessment) — Aptitude, achievement, and intelligence testing

78. nce-s-ass-030 · Research and Program Evaluation · item analysis · hard · In review

A counselor educator runs an item analysis on a classroom test. Item 7: p = .52, D = .41. Item 12: p = .48, D = -.22. Item 19: p = .90, D = .10. Item 23: p = .30, D = .35. Which item should be revised or removed FIRST?
A. Item 7, because its difficulty sits too near the midpoint
A p near .50 with D = .41 is close to ideal; it is the strongest item here.
B. Item 23, because fewer than a third of students got it right
A hard item is acceptable when it discriminates well, and D = .35 is good.
C. Item 12, because low scorers outperform the high scorers ✔ KEY
A negative discrimination index means the item works backward and is likely flawed (miskeyed or misleading).
D. Item 19, because nearly every student answered it correctly
An easy item with weak but positive discrimination is a lower priority than one that discriminates negatively.
Rationale: Item difficulty (p) is the proportion of examinees answering correctly, so a higher p means an easier item. The discrimination index (D) is the proportion correct in the high-scoring group minus the proportion correct in the low-scoring group. Positive D is desired; a negative D signals a defective item and is the top priority for review.
References: Hays (Assessment) — Item analysis: item difficulty and item discrimination
☐ Approve☐ Revise☐ RetireNotes:

79. nce-s-ass-031 · Research and Program Evaluation · reliability types · hard · In review

A test developer splits a 60-item anxiety scale into odd- and even-numbered items and finds that the two halves correlate .60. Using the Spearman-Brown formula, what is the estimated reliability of the full 60-item scale?
A. .36
Squaring .60 gives shared variance, not a reliability estimate for the lengthened test.
B. .60
This is the half-test correlation, which underestimates the reliability of the full-length test.
C. .80
This overstates the correction; 2(.60)/(1 + .60) is .75.
D. .75 ✔ KEY
Spearman-Brown: 2r/(1 + r) = 1.20/1.60 = .75.
Rationale: Split-half reliability correlates two halves of one administration, but each half is only half as long as the real test, and shorter tests are less reliable. The Spearman-Brown prophecy formula corrects for this: r(full) = 2r(half) / (1 + r(half)). The formula can also project reliability for other changes in test length.
References: Hays (Assessment) — Split-half reliability and the Spearman-Brown formula
☐ Approve☐ Revise☐ RetireNotes:

80. nce-s-ass-032 · Research and Program Evaluation · reliability types · medium · In review

Two counselors independently review 50 recorded intakes and judge whether each client meets criteria for a substance use disorder. They agree on 90% of cases, but most clients fall into the "does not meet" category. Why would a researcher report Cohen's kappa instead of simple percent agreement?
A. Kappa shows how stable each rater's judgments are over time
Stability over time is test-retest (or intra-rater) reliability, not what kappa between two raters addresses.
B. Kappa adjusts for the agreement expected by chance alone ✔ KEY
When one category dominates, raters agree often by chance; kappa removes that chance agreement.
C. Kappa proves the raters' diagnoses are accurate and valid
Agreement is reliability; two raters can agree and both be wrong, so kappa does not establish validity.
D. Kappa estimates internal consistency of the rating form items
Internal consistency is estimated with coefficient alpha or KR-20, not kappa.
Rationale: Inter-rater reliability reflects how consistently different raters score the same material. Percent agreement is inflated when base rates are lopsided, because raters will often agree by chance. Cohen's kappa compares observed agreement with chance-expected agreement, giving a more honest estimate for categorical judgments.
References: Erford (Research and Evaluation in Counseling) — Inter-rater reliability: Cohen's kappa
☐ Approve☐ Revise☐ RetireNotes:

81. nce-s-ass-033 · Assessment and Testing · validity types · easy · In review

Clients who try out a new workplace stress inventory say, "It's obviously asking about job stress." The developer cites these comments in the manual as the main evidence that the inventory is valid. What do the comments actually reflect?
A. Face validity, which is not real psychometric evidence ✔ KEY
Face validity is how a test looks to test takers; it can aid cooperation but is not evidence of what the test measures.
B. Content validity, shown through expert review of items
Content validity requires systematic expert judgment of how well items sample the domain, not test takers' impressions.
C. Construct validity, shown by the internal factor structure
Construct validity requires accumulated empirical evidence such as factor analysis or convergent correlations.
D. Concurrent validity, shown by agreement with a criterion
Concurrent validity requires correlating scores with a criterion measured at the same time.
Rationale: Face validity is the superficial appearance that a test measures what it claims. Content validity, by contrast, is established when experts judge items against a defined content domain or table of specifications. Only content, criterion-related, and construct evidence support score interpretation; face validity does not.
References: Hays (Assessment) — Validity: content validity versus face validity
☐ Approve☐ Revise☐ RetireNotes:

82. nce-s-ass-034 · Assessment and Testing · validity types · medium · In review

A developer theorizes that counselor burnout has three distinct dimensions and writes items for each. A factor analysis of responses from 900 counselors shows the items clustering on three factors that match the theorized dimensions. This result is BEST described as evidence of which kind of validity?
A. Predictive validity tied to a future outcome criterion
Predictive validity requires correlating scores with a criterion measured later.
B. Test-retest reliability across repeated administrations
Factor structure from one administration says nothing about stability over time, and it is reliability, not validity.
C. Construct validity based on the test's internal structure ✔ KEY
Items grouping into the theorized dimensions supports the claim that the test measures the intended construct.
D. Content validity confirmed by the judgments of experts
Content validity rests on expert review of item coverage, not on statistical clustering of responses.
Rationale: Construct validity asks whether a test measures the theoretical trait it is meant to measure. Factor analysis provides internal-structure evidence: if items load on factors that match the theory, the score structure fits the construct. Convergent and discriminant correlations are other common sources of construct evidence.
References: Hays (Assessment) — Construct validity: factor analysis and internal structure

83. nce-s-ass-035 · Assessment and Testing · norm- vs criterion-referenced tests · medium · In review

A counselor plans to give a self-esteem inventory to a 72-year-old Filipino American retiree. The manual reports norms from about 2,000 undergraduates at four Midwestern universities. What is the counselor's PRIMARY concern in interpreting her percentile score?
A. A 2,000-person sample is too small to yield stable norms
Two thousand is a substantial norm sample; size is not the problem here.
B. Self-report formats are invalid for clients over age 65
Older adults can complete self-report measures; age alone does not invalidate the format.
C. Percentile ranks first need a criterion-referenced cutoff
Percentiles are norm-referenced by definition and do not depend on a criterion cutoff.
D. The norm group does not represent clients like this one ✔ KEY
Her score would be compared with young college students, a group that differs from her in age, culture, and life stage.
Rationale: Norm-referenced scores mean something only relative to the norm group. Counselors check that the norm sample is representative of the client (age, culture, language, setting) and reasonably current. When the norm group does not match, percentile and standard scores can mislead, so the counselor should seek better-matched norms or interpret with explicit caution.
References: Hays (Assessment) — Norm groups: representativeness, size, and recency

84. nce-s-ass-036 · Assessment and Testing · standard scores, percentiles and the normal curve · medium · In review

A third grader earns a grade-equivalent score of 6.2 on a mathematics achievement test given to her class. Her parent asks whether she should be moved into sixth-grade math. Which interpretation should the counselor give?
A. She has mastered most of the sixth-grade math curriculum
The test sampled third-grade content; it did not test sixth-grade skills.
B. She did very well on third-grade content, not sixth-grade ✔ KEY
A grade equivalent of 6.2 means her score matches what an average early sixth grader is estimated to earn on this third-grade test.
C. Her score is three standard deviations above her grade mean
Grade equivalents are not equal-interval units and cannot be converted into standard deviations this way.
D. She would score at the 62nd percentile among sixth graders
The decimal in a grade equivalent refers to the month of the school year, not a percentile.
Rationale: Grade and age equivalents are developmental scores that are easy to misread. They are often extrapolated rather than based on students actually tested at that grade, they are not equal-interval, and they do not indicate mastery of higher-grade material. Percentile ranks or standard scores within the student's own grade are better for interpretation.
References: Hays (Assessment) — Developmental scores: limits of grade and age equivalents

85. nce-s-ass-037 · Assessment and Testing · standard scores, percentiles and the normal curve · hard · In review

A school district reports its students' achievement results as normal curve equivalents (NCEs) and as stanines. A school board member asks the counselor how to read these scores. Which statement is accurate?
A. NCEs are equal-interval scores (mean 50) that can be averaged ✔ KEY
NCEs are normalized standard scores (mean 50, SD about 21.06) on an equal-interval scale, which is why programs average them.
B. Stanines divide students into nine bands holding equal percentages
Stanine bands are equal in width (half an SD) but hold unequal percentages, about 4% at each end and 20% in the middle.
C. An NCE of 50 and a stanine of 9 describe the same relative standing
An NCE of 50 is average, matching stanine 5; stanine 9 is the top band.
D. NCEs equal percentile ranks at every point along the whole score scale
NCEs match percentile ranks only at 1, 50, and 99; elsewhere they differ because percentiles are not equal-interval.
Rationale: Stanines ("standard nines") run from 1 to 9 with a mean of 5 and an SD of about 2. Normal curve equivalents run from 1 to 99 with a mean of 50 and an SD of about 21.06, chosen so they line up with percentiles at 1, 50, and 99. Unlike percentile ranks, NCEs are equal-interval and can be averaged and compared across groups.
References: Hays (Assessment) — Standard scores: stanines and normal curve equivalents
☐ Approve☐ Revise☐ RetireNotes:

86. nce-s-ass-038 · Research and Program Evaluation · standard scores, percentiles and the normal curve · medium · In review

On a very difficult practice exam, most examinees earn low scores and a handful earn very high scores. How are the mean, median, and mode MOST likely to be ordered in this distribution?
A. Mean lowest, median next, and mode highest of the three
This ordering describes a negatively skewed distribution, where a few very low scores pull the mean down.
B. Mean, median, and mode fall at the same point in the curve
The three measures coincide only in a symmetrical distribution such as the normal curve.
C. Mode lowest, median next, and mean highest of the three ✔ KEY
A few very high scores create a positive skew and pull the mean toward the right tail.
D. Median lowest, with mean and mode equal and above it
The median lies between the mode and mean in a skewed distribution; it is not the lowest.
Rationale: Skew is named for the tail. When scores bunch at the low end with a tail to the right, the distribution is positively skewed, and the mean (most sensitive to extreme scores) is pulled toward the tail: mode < median < mean. In a negative skew the order reverses. The median is often preferred for describing skewed data.
References: Erford (Research and Evaluation in Counseling) — Descriptive statistics: skewness and measures of central tendency
☐ Approve☐ Revise☐ RetireNotes:

87. nce-s-ass-039 · Research and Program Evaluation · correlation and shared variance · medium · In review

In a clinic sample, weekly exercise hours correlate -.70 with depression scores, and nightly hours of sleep correlate +.40 with depression scores. Which conclusion is accurate?
A. The sleep link is stronger because its coefficient is positive
The sign shows direction only; strength depends on the absolute value, and .70 exceeds .40.
B. Exercise accounts for 70% of the variance in depression scores
Shared variance is r squared, so -.70 corresponds to 49%, not 70%.
C. The negative coefficient shows exercise reduces depression
Correlation does not establish causation; a third variable or reverse direction could explain it.
D. The exercise link is stronger and shares 49% of the variance ✔ KEY
The absolute value .70 is larger than .40, and r squared (.49) gives the proportion of shared variance.
Rationale: A correlation coefficient's sign gives direction and its absolute value gives strength. Squaring r gives the coefficient of determination, the proportion of variance the two variables share. No correlation, however strong, demonstrates cause and effect.
References: Erford (Research and Evaluation in Counseling) — Correlation: direction, strength, and coefficient of determination
☐ Approve☐ Revise☐ RetireNotes:

88. nce-s-ass-040 · Assessment and Testing · screening instruments (PHQ-9, GAD-7, AUDIT, CAGE) · medium · In review

An elementary school principal proposes using scores from a 10-minute behavior screening checklist, completed once by each teacher, to decide which students are placed in special education services. What is the school counselor's BEST advice?
A. Accept the plan if the checklist has internal consistency above .80
Good reliability does not make a brief screener valid for a high-stakes placement decision.
B. Use the screener to flag students for a full multi-measure evaluation ✔ KEY
Screeners identify who needs further assessment; high-stakes decisions require comprehensive evaluation from multiple sources.
C. Raise the cutoff score so that fewer students are wrongly identified
Changing the cutoff trades false positives for false negatives and still leaves a single screener driving placement.
D. Keep the screener but have a second teacher rescore every checklist
Double scoring reduces clerical error but does not address the misuse of a screener for a high-stakes decision.
Rationale: Screening instruments are brief and built to cast a wide net, accepting some false positives. High-stakes decisions such as placement, diagnosis, or eligibility should rest on multiple measures, multiple informants, and qualified professional judgment rather than a single score.
References: Hays (Assessment) — Screening versus comprehensive assessment; high-stakes testing

89. nce-s-ass-041 · Assessment and Testing · screening instruments (PHQ-9, GAD-7, AUDIT, CAGE) · easy · In review

A counselor at a university counseling center plans to give the Beck Depression Inventory-II (BDI-II) to new clients. Which description of the BDI-II is accurate?
A. A 21-item self-report of depressive symptoms over two weeks ✔ KEY
The BDI-II is a 21-item self-report of depressive symptom severity covering the past two weeks, including today.
B. A clinician-administered interview that yields DSM-5-TR diagnoses
The BDI-II is a self-report severity measure, not a diagnostic interview.
C. A projective technique that uncovers depressive themes in children
It is an objective self-report inventory, and it is intended for adolescents and adults.
D. A nine-item primary care screen keyed to DSM depression criteria
That describes the PHQ-9; the BDI-II has 21 items and was developed separately from primary care screening.
Rationale: The BDI-II measures the severity of depressive symptoms in adolescents and adults, with each item rated 0 to 3. It is useful for screening and tracking change but does not by itself establish a diagnosis; a clinical interview is still needed.
References: Hays (Assessment) — Assessment of depression: Beck Depression Inventory-II

90. nce-s-ass-042 · Assessment and Testing · personality inventories (MMPI-3, NEO-PI) · hard · In review

On an MMPI-3 completed during a disability evaluation, the inconsistent-responding scales are within normal limits, but the infrequent-responding (overreporting) scales are markedly elevated, as are many clinical scales. What is the MOST appropriate interpretation?
A. Random responding, so the client probably could not read the items
Normal inconsistency scales argue against random or content-unrelated responding.
B. Defensive underreporting, so true problems are likely even more severe
Underreporting is shown by the underreporting scales, not by elevated infrequent-responding scales.
C. A valid profile, so the elevations confirm severe psychopathology
Marked elevations on overreporting scales call the validity of substantive scale scores into question.
D. Possible overreporting, so the clinical scales need cautious reading ✔ KEY
Elevated infrequent-responding scales with consistent responding suggest symptom overreporting, which limits face-value interpretation.
Rationale: MMPI-3 validity scales fall into three groups: inconsistent responding (CRIN, VRIN, TRIN), overreporting (F, Fp, Fs, FBS, RBS), and underreporting (L, K). The validity scales are reviewed before any substantive scale. When overreporting indicators are markedly elevated, substantive elevations may reflect exaggeration rather than true symptoms, particularly in settings with external incentives.
References: Hays (Assessment) — MMPI-3 validity scales: inconsistency, overreporting, underreporting
⚑ Check: Verify the MMPI-3 validity scale names and groupings listed in the rationale against the current manual.

91. nce-s-ass-043 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · intake interview structure · medium · In review

A counselor uses the SCID-5 at intake. It follows a set order of questions keyed to DSM-5 criteria but lets the counselor ask follow-up questions and use clinical judgment in rating each criterion. How is this interview format BEST classified?
A. Fully structured, read word for word with no clinical judgment
Fully structured interviews (often designed for lay interviewers) leave no room for follow-up probes or clinician judgment.
B. Unstructured, with topics guided mainly by the client's story
Unstructured interviews have no set question sequence, unlike the SCID-5.
C. Semi-structured, a set guide with room for clinician probes ✔ KEY
The SCID-5 is a semi-structured interview: standard questions plus clinician follow-up and judgment.
D. A mental status exam, since it records observed presentation
An MSE describes current presentation; the SCID-5 is a diagnostic interview organized by DSM criteria.
Rationale: Structured interviews maximize reliability and coverage but limit flexibility; unstructured interviews maximize flexibility and rapport but are less reliable and can miss areas. Semi-structured interviews such as the SCID-5 combine a standard framework with clinician probes, balancing reliability and clinical depth.
References: Hays (Assessment) — Clinical interviews: structured, semi-structured, and unstructured

92. nce-s-ass-044 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · mental status examination · easy · In review

Asked how she has been feeling, a 40-year-old client says, "Honestly, I'm good, really good." Throughout the interview she is tearful, her voice trembles, and her facial expression changes little. How should the counselor document this in the mental status examination?
A. Mood as the observed tears; affect as her statement of "good"
This reverses the terms: mood is self-reported, affect is observed.
B. Mood reported as "good"; affect tearful and mood-incongruent ✔ KEY
Mood is the client's stated emotional state; affect is the counselor's observation, here incongruent with the stated mood.
C. Thought content as denial; thought process as tearful and flat
Tearfulness is an affect finding, and thought process describes how ideas connect, not emotional expression.
D. Insight as intact, because she can describe how she is feeling
Her report conflicts with her presentation, so this does not show intact insight into her condition.
Rationale: In the MSE, mood is the sustained emotion the client reports (often quoted), and affect is the observed expression of emotion, described by range, intensity, stability, and congruence with mood and content. Thought process is how thoughts connect; thought content is what the client thinks about; insight and judgment are rated separately.
References: Hays (Assessment) — Mental status examination: mood and affect

93. nce-s-ass-045 · Assessment and Testing · collateral information and records · medium · In review

A 9-year-old is referred for inattention and angry outbursts. On a behavior rating scale, the parent's ratings are highly elevated, the teacher's ratings are in the average range, and the child's self-report is low. How should the counselor interpret these results?
A. Informant differences are common and show where problems appear ✔ KEY
Discrepancies often reflect real differences across settings and relationships, and they guide further assessment.
B. The teacher's ratings are most objective, so set the parent's aside
No single informant is automatically correct; discarding the parent data loses information about home behavior.
C. Self-reports from children this age are invalid and add nothing
Children can provide useful information, especially about internal states, when measures fit their age.
D. The gap shows the parent is exaggerating, so assessment can end
The discrepancy is a reason to explore further, not evidence of exaggeration or a reason to stop.
Rationale: Assessment of children and adolescents relies on multiple informants (parents, teachers, the youth) and multiple methods, as in the Achenbach System of Empirically Based Assessment. Low cross-informant agreement is typical, because behavior varies by setting and each rater sees a different slice. Counselors integrate the reports with observation and history rather than choosing one.
References: Hays (Assessment) — Assessing children: multiple informants and settings

94. nce-s-ass-046 · Assessment and Testing · DSM-5-TR diagnostic structure and specifiers · hard · In review

For the past four weeks, a 33-year-old client has had depressed mood and two other depressive symptoms, causing clear impairment at work. There is no identifiable stressor, and no other mental disorder or medical cause explains the symptoms. The counselor wants the record to state why full criteria are not met. Which diagnosis BEST fits?
A. Unspecified depressive disorder, because no full criteria are met
Unspecified is used when the clinician chooses not to give the reason or lacks the information; here the counselor wants to state it.
B. Major depressive disorder, single episode, mild in severity
A major depressive episode requires at least five symptoms; this client has three.
C. Adjustment disorder with depressed mood, given the impairment
Adjustment disorder requires an identifiable stressor, which is absent.
D. Other specified depressive disorder, noting the specific reason ✔ KEY
Other specified is used when the clinician records the reason the presentation falls short, such as insufficient symptoms.
Rationale: DSM-5-TR offers two options for presentations that cause distress or impairment but do not meet full criteria for a disorder in a class. "Other specified" is used when the clinician chooses to communicate the specific reason (for example, a depressive episode with insufficient symptoms). "Unspecified" is used when the clinician chooses not to specify the reason, including when there is not enough information, as in an emergency setting.
References: DSM-5-TR — Use of other specified and unspecified disorders; Other Specified Depressive Disorder

95. nce-s-ass-047 · Social and Cultural Diversity · culturally fair assessment · easy · In review

A counselor is seeing a 38-year-old client who recently immigrated and describes her distress mainly in terms of family duty and spiritual concerns. The counselor wants a DSM-5-TR tool that explores the client's own explanation of the problem, cultural identity, sources of support, and expectations of help. Which tool should the counselor use?
A. Level 1 Cross-Cutting Symptom Measure
This measure screens symptom domains; it does not explore cultural explanations or help-seeking.
B. WHO Disability Assessment Schedule 2.0
The WHODAS 2.0 assesses functioning and disability, not cultural understanding of the problem.
C. Cultural Formulation Interview ✔ KEY
The CFI asks about the cultural definition of the problem, perceived causes, cultural identity, stressors and supports, and help-seeking.
D. Structured Clinical Interview (SCID-5)
The SCID-5 is a diagnostic interview organized by criteria, not a cultural assessment.
Rationale: The DSM-5-TR Cultural Formulation Interview is a person-centered, semi-structured set of questions in Section III. It covers the cultural definition of the problem, cultural perceptions of cause, context, and support, cultural factors affecting coping and help-seeking, and current help-seeking expectations. Informant and supplementary modules extend it.
References: DSM-5-TR — Section III: Cultural Formulation Interview · Sue & Sue (Counseling the Culturally Diverse) — Culturally sensitive assessment
☐ Approve☐ Revise☐ RetireNotes:

96. nce-s-ass-048 · Assessment and Testing · screening instruments (PHQ-9, GAD-7, AUDIT, CAGE) · medium · In review

A 76-year-old client's daughter reports that he has trouble finding words and has missed paying several bills. On a brief cognitive screen such as the Montreal Cognitive Assessment (MoCA), he scores below the screening cutoff. What should the counselor do NEXT?
A. Diagnose a major neurocognitive disorder based on the score
A screening score cannot establish a neurocognitive diagnosis or its cause.
B. Refer for a full medical and neuropsychological evaluation ✔ KEY
A positive screen calls for comprehensive evaluation to clarify the cause, including reversible conditions.
C. Readminister the same screen next week to confirm the result
Repeating the screen delays evaluation and adds practice effects without clarifying the cause.
D. Reassure the family that these changes reflect normal aging
Word-finding problems and new difficulty with finances go beyond expected age-related change and need evaluation.
Rationale: Cognitive screens such as the MoCA or MMSE flag possible impairment but are influenced by education, language, hearing, vision, and mood. A positive screen in an older adult should prompt medical evaluation (to rule out causes such as medication effects, thyroid problems, delirium, or depression) and fuller neuropsychological testing before any diagnosis is made.
References: Hays (Assessment) — Assessment of older adults: cognitive screening · DSM-5-TR — Neurocognitive disorders: major and mild NCD
☐ Approve☐ Revise☐ RetireNotes:

97. nce-s-clf-001 · Assessment and Testing · depressive disorders · easy · In review

Under DSM-5-TR, what is the minimum duration for the symptoms of a major depressive episode?
A. One week
One week is the minimum duration for a manic episode, not a major depressive episode.
B. Two weeks ✔ KEY
A major depressive episode requires five or more symptoms present during the same 2-week period.
C. One month
One month is not the major depressive episode threshold; it is used for other diagnoses, such as the active phase of schizophrenia.
D. Six months
Six months is the duration for generalized anxiety disorder, not a major depressive episode.
Rationale: DSM-5-TR requires at least five of nine symptoms, including depressed mood or loss of interest or pleasure, present nearly every day during the same 2-week period and representing a change from prior functioning. Knowing duration thresholds helps counselors tell a depressive episode apart from shorter, situational low mood.
References: DSM-5-TR — Major Depressive Disorder, Criterion A
☐ Approve☐ Revise☐ RetireNotes:

98. nce-s-clf-002 · Assessment and Testing · depressive disorders · medium · In review

A 41-year-old client reports feeling down more days than not for the past 3 years, with low energy, poor appetite, and hopelessness. She cannot recall a stretch of more than a few weeks without these symptoms and has never had a manic or hypomanic episode. Full criteria for a major depressive episode have not been met. Which diagnosis BEST fits?
A. Cyclothymic disorder
Cyclothymic disorder requires numerous periods of hypomanic symptoms alongside depressive symptoms; she reports no elevated mood.
B. Adjustment disorder with depressed mood
Adjustment disorder follows an identifiable stressor and does not persist more than 6 months after the stressor ends; this is a chronic 3-year pattern.
C. Disruptive mood dysregulation disorder
DMDD is a childhood diagnosis (first given between ages 6 and 18, onset before 10) marked by severe temper outbursts.
D. Persistent depressive disorder ✔ KEY
Depressed mood more days than not for at least 2 years in an adult, with two or more associated symptoms and no symptom-free period longer than 2 months, fits persistent depressive disorder.
Rationale: Persistent depressive disorder requires depressed mood for most of the day, more days than not, for at least 2 years in adults (1 year in children and adolescents), plus at least two associated symptoms such as appetite change, low energy, or hopelessness. During that period the person has not been without symptoms for more than 2 months at a time, and there has never been a manic or hypomanic episode.
References: DSM-5-TR — Persistent Depressive Disorder, Criteria A–C
☐ Approve☐ Revise☐ RetireNotes:

99. nce-s-clf-003 · Human Growth and Development · depressive disorders · hard · In review

A 15-year-old has shown irritable mood on most days for the past 14 months, along with low self-esteem and poor concentration. He has not gone more than a few weeks without these symptoms, has no history of severe temper outbursts, and has never met criteria for a manic or hypomanic episode. Which statement is MOST accurate?
A. Criteria for persistent depressive disorder can be met at this point ✔ KEY
For children and adolescents, mood may be irritable and the duration requirement is 1 year, so 14 months is sufficient.
B. Persistent depressive disorder requires 2 full years in any age group
The 2-year requirement applies to adults; DSM-5-TR shortens it to 1 year for children and adolescents.
C. Irritable rather than sad mood rules out a depressive disorder diagnosis
DSM-5-TR allows irritable mood to satisfy the mood criterion for depressive disorders in children and adolescents.
D. Disruptive mood dysregulation disorder is the required diagnosis here
DMDD requires severe, recurrent temper outbursts with onset before age 10, which this teen does not show.
Rationale: DSM-5-TR modifies persistent depressive disorder for youth in two ways: mood can be irritable rather than depressed, and the minimum duration is 1 year instead of 2. Counselors working with adolescents should recognize that chronic irritability can be a depressive presentation rather than simply a behavior problem.
References: DSM-5-TR — Persistent Depressive Disorder, Criterion A (children and adolescents)
☐ Approve☐ Revise☐ RetireNotes:

100. nce-s-clf-004 · Assessment and Testing · bipolar disorders · easy · In review

Unless hospitalization is required, what is the minimum duration of a manic episode in DSM-5-TR?
A. Four consecutive days
Four consecutive days is the minimum for a hypomanic episode, not a manic episode.
B. Two weeks
Two weeks is the duration requirement for a major depressive episode.
C. One week ✔ KEY
A manic episode lasts at least 1 week and is present most of the day, nearly every day, or any duration if hospitalization is needed.
D. One month
One month is not a mood episode threshold; it is too long for mania.
Rationale: A manic episode is a distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood plus increased activity or energy lasting at least 1 week (any duration if hospitalization is necessary). A hypomanic episode needs only 4 consecutive days and does not cause marked impairment or require hospitalization.
References: DSM-5-TR — Bipolar I Disorder, Manic Episode Criterion A
☐ Approve☐ Revise☐ RetireNotes:

101. nce-s-clf-005 · Assessment and Testing · bipolar disorders · medium · In review

A 30-year-old client has had two major depressive episodes. Last spring she had 5 days of unusually elevated mood, less need for sleep, and increased productivity that friends noticed but that did not impair her work or require hospitalization. She has never had psychotic symptoms or a longer elevated period. Which diagnosis is MOST appropriate?
A. Bipolar II disorder ✔ KEY
At least one hypomanic episode (4+ days, no marked impairment) plus at least one major depressive episode, with no history of mania, defines bipolar II disorder.
B. Bipolar I disorder
Bipolar I requires at least one full manic episode; her elevated period was hypomanic.
C. Cyclothymic disorder
Cyclothymic disorder requires symptoms that never meet full criteria for a major depressive episode; she has had two.
D. Major depressive disorder
A history of a hypomanic episode excludes a diagnosis of major depressive disorder.
Rationale: Bipolar II disorder requires at least one hypomanic episode and at least one major depressive episode, with no lifetime manic episode. Hypomania lasts at least 4 consecutive days, is observable by others, and is not severe enough to cause marked impairment or hospitalization. Screening for past hypomania is essential before treating depression as unipolar.
References: DSM-5-TR — Bipolar II Disorder, Criteria A–B; Hypomanic Episode
☐ Approve☐ Revise☐ RetireNotes:

102. nce-s-clf-006 · Assessment and Testing · bipolar disorders · hard · In review

A 27-year-old with no prior psychiatric history develops grandiosity, pressured speech, almost no sleep, and reckless spending. On the third day he is psychiatrically hospitalized because his behavior has become dangerous. Substances and medical causes are ruled out. Which statement is MOST accurate?
A. The episode is hypomanic because it lasted fewer than 4 days
Hypomania itself requires at least 4 days and, by definition, does not require hospitalization.
B. No bipolar diagnosis applies until symptoms reach 1 full week
The 1-week requirement is waived when hospitalization is necessary.
C. Bipolar I also requires a prior major depressive episode on record
A single manic episode is sufficient for bipolar I; a depressive episode is common but not required.
D. The episode is manic since hospitalization waives the duration ✔ KEY
DSM-5-TR counts a manic episode of any duration if hospitalization is necessary, so bipolar I disorder can be diagnosed.
Rationale: The 1-week duration for a manic episode applies unless hospitalization is necessary, in which case any duration qualifies. Bipolar I disorder requires only one lifetime manic episode; major depressive episodes are common but are not required for the diagnosis.
References: DSM-5-TR — Bipolar I Disorder, Manic Episode Criterion A and Criterion for Bipolar I
☐ Approve☐ Revise☐ RetireNotes:

103. nce-s-clf-007 · Assessment and Testing · anxiety disorders · easy · In review

For a diagnosis of generalized anxiety disorder, excessive anxiety and worry must occur more days than not for at least how long?
A. 1 month
One month is the post-attack worry period for panic disorder, not the GAD duration.
B. 6 months ✔ KEY
GAD requires excessive anxiety and worry more days than not for at least 6 months.
C. 3 months
Three months is not the GAD threshold.
D. 2 years
Two years is the adult duration for persistent depressive disorder.
Rationale: Generalized anxiety disorder involves excessive, hard-to-control worry about a number of events or activities, occurring more days than not for at least 6 months, with three or more associated symptoms in adults (only one in children), such as restlessness, fatigue, or sleep disturbance.
References: DSM-5-TR — Generalized Anxiety Disorder, Criterion A

104. nce-s-clf-008 · Assessment and Testing · anxiety disorders · medium · In review

A 36-year-old client has had several sudden, unexpected surges of intense fear with a pounding heart, shortness of breath, and dizziness. For the past 2 months he has worried constantly about having another attack and has stopped exercising because a racing heart might trigger one. Medical causes have been ruled out. Which diagnosis BEST fits?
A. Panic disorder ✔ KEY
Recurrent unexpected panic attacks followed by at least 1 month of persistent worry or maladaptive behavior change define panic disorder.
B. Agoraphobia
Agoraphobia requires fear or avoidance in at least two situations such as crowds or public transit; his avoidance centers on bodily sensations.
C. Illness anxiety disorder
Illness anxiety centers on a preoccupation with having a serious illness, not on recurrent unexpected panic attacks.
D. Specific phobia, situational
Specific phobia involves fear cued by a particular object or situation, whereas these attacks are unexpected.
Rationale: Panic disorder requires recurrent unexpected panic attacks, with at least one attack followed by 1 month or more of persistent concern about further attacks or their consequences, or a significant maladaptive change in behavior such as avoiding exercise. Interoceptive avoidance like this is a common target of cognitive behavioral treatment.
References: DSM-5-TR — Panic Disorder, Criteria A–B · Barlow PCT — Interoceptive avoidance and exposure
