156. nce-s-clf-060 · Assessment and Testing · depressive disorders · hard · In review

A 44-year-old with major depressive disorder has had a depressive episode each of the last three winters, with full remission every spring. A counselor is considering the "with seasonal pattern" specifier. Which finding would argue MOST strongly AGAINST using it?
A. She sleeps more and craves carbohydrates in her episodes
Hypersomnia and carbohydrate craving are common in winter-pattern episodes and are consistent with the specifier.
B. She lives far north, where winter daylight is quite short
Higher latitude is associated with winter-pattern depression and does not argue against the specifier.
C. Her construction job lays her off each winter, without pay ✔ KEY
DSM-5-TR excludes cases in which an obvious seasonal psychosocial stressor, such as regular seasonal unemployment, explains the timing.
D. Each episode has ended completely by the following April
Full remission at a characteristic time of year is one of the requirements for the specifier.
Rationale: The seasonal pattern specifier requires a regular temporal relationship between episode onset and a particular time of year, full remission (or a switch to mania or hypomania) at a characteristic time, two seasonal episodes in the last 2 years with no nonseasonal episodes in that period, and seasonal episodes substantially outnumbering nonseasonal ones over the lifetime. It does not apply when an obvious season-linked psychosocial stressor, such as being unemployed every winter, accounts for the pattern.
References: DSM-5-TR — Specifiers for Depressive Disorders: with seasonal pattern
☐ Approve☐ Revise☐ RetireNotes:

157. nce-s-clf-061 · Human Growth and Development · depressive disorders · easy · In review

A 29-year-old developed a major depressive episode during the second trimester of her first pregnancy. Her midwife assumed the "with peripartum onset" specifier could only be used after delivery. Which statement is accurate under DSM-5-TR?
A. It applies to onset in pregnancy or within 4 weeks after delivery ✔ KEY
DSM-5-TR applies the peripartum onset specifier when mood symptoms begin during pregnancy or in the 4 weeks following delivery.
B. It applies only to episodes that begin after the baby is delivered
DSM-5 replaced the older "postpartum onset" specifier with "peripartum onset" to include onset during pregnancy.
C. It applies to any onset up to one year following the delivery date
One year is used in some research and screening, but the DSM-5-TR specifier window is 4 weeks after delivery.
D. It can be used only when the episode includes psychotic features
Psychotic features are not required; peripartum episodes may occur with or without psychosis.
Rationale: DSM-5-TR uses "with peripartum onset" when the onset of mood symptoms occurs during pregnancy or in the 4 weeks following delivery. A substantial share of postpartum major depressive episodes actually begin during pregnancy, which is why the earlier postpartum-only specifier was broadened. Peripartum episodes should be distinguished from the brief, milder "baby blues."
References: DSM-5-TR — Specifiers for Depressive Disorders: with peripartum onset
☐ Approve☐ Revise☐ RetireNotes:

158. nce-s-clf-062 · Assessment and Testing · depressive disorders · easy · In review

A client in a current major depressive episode also says she feels keyed up and tense most days and has a nagging fear that something awful is about to happen. She does not meet criteria for an anxiety disorder. Which specifier BEST captures these features?
A. With mixed features
"With mixed features" refers to manic or hypomanic symptoms during a depressive episode, not anxiety.
B. With anxious distress ✔ KEY
Feeling keyed up or tense and fearing that something awful may happen are two of the five anxious distress symptoms; two are required.
C. With melancholic features
Melancholic features center on near-total loss of pleasure, early-morning awakening, and marked psychomotor change.
D. With atypical features
Atypical features involve mood reactivity plus symptoms such as hypersomnia, leaden paralysis, and rejection sensitivity.
Rationale: The anxious distress specifier requires at least two of five symptoms during most days of the episode: feeling keyed up or tense, feeling unusually restless, difficulty concentrating because of worry, fear that something awful may happen, and feeling one might lose control. Anxious distress is linked to higher suicide risk, longer illness, and poorer treatment response, so it is worth assessing even when no separate anxiety disorder is present.
References: DSM-5-TR — Specifiers for Depressive Disorders: with anxious distress
☐ Approve☐ Revise☐ RetireNotes:

159. nce-s-clf-063 · Assessment and Testing · bipolar disorders · medium · In review

For about 3 years, a 34-year-old graphic designer has swung between stretches of high energy, talkativeness, and big plans and stretches of low mood and fatigue. Neither the highs nor the lows have ever met full criteria for a hypomanic, manic, or major depressive episode, and she is rarely symptom-free for more than a month. Which diagnosis BEST fits?
A. Bipolar II disorder
Bipolar II requires at least one full hypomanic episode and at least one major depressive episode.
B. Persistent depressive disorder
Persistent depressive disorder involves chronic depressed mood without the recurring hypomanic symptoms she reports.
C. Bipolar I disorder, mild
Bipolar I requires at least one full manic episode, which has never occurred.
D. Cyclothymic disorder ✔ KEY
Two or more years of numerous subthreshold hypomanic and depressive periods, with no gap over 2 months and no full episodes, fits cyclothymic disorder.
Rationale: Cyclothymic disorder requires at least 2 years (1 year in children and adolescents) of numerous periods with hypomanic symptoms and numerous periods with depressive symptoms that never meet full episode criteria. Symptoms are present at least half the time, the person is never symptom-free for more than 2 months, and criteria for a major depressive, manic, or hypomanic episode have never been met.
References: DSM-5-TR — Cyclothymic Disorder, Criteria A–C
☐ Approve☐ Revise☐ RetireNotes:

160. nce-s-clf-064 · Assessment and Testing · bipolar disorders · hard · In review

Three weeks after starting an antidepressant for depression, a 38-year-old develops elevated mood, grandiosity, little need for sleep, and reckless spending. His prescriber stops the medication, but the full manic syndrome continues well beyond the drug's expected physiological effect. Which statement is MOST accurate?
A. Mania that starts on an antidepressant is coded only as medication-induced
Onset during antidepressant treatment does not by itself settle the question; persistence beyond the drug effect matters.
B. Persisting at full level past the drug effect supports bipolar I disorder ✔ KEY
DSM-5-TR states that a full manic episode persisting beyond the physiological effect of antidepressant treatment is sufficient evidence for bipolar I.
C. Bipolar II should be diagnosed because a medication triggered the mood
Bipolar II requires hypomanic, not manic, episodes; the trigger does not change a full manic syndrome into hypomania.
D. No bipolar diagnosis can be made until a medication-free year has passed
DSM-5-TR sets no medication-free waiting period; the criterion is persistence beyond the physiological effect.
Rationale: DSM-5-TR notes that a full manic episode emerging during antidepressant treatment (medication or electroconvulsive therapy) that persists at a fully syndromal level beyond the physiological effect of that treatment is sufficient evidence for a manic episode and therefore a bipolar I diagnosis. If the symptoms resolve as the drug effect wears off, a substance/medication-induced bipolar and related disorder is more appropriate.
References: DSM-5-TR — Bipolar I Disorder, Manic Episode Criterion D note on antidepressant treatment
☐ Approve☐ Revise☐ RetireNotes:

161. nce-s-clf-065 · Assessment and Testing · anxiety disorders · medium · In review

For 8 months, a 47-year-old has avoided buses, crowded grocery stores, and long checkout lines. She fears that if she suddenly felt faint or lost bladder control, escape would be hard and no one would help. She has never had an unexpected panic attack and is not worried about being judged by others. Which diagnosis BEST fits?
A. Specific phobia, situational type
Specific phobia centers on one circumscribed situation; fear across several situations for this escape-related reason points to agoraphobia.
B. Social anxiety disorder
Social anxiety centers on fear of scrutiny or negative evaluation, which she denies.
C. Agoraphobia ✔ KEY
Fear in two or more agoraphobic situations because escape may be difficult or help unavailable if incapacitating symptoms occur fits agoraphobia.
D. Panic disorder
Panic disorder requires recurrent unexpected panic attacks, which she has never had.
Rationale: Agoraphobia requires marked fear or anxiety about at least two of five situations: public transportation, open spaces, enclosed spaces, standing in line or being in a crowd, and being outside the home alone. The fear is driven by thoughts that escape might be difficult or help unavailable if panic-like or other incapacitating or embarrassing symptoms occur. Since DSM-5, agoraphobia can be diagnosed without panic disorder, and it typically lasts 6 months or more.
References: DSM-5-TR — Agoraphobia, Criteria A–B
☐ Approve☐ Revise☐ RetireNotes:

162. nce-s-clf-066 · Social and Cultural Diversity · anxiety disorders · medium · In review

A 6-year-old has not spoken at school for 4 months, although she talks freely with her parents at home. Her teacher refers her for evaluation of selective mutism. Which additional finding would make selective mutism LESS likely as the explanation?
A. Her family moved recently and she is still learning the school language ✔ KEY
DSM-5-TR excludes failure to speak that is due to lack of knowledge of, or comfort with, the language required in that setting.
B. She whispers to one close friend at recess but to no one else there
Limited speech with one trusted peer is common in selective mutism and does not argue against it.
C. Her silence has lasted well beyond the first month of the school year
Duration of at least 1 month, not limited to the first month of school, is a requirement and supports the diagnosis.
D. Her parents describe her as very shy and anxious in new situations
Shyness and social anxiety are commonly associated with selective mutism.
Rationale: Selective mutism is a consistent failure to speak in specific social situations where speech is expected, such as school, despite speaking in other situations. It must last at least 1 month (not limited to the first month of school), interfere with achievement or communication, and not be due to lack of knowledge of or comfort with the spoken language. Counselors working with newly arrived immigrant children should consider normal second-language "silent periods" before diagnosing.
References: DSM-5-TR — Selective Mutism, Criteria A–D
☐ Approve☐ Revise☐ RetireNotes:

163. nce-s-clf-067 · Assessment and Testing · trauma- and stressor-related disorders · medium · In review

Five weeks after being unexpectedly laid off from a job of 20 years, a 56-year-old reports sadness, frequent replaying of the termination meeting, poor sleep, and withdrawal from friends. His distress is out of proportion to what is typical and is affecting family life. Criteria for a major depressive episode are not met. Which diagnosis is MOST appropriate?
A. Posttraumatic stress disorder
PTSD requires exposure to actual or threatened death, serious injury, or sexual violence; a layoff does not meet Criterion A.
B. Acute stress disorder
Acute stress disorder has the same Criterion A trauma requirement as PTSD, which a layoff does not satisfy.
C. Major depressive disorder
The stem states that criteria for a major depressive episode are not met.
D. Adjustment disorder ✔ KEY
Distressing symptoms beginning within 3 months of an identifiable, non-traumatic stressor, out of proportion and impairing, fit adjustment disorder.
Rationale: Adjustment disorder involves emotional or behavioral symptoms that begin within 3 months of an identifiable stressor, are out of proportion to the stressor or impair functioning, and do not meet criteria for another mental disorder. Unlike PTSD and acute stress disorder, the stressor need not involve death, serious injury, or sexual violence. Symptoms should resolve within 6 months after the stressor or its consequences end.
References: DSM-5-TR — Adjustment Disorders, Criteria A–E
☐ Approve☐ Revise☐ RetireNotes:

164. nce-s-clf-068 · Human Growth and Development · trauma- and stressor-related disorders · medium · In review

A 4-year-old was adopted at age 2 after living in an understaffed orphanage. Her adoptive parents say she happily wanders off with strangers at the park, climbs onto unfamiliar adults' laps, and does not look back to check on them. Which diagnosis BEST fits this pattern?
A. Reactive attachment disorder, persistent
Reactive attachment disorder involves an inhibited, emotionally withdrawn child who rarely seeks or responds to comfort.
B. Disinhibited social engagement disorder ✔ KEY
Overly familiar behavior with strangers, wandering off without checking back, and a history of insufficient care define this disorder.
C. Attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder
ADHD impulsivity is not specific to indiscriminate social approach and is not tied to a history of social neglect.
D. Autism spectrum disorder, level 1
Autism involves deficits in social communication and restricted interests, not eager, indiscriminate approach to strangers.
Rationale: Both reactive attachment disorder and disinhibited social engagement disorder follow a pattern of extremely insufficient care, such as neglect or institutional rearing. Reactive attachment disorder is an inhibited, withdrawn pattern with minimal comfort seeking, while disinhibited social engagement disorder is a pattern of culturally inappropriate, overly familiar behavior with unfamiliar adults and diminished checking back with caregivers. The child must have a developmental age of at least 9 months.
References: DSM-5-TR — Disinhibited Social Engagement Disorder, Criteria A–C

165. nce-s-clf-069 · Assessment and Testing · obsessive-compulsive and related disorders · medium · In review

A 22-year-old man who lifts weights daily is convinced his body looks small and weak, although friends describe him as very muscular. He checks mirrors for hours, wears baggy layers to hide his build, and skips social events. He is not focused on body fat or thinness. Which diagnosis BEST fits?
A. Anorexia nervosa, restricting type
Anorexia nervosa involves significantly low body weight and fear of weight gain, not a belief of being too small.
B. Obsessive-compulsive disorder
When obsessions and rituals center only on perceived appearance flaws, body dysmorphic disorder is the more specific diagnosis.
C. Body dysmorphic disorder ✔ KEY
Preoccupation with a perceived appearance flaw plus repetitive checking fits BDD; the belief that his build is too small calls for the muscle dysmorphia specifier.
D. Illness anxiety disorder
Illness anxiety disorder centers on fear of having a serious illness, not on perceived appearance flaws.
Rationale: Body dysmorphic disorder is preoccupation with perceived defects in appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others, along with repetitive behaviors such as mirror checking or comparing, causing distress or impairment. The "with muscle dysmorphia" specifier applies when the person believes his or her build is too small or insufficiently muscular. DSM-5-TR also specifies the degree of insight.
References: DSM-5-TR — Body Dysmorphic Disorder, Criteria A–D and muscle dysmorphia specifier

166. nce-s-clf-070 · Assessment and Testing · obsessive-compulsive and related disorders · easy · In review

A 66-year-old describes himself as "a collector." Which finding MOST clearly points to hoarding disorder rather than ordinary collecting?
A. Clutter fills living areas, and parting with items causes distress ✔ KEY
Persistent difficulty discarding, distress at parting with items, and clutter that blocks the use of living spaces are the core of hoarding disorder.
B. He owns several hundred items of one specific type in his home
A large number of items is common in collecting; what matters is whether clutter compromises living areas.
C. He spends a good share of his spare money on additions each year
Spending on a hobby is not diagnostic; collectors often budget substantial amounts.
D. He feels warm sentimental attachment to items that were gifts
Sentimental attachment to meaningful objects is normal and not in itself a sign of hoarding disorder.
Rationale: Hoarding disorder is persistent difficulty discarding or parting with possessions, regardless of their actual value, due to a perceived need to save them and distress associated with discarding. The result is an accumulation that congests and clutters active living areas and substantially compromises their intended use. Collecting, by contrast, tends to be organized and intentional and does not block the use of living space. A specifier notes whether excessive acquisition is present.
References: DSM-5-TR — Hoarding Disorder, Criteria A–C
☐ Approve☐ Revise☐ RetireNotes:

167. nce-s-clf-071 · Assessment and Testing · substance use and addictive disorders · medium · In review

Two days after stopping several weeks of heavy methamphetamine use, a client reports feeling deeply down and exhausted. He is sleeping for long stretches, has vivid unpleasant dreams, and is eating much more than usual. Which withdrawal syndrome do these symptoms MOST closely match?
A. Opioid withdrawal
Opioid withdrawal features flu-like signs such as tearing, runny nose, yawning, dilated pupils, muscle aches, and diarrhea.
B. Cannabis withdrawal
Cannabis withdrawal typically brings irritability, anxiety, restlessness, trouble sleeping, and decreased appetite.
C. Alcohol withdrawal
Alcohol withdrawal involves autonomic hyperactivity, tremor, insomnia, and possible seizures, not hypersomnia with increased appetite.
D. Stimulant withdrawal ✔ KEY
Dysphoric mood with fatigue, vivid unpleasant dreams, sleep change, and increased appetite matches stimulant withdrawal.
Rationale: Stimulant withdrawal requires dysphoric mood plus at least two of: fatigue; vivid, unpleasant dreams; insomnia or hypersomnia; increased appetite; and psychomotor retardation or agitation, developing within hours to days after stopping. The "crash" can bring marked depression, so counselors should screen for suicidal thinking during early stimulant withdrawal.
References: DSM-5-TR — Stimulant Withdrawal, Criteria A–B
☐ Approve☐ Revise☐ RetireNotes:

168. nce-s-clf-072 · Assessment and Testing · substance use and addictive disorders · easy · In review

After losing a large sum at an online casino, a client logs back in the next morning, certain that a few more bets will win back what he lost. Which DSM-5-TR gambling disorder criterion does this behavior illustrate?
A. Tolerance
Tolerance in gambling disorder is needing to gamble with increasing amounts of money to achieve the desired excitement.
B. Chasing losses ✔ KEY
Returning another day to get even after losing money is the "chasing" criterion of gambling disorder.
C. Relying on bailouts
This criterion refers to relying on others for money to relieve a desperate financial situation caused by gambling.
D. Withdrawal
Withdrawal refers to restlessness or irritability when attempting to cut down or stop gambling.
Rationale: Gambling disorder is the only behavioral (non-substance) addiction in the DSM-5-TR substance-related and addictive disorders chapter. It requires at least four of nine criteria within 12 months, including chasing losses, needing larger amounts to get the desired excitement, restlessness when cutting down, lying to conceal gambling, and relying on others for bailouts. Chasing is considered one of the hallmark features of problem gambling.
References: DSM-5-TR — Gambling Disorder, Criterion A6 (chasing losses)

169. nce-s-clf-073 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · substance use and addictive disorders · medium · In review

A 30-year-old with opioid use disorder says he is not ready to stop using but wants to keep seeing the counselor. Which counselor action BEST reflects a harm reduction approach?
A. Require a period of abstinence before further sessions are offered
Making abstinence a condition of care pushes away clients who are not ready and conflicts with harm reduction.
B. Confront his denial each session until he admits he needs to quit
Confrontational approaches tend to raise resistance and are not part of harm reduction or motivational work.
C. Help him get naloxone and plan not to use alone, while exploring change ✔ KEY
Reducing overdose risk now, while keeping him engaged and exploring change, is the essence of harm reduction.
D. Refer him to a mutual-help group and end individual counseling for now
Mutual-help groups can help, but ending counseling removes support when he is open to engagement.
Rationale: Harm reduction meets clients where they are, aiming to reduce the health and social harms of substance use without requiring abstinence first. For opioid use, this includes naloxone access, not using alone, and sterile supplies, alongside offering medications for opioid use disorder. Staying engaged also leaves room for motivational work that may later lead to reduced use or recovery.
References: SAMHSA TIP 63 — Medications for Opioid Use Disorder: overdose prevention and naloxone
⚑ Check: Confirm that SAMHSA TIP 63 is the best approved citation for naloxone distribution as a harm reduction practice.

170. nce-s-clf-074 · Assessment and Testing · neurodevelopmental disorders · easy · In review

Under DSM-5-TR, the severity level of intellectual developmental disorder (mild, moderate, severe, profound) is determined mainly by which factor?
A. Adaptive functioning in conceptual, social and practical areas ✔ KEY
DSM-5-TR defines severity by adaptive functioning, because that determines the level of support needed.
B. The full-scale IQ score range obtained on standardized testing
IQ is part of assessing intellectual deficits, but DSM-5 moved severity away from IQ ranges.
C. The age at which the intellectual deficits were first observed
Onset during the developmental period is required, but age of onset does not set severity.
D. Whether a specific genetic or medical cause has been identified
Etiology may be noted, but it does not determine the severity level.
Rationale: Intellectual developmental disorder requires deficits in intellectual functions and in adaptive functioning, with onset during the developmental period. DSM-5-TR bases the severity specifiers on adaptive functioning across conceptual, social, and practical domains rather than IQ scores, because adaptive functioning determines the level of supports required. IQ scores remain part of the assessment but are less valid at the lower end of the range.
References: DSM-5-TR — Intellectual Developmental Disorder, severity specifiers

171. nce-s-clf-075 · Human Growth and Development · neurodevelopmental disorders · medium · In review

An 11-year-old has had eye blinking and shoulder shrugging since age 9, and over the past year he has also developed frequent throat clearing and sniffing. The tics wax and wane but have never stopped for long. He has never used obscene words. Which diagnosis BEST fits?
A. Persistent (chronic) motor tic disorder
Persistent tic disorder involves motor or vocal tics, but not both; he has both.
B. Provisional tic disorder
Provisional tic disorder applies when tics have been present for less than 1 year.
C. Stereotypic movement disorder
Stereotypies are rhythmic, seemingly purposeless movements that begin earlier and lack the vocal tics described.
D. Tourette's disorder ✔ KEY
Multiple motor tics and at least one vocal tic, present for more than 1 year with onset before 18, meet criteria for Tourette's disorder.
Rationale: Tourette's disorder requires both multiple motor tics and one or more vocal tics at some time during the illness, although not necessarily at the same time. Tics may wax and wane but must have persisted for more than 1 year since the first tic, with onset before age 18. Coprolalia (uttering obscenities) occurs in only a minority of cases and is not required.
References: DSM-5-TR — Tourette's Disorder, Criteria A–D

172. nce-s-clf-076 · Assessment and Testing · schizophrenia spectrum disorders · hard · In review

For 8 months, a 58-year-old accountant has been certain that a neighbor is poisoning his garden and reading his mail. He has filed police reports and installed cameras. He reports no hallucinations, his speech is organized, and he continues to work effectively. No mood episodes or substance causes are present. Which diagnosis is MOST appropriate?
A. Schizophrenia
Schizophrenia requires two or more Criterion A symptoms and usually marked functional decline; he has a single fixed delusion and functions well.
B. Delusional disorder ✔ KEY
One or more delusions lasting 1 month or longer, without other Criterion A symptoms and with functioning not markedly impaired, fits delusional disorder (persecutory type).
C. Paranoid personality disorder
Paranoid personality disorder involves pervasive distrust and suspiciousness, not a fixed false belief held with delusional conviction.
D. Brief psychotic disorder
Brief psychotic disorder lasts at least 1 day but less than 1 month, and he has had symptoms for 8 months.
Rationale: Delusional disorder requires one or more delusions lasting at least 1 month, Criterion A for schizophrenia never having been met, and functioning that is not markedly impaired apart from the impact of the delusion. Behavior is not obviously bizarre. Subtypes include persecutory, jealous, erotomanic, grandiose, and somatic, and DSM-5-TR adds a "with bizarre content" specifier.
References: DSM-5-TR — Delusional Disorder, Criteria A–E and persecutory type

173. nce-s-clf-077 · Assessment and Testing · schizophrenia spectrum disorders · hard · In review

Over 2 years of illness, a 33-year-old has had major depressive episodes for most of the time, often with voices and persecutory beliefs. Between episodes there was a 3-week stretch when the voices and delusions continued while her mood was stable. She has never had mania, and substances are ruled out. Which diagnosis is MOST appropriate?
A. Major depressive disorder with psychotic features
In MDD with psychotic features, psychosis occurs only during mood episodes; here it persisted for weeks without one.
B. Schizophrenia with comorbid depressive episodes
Schizophrenia fits when mood episodes are present for only a minority of the illness; hers spanned most of it.
C. Schizoaffective disorder, depressive type ✔ KEY
Psychosis for 2 or more weeks without a mood episode, with mood episodes present for most of the illness, fits schizoaffective disorder.
D. Bipolar I disorder with psychotic features
Bipolar I requires a manic episode, which has never occurred.
Rationale: Schizoaffective disorder requires an uninterrupted period of illness in which a major mood episode co-occurs with Criterion A of schizophrenia, plus delusions or hallucinations for 2 or more weeks in the absence of a major mood episode at some point in the lifetime illness. Mood episodes must be present for the majority of the total duration of the active and residual illness. The depressive type applies when only major depressive episodes have occurred.
References: DSM-5-TR — Schizoaffective Disorder, Criteria A–C

174. nce-s-clf-078 · Assessment and Testing · personality disorders · hard · In review

A 26-year-old seeks counseling for "bipolar mood swings." She describes intense anger, chronic emptiness, and fear of being left by her partner. A counselor is weighing borderline personality disorder against a bipolar disorder. Which finding MOST supports borderline personality disorder?
A. Impulsive spending sprees during periods of high distress
Impulsive spending occurs in both borderline personality disorder and manic or hypomanic episodes, so it does not discriminate.
B. Mood shifts lasting hours, set off by perceived rejection ✔ KEY
Reactive mood shifts lasting hours and triggered by interpersonal events are typical of BPD, whereas bipolar episodes last days or longer.
C. Several past periods of low mood with fatigue and poor focus
Depressive periods occur in both conditions and do not distinguish them.
D. A biological parent with a documented bipolar I diagnosis
A family history of bipolar disorder raises the likelihood of a bipolar diagnosis rather than supporting BPD.
Rationale: Affective instability in borderline personality disorder involves marked reactivity of mood, with episodes usually lasting a few hours and rarely more than a few days, often triggered by interpersonal stress or perceived abandonment. Bipolar mood episodes are sustained, distinct periods (at least 4 days for hypomania, 1 week for mania) with changes such as decreased need for sleep that are not simply reactions to relationships. The two can co-occur, so careful history taking matters.
References: DSM-5-TR — Borderline Personality Disorder, Differential Diagnosis (bipolar disorders)

175. nce-s-clf-079 · Assessment and Testing · personality disorders · easy · In review

A 35-year-old has few close friends, dresses in an unusual way, and speaks in vague, odd phrases. She believes she can sense what strangers are thinking and that song lyrics on the radio carry special messages for her. Her social anxiety does not ease as she gets to know people. Which personality disorder BEST fits?
A. Schizoid personality disorder
Schizoid personality disorder involves detachment and restricted emotion without the odd beliefs and perceptual distortions described.
B. Avoidant personality disorder
Avoidant personality disorder (Cluster C) involves fear of rejection without magical thinking or ideas of reference.
C. Paranoid personality disorder
Paranoid personality disorder centers on distrust and suspicion of others' motives rather than eccentric beliefs and speech.
D. Schizotypal personality disorder ✔ KEY
Ideas of reference, magical thinking, odd speech and appearance, and persistent social anxiety define schizotypal personality disorder.
Rationale: Schizotypal personality disorder, in Cluster A, combines acute discomfort with close relationships and cognitive or perceptual distortions and eccentric behavior. Features include ideas of reference, odd beliefs or magical thinking, unusual perceptual experiences, odd speech, eccentric appearance, and excessive social anxiety that does not diminish with familiarity. Schizoid personality disorder shares the social detachment but lacks the cognitive and perceptual oddities.
References: DSM-5-TR — Schizotypal Personality Disorder, Criterion A

176. nce-s-clf-080 · Assessment and Testing · eating disorders · medium · In review

A 19-year-old reports recurrent binge-eating episodes followed by purging, along with an intense fear of gaining weight. Her physician notes that her body weight is significantly low for her age, sex, and developmental trajectory. Which diagnosis is MOST appropriate?
A. Anorexia nervosa ✔ KEY
Significantly low body weight with fear of weight gain and recurrent binge-purge behavior fits anorexia nervosa, binge-eating/purging type.
B. Bulimia nervosa
Bulimia nervosa cannot be diagnosed when the disturbance occurs exclusively during episodes of anorexia nervosa.
C. Binge-eating disorder
Binge-eating disorder excludes regular compensatory behaviors such as purging.
D. Purging disorder
Purging disorder (an other specified presentation) involves purging without binge eating, and she does binge; she also meets full criteria for anorexia nervosa.
Rationale: The key feature separating anorexia nervosa from bulimia nervosa is significantly low body weight. Binge eating and purging can occur in both, but when they happen in a person with significantly low weight and fear of weight gain, the diagnosis is anorexia nervosa, binge-eating/purging type. Bulimia nervosa Criterion E states the disturbance does not occur exclusively during episodes of anorexia nervosa.
References: DSM-5-TR — Anorexia Nervosa subtypes; Bulimia Nervosa, Criterion E

177. nce-s-clf-081 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · eating disorders · medium · In review

A client in outpatient counseling for bulimia nervosa says her purging has increased over the past month. Today she mentions feeling dizzy and noticing her heart "fluttering" at times. What should the counselor do?
A. Teach slow breathing, since palpitations often reflect anxiety
Attributing cardiac symptoms to anxiety before medical evaluation risks missing a dangerous physical complication.
B. Keep the session focused on the binge triggers and revisit later
Delaying attention to new physical warning signs is unsafe in an eating disorder with frequent purging.
C. Arrange prompt medical evaluation of her physical warning signs ✔ KEY
Frequent purging can cause electrolyte disturbances that affect heart rhythm, so dizziness and palpitations warrant prompt medical assessment.
D. Suggest she adjust her own fluid intake and recheck how she feels
Self-management advice is outside the counselor's scope and does not address possible electrolyte or cardiac problems.
Rationale: Bulimia nervosa carries significant medical risk; repeated purging can lead to fluid and electrolyte disturbances that may cause dangerous heart rhythm problems. Counselors should coordinate with medical providers and refer for prompt evaluation when clients report physical warning signs such as dizziness, fainting, or palpitations. Eating disorder care is best delivered by a team that includes medical monitoring.
References: NICE guidelines — NG69 Eating disorders: physical health assessment and monitoring

178. nce-s-clf-082 · Assessment and Testing · neurocognitive disorders · medium · In review

A 72-year-old has had a gradual decline in thinking over the past year. His wife reports that he is clear one day and very confused the next, often sees detailed images of children in the house who are not there, and shouts and kicks while dreaming. Mild stiffness and a shuffling gait appeared months after the cognitive changes. Which diagnosis BEST fits?
A. Major NCD due to Alzheimer's disease
Alzheimer's disease usually begins with memory and learning decline; fluctuations, early visual hallucinations, and REM sleep behavior are not core features.
B. Major frontotemporal NCD
Frontotemporal disorder presents with early behavioral disinhibition, apathy, or language decline rather than these features.
C. Major vascular NCD
Vascular disorder is tied to cerebrovascular events and often shows stepwise decline in attention and executive function.
D. Major NCD with Lewy bodies ✔ KEY
Fluctuating cognition, recurrent detailed visual hallucinations, and later parkinsonism are core Lewy body features; REM sleep behavior disorder is a suggestive feature.
Rationale: In DSM-5-TR, the core features of neurocognitive disorder with Lewy bodies are fluctuating cognition with marked variations in attention and alertness, recurrent well-formed and detailed visual hallucinations, and spontaneous parkinsonism beginning after cognitive decline. Suggestive features include REM sleep behavior disorder and severe sensitivity to antipsychotic (neuroleptic) medications, which is why accurate identification matters for care coordination.
References: DSM-5-TR — Major or Mild Neurocognitive Disorder With Lewy Bodies, Criteria C–D

179. nce-s-clf-083 · Assessment and Testing · neurocognitive disorders · easy · In review

Which presentation MOST suggests vascular neurocognitive disorder rather than neurocognitive disorder due to Alzheimer's disease?
A. Stepwise drops in attention and planning after small strokes ✔ KEY
Onset tied to cerebrovascular events and prominent decline in complex attention and executive function point to vascular NCD.
B. Slow, steady loss of recent memory that began without warning
Insidious onset with gradual, early memory and learning decline is typical of Alzheimer's disease.
C. Early disinhibition and loss of empathy with intact memory
Early behavioral disinhibition and loss of empathy suggest the behavioral variant of frontotemporal NCD.
D. Recurrent visual hallucinations with alertness that fluctuates
Recurrent visual hallucinations with fluctuating alertness are core features of NCD with Lewy bodies.
Rationale: Vascular neurocognitive disorder is suggested when onset is temporally related to one or more cerebrovascular events, or when decline is prominent in complex attention (including processing speed) and frontal-executive function. Its course is often stepwise. Alzheimer's disease, by contrast, has an insidious onset and gradual progression, with memory and learning typically affected early.
References: DSM-5-TR — Major or Mild Vascular Neurocognitive Disorder, Criterion B

180. nce-s-clf-084 · Human Growth and Development · grief and loss · medium · In review

Fourteen months after her husband died, a 67-year-old client accepts the death and has let herself feel its pain. Now she is struggling to manage the finances he always handled, to drive on the highway alone, and to think of herself as "single." Which of Worden's tasks of mourning is she MOST working on?
A. Accepting the reality of the loss
The stem states she already accepts that the death has happened.
B. Processing the pain of the grief
She has allowed herself to feel the pain; her current struggle is with new roles and identity.
C. Adjusting to a world without him ✔ KEY
Taking on roles the deceased filled and redefining her sense of self are the external and internal adjustments of this task.
D. Finding an enduring connection
This task involves keeping a bond with the deceased while moving forward, not mastering new skills and roles.
Rationale: Worden described four tasks of mourning: accepting the reality of the loss, processing the pain of grief, adjusting to a world without the deceased, and finding an enduring connection with the deceased while embarking on a new life. Adjustment includes external changes (new roles and skills), internal changes (sense of self), and spiritual changes (beliefs about the world). Unlike stage models, tasks are active and can be revisited.
References: Berk (Development Through the Lifespan) — Ch. 19: Bereavement, the experience of grieving

181. nce-s-clf-085 · Human Growth and Development · grief and loss · hard · In review

Six months after his teenage son died, a father has some days filled with looking at photos and crying, and other days focused on returning to work and learning to cook for his family. He worries that this "back and forth" means he is grieving the wrong way. Which framework BEST normalizes his experience?
A. Kübler-Ross's stages of dying, read as a fixed sequence
Kübler-Ross described reactions of dying patients; read as a fixed sequence, it would frame his back-and-forth as a problem.
B. Stroebe and Schut's dual process model of coping with loss ✔ KEY
The dual process model describes healthy oscillation between loss-oriented and restoration-oriented coping.
C. Bowlby's phases of mourning, ending in reorganization
Bowlby's phases describe a general progression and do not center on alternating between two kinds of coping.
D. Caplan's crisis theory, with its time-limited disequilibrium
Crisis theory addresses short-term disequilibrium after an event, not the ongoing rhythm of bereavement coping.
Rationale: Stroebe and Schut's dual process model proposes that bereaved people oscillate between loss-oriented coping (grief work, yearning, reviewing the relationship) and restoration-oriented coping (attending to life changes, new roles, distraction). Moving back and forth, including taking breaks from grief, is considered adaptive. Kübler-Ross's stages (denial, anger, bargaining, depression, acceptance) came from work with dying patients and are not a required sequence for the bereaved.
References: Berk (Development Through the Lifespan) — Ch. 19: Bereavement, the dual-process model of coping with loss
⚑ Check: Berk is cited as "Ch. 19"; confirm the chapter number for the edition in use.

182. nce-s-clf-086 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · crisis and disaster response · easy · In review

According to Caplan's crisis theory, which statement BEST describes a crisis?
A. A time-limited upset when usual coping fails, which may lead to growth ✔ KEY
Caplan described crisis as a temporary disequilibrium when customary problem solving fails, with potential for growth or deterioration.
B. A lasting pattern of poor coping rooted in long-standing personality traits
Crisis is an acute, time-limited state, not an enduring personality pattern.
C. A mental disorder that calls for formal diagnosis and long-term therapy
A crisis is a normal human reaction, not a mental disorder, and calls for brief, focused intervention.
D. A reaction set by how severe the event is, apart from how it is perceived
Crisis theory stresses the person's perception of the event and coping resources, not the event alone.
Rationale: Gerald Caplan described a crisis as a state of psychological disequilibrium that occurs when a person faces an obstacle to important life goals that cannot be overcome with customary problem-solving methods. Crises are time-limited, typically resolving within weeks, and the outcome can be either growth and improved coping or deterioration. This is why early, brief intervention is emphasized.
References: Gladding (Counseling: A Comprehensive Profession) — Crisis counseling: the nature of crises
⚑ Check: Confirm Gladding's crisis counseling coverage of Caplan; no crisis-theory text (e.g., James & Gilliland) is on the approved list.

183. nce-s-clf-087 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · crisis and disaster response · medium · In review

Four months after a flood, the volunteers and news crews have left a small town. Residents who once worked side by side now voice anger at slow insurance payouts and agency red tape, and many feel forgotten. In the commonly used phases of community response to disaster, which phase is the town MOST likely in?
A. Heroic phase
The heroic phase occurs during and right after impact, when people act to save lives and property.
B. Honeymoon phase
The honeymoon phase brings community bonding and optimism as aid arrives, which has faded here.
C. Reconstruction phase
Reconstruction comes later, as residents take responsibility for rebuilding and find new meaning.
D. Disillusionment phase ✔ KEY
As outside support withdraws and bureaucratic delays mount, frustration, anger, and feeling abandoned mark disillusionment.
Rationale: Disaster mental health models describe community phases: pre-disaster, impact, heroic, honeymoon, disillusionment, and reconstruction. The disillusionment phase can last months or longer, as optimism gives way to fatigue and anger when aid slows and the reality of losses sets in. Counselors can anticipate rising stress during this phase and increase outreach.
References: Gladding (Counseling: A Comprehensive Profession) — Crisis and disaster counseling: phases of disaster response

184. nce-s-clf-088 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · intimate partner violence · easy · In review

A week after her partner assaulted her, a client tells the counselor that he cried, brought flowers, promised it would never happen again, and has been "the man I fell in love with." She feels hopeful. In Walker's cycle of violence, which phase is this?
A. Tension-building phase
Tension building involves rising strain, minor incidents, and the victim trying to keep the peace.
B. Acute battering phase
The acute battering phase is the explosion of violence itself, which has already passed.
C. Honeymoon phase ✔ KEY
Apologies, gifts, promises, and loving behavior after an assault mark the honeymoon (loving contrition) phase.
D. Resolution phase
Walker's cycle has no resolution phase; without intervention, the cycle tends to repeat.
Rationale: Lenore Walker's cycle of violence has three phases: tension building, an acute battering incident, and loving contrition (the honeymoon phase). The honeymoon phase reinforces hope that the abuse will end and helps explain why many survivors stay. Over time the honeymoon phase often shortens or disappears, so counselors should use this period to support safety planning.

185. nce-s-clf-089 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · intimate partner violence · medium · In review

A client says her husband controls all their money, reads her messages, has cut her off from friends, and threatens to take the children if she leaves. She adds, "It isn't really abuse, because he has never hit me." Which framework would BEST help her see these behaviors as connected forms of abuse?
A. Walker's cycle of violence
The cycle of violence describes phases around violent episodes, and she reports no physical violence.
B. Duluth Power and Control Wheel ✔ KEY
The wheel shows tactics such as economic abuse, isolation, and using children as parts of one pattern of coercive control.
C. Gottman's Four Horsemen
The Four Horsemen describe destructive communication in conflict, not coercive control or abuse.
D. Bowen's triangulation concept
Triangulation describes drawing a third party into a dyad's anxiety, not a pattern of abusive control.
Rationale: The Power and Control Wheel, developed by the Domestic Abuse Intervention Project in Duluth, Minnesota, shows abuse as a pattern of coercive control. Its spokes are tactics such as intimidation, isolation, economic abuse, using children, and threats, held together by a rim of physical and sexual violence. Coercive control can be dangerous even without physical assault, and conjoint couples counseling is generally contraindicated while it is ongoing.

186. nce-s-clf-090 · Career Development · career and vocational concerns · hard · In review

A 46-year-old warehouse supervisor lost his job four months ago. A generous severance package covers his bills for the next year, yet he reports feeling aimless, sleeping late, missing his coworkers, and no longer knowing "who I am." Which framework BEST explains why his distress persists despite financial security?
A. Holland's congruence between personality type and setting
Congruence concerns fit between a person's type and a work environment; it does not explain distress from losing work altogether.
B. Krumboltz's planned happenstance and openness to chance
Planned happenstance is a stance for using unplanned events, not an account of why job loss is distressing.
C. Jahoda's latent deprivation model of unemployment ✔ KEY
Jahoda argued that work supplies latent benefits such as time structure, social contact, purpose, status and activity; losing them causes distress even when income continues.
D. Gottfredson's compromise among sex type, prestige and field
Compromise describes how people give up preferred options when choosing; it does not address the psychological functions of employment.
Rationale: Jahoda's latent deprivation model holds that employment has a manifest function (income) and latent functions: time structure, regular social contact, shared purpose, status and identity, and regular activity. Unemployed people lose these latent benefits even when money is not a problem, which helps explain aimlessness and identity loss. Counselors can help clients rebuild structure, contact and purpose while job searching.
References: Sharf (Career Development Theory) — Career counseling for unemployment and job loss; Jahoda's latent deprivation model
⚑ Check: Verify that the cited Sharf edition discusses Jahoda's latent deprivation model; the model itself (Jahoda, 1981-1982) is well established.

187. nce-s-clf-091 · Career Development · career and vocational concerns · medium · In review

A 34-year-old client has completed several interest inventories, researched dozens of occupations, and shadowed workers in two fields, yet still cannot choose. She describes the same agonizing paralysis when picking an apartment, a car, or even a restaurant, and says any choice feels like it could ruin her life. Which approach BEST fits her concern?
A. Address the anxiety behind her pervasive indecisiveness ✔ KEY
Indecision that spans life domains and persists despite ample information reflects chronic indecisiveness, which calls for work on anxiety and personal concerns, not more data.
B. Give more occupational information and a new inventory
More information helps a developmentally undecided client who lacks data; this client already has extensive information and remains stuck.
C. Treat her concern as normal, developmental undecidedness
Developmental undecidedness is situational and resolves with exploration; her paralysis is pervasive and long-standing.
D. Have her pick the highest-rated field and commit to it now
Pushing a fast commitment ignores the anxiety driving her indecisiveness and is likely to increase her distress.
Rationale: Career literature distinguishes career indecision (undecidedness), a common developmental state that usually responds to information and exploration, from indecisiveness, a chronic trait-like difficulty making decisions across many areas of life, often tied to anxiety, perfectionism or fear of commitment. Indecisive clients benefit from attention to these personal factors alongside career work.
References: Sharf (Career Development Theory) — Career indecision versus chronic indecisiveness in career counseling

188. nce-s-clf-092 · Career Development · career and vocational concerns · medium · In review

An emergency room nurse works predictable shifts and is home every evening. She says the fatigue, tension and irritability she carries home from chaotic shifts leave her snapping at her children and too drained to enjoy family time. Which form of work-family conflict does this BEST illustrate?
A. Time-based conflict over hours that work takes from home
Time-based conflict occurs when hours spent in one role make it hard to meet another role's demands; her schedule leaves her home every evening.
B. Behavior-based conflict from work habits that misfit home
Behavior-based conflict involves role behaviors, such as a brisk clinical style, that are inappropriate at home; her problem is spillover of strain.
C. Family-to-work conflict in which home duties disrupt her job
This reverses the direction; here work is interfering with family life, not the other way around.
D. Strain-based conflict as work stress spills into home life ✔ KEY
Strain-based conflict occurs when stress, fatigue or irritability from one role impairs functioning in another, as her work strain does at home.
Rationale: Greenhaus and Beutell described three forms of work-family conflict: time-based, strain-based and behavior-based, each of which can run work-to-family or family-to-work. Identifying the form guides intervention; strain-based conflict points toward recovery routines, stress management and boundary rituals between roles rather than schedule changes.
References: Sharf (Career Development Theory) — Work-family conflict and life roles: time-based, strain-based and behavior-based conflict
⚑ Check: Confirm the cited Sharf edition covers the Greenhaus and Beutell (1985) three-form typology of work-family conflict.
☐ Approve☐ Revise☐ RetireNotes:

189. nce-s-clf-093 · Career Development · career and vocational concerns · medium · In review

A 27-year-old client with well-controlled epilepsy has a job interview next week. She asks her counselor whether she is required to tell the interviewer about her seizure disorder. Under the Americans with Disabilities Act, which statement is MOST accurate?
A. She must disclose it at the interview so the employer can judge risk
The ADA does not require applicants to disclose a disability at an interview; employers may ask only about the ability to do job functions.
B. Disclosure is her choice; it is needed if she seeks accommodation ✔ KEY
Disclosure under the ADA is generally voluntary; an employer needs to know about the disability only when an accommodation is requested.
C. Disclosure is only required if the job involves operating machinery
Job tasks do not create a duty to disclose at the interview; safety concerns are handled through job-related standards after an offer.
D. The employer may ask about any medical condition before an offer
Before a conditional job offer, the ADA bars disability-related questions and medical exams.
Rationale: The ADA prohibits disability-related inquiries and medical examinations before a conditional job offer and does not require applicants to disclose. Disclosure becomes necessary mainly when a person requests a reasonable accommodation. Career counselors help clients with disabilities weigh whether, when and how to disclose, and how to describe needed accommodations in terms of job performance.
References: Sharf (Career Development Theory) — Career development of people with disabilities; ADA disclosure and reasonable accommodation
⚑ Check: Confirm the ADA pre-offer inquiry rules as stated and that the cited source covers disability disclosure; Sharf may not discuss ADA specifics.

190. nce-s-clf-094 · Career Development · career and vocational concerns · medium · In review

A 19-year-old first-generation college student from a Filipino American family says her parents expect her to become a nurse, though she is drawn to graphic design. She values her family's sacrifices and wants their blessing. Her counselor is trained in a decision model that stresses individual choice. What is the MOST culturally responsive approach?
A. Encourage her to choose design and assert independence from family
This imposes an individualistic value and ignores that family harmony and approval matter to her.
B. Advise her to follow her parents' wishes to keep peace in the family
This dismisses her own interests and is just as directive as pushing independence.
C. Explore her values and include family input in the decision process ✔ KEY
Honoring collectivist values while exploring her interests, and inviting family into the process when she wishes, respects her worldview.
D. Use the standard model as written, since good models fit all clients
Career models built on Western individualism may not fit clients from collectivist backgrounds and need cultural adaptation.
Rationale: Many career theories assume individualistic decision making. With clients from collectivist backgrounds, career choice may be a family decision tied to obligation and gratitude. Culturally responsive counselors explore the client's own values, acknowledge family expectations, and may involve family in the process rather than framing autonomy as the goal.
References: Sue & Sue (Counseling the Culturally Diverse) — Collectivism, family roles and culturally responsive career counseling

191. nce-s-clf-095 · Human Growth and Development · couples and family concerns · medium · In review

Parents of a 15-year-old argue constantly with their son about curfew, friends and privacy, while also arranging care for the father's ailing mother. In Carter and McGoldrick's family life cycle, which key emotional task does the family face in this stage?
A. Increasing the flexibility of family boundaries for independence ✔ KEY
The families-with-adolescents stage calls for more flexible boundaries to allow teen independence and to absorb grandparents' frailties.
B. Accepting new members into the system as the couple has children
Accepting new members is the task of families with young children.
C. Accepting many exits from and entries into the family system
Accepting exits and entries is the task of launching children and moving on.
D. Accepting the shifting of roles from one generation to the next
Accepting shifting generational roles is the task of families in later life.
Rationale: Carter and McGoldrick describe stages of the family life cycle, each with a key emotional process. In families with adolescents, boundaries must become more flexible so teens can move in and out of the system, while parents often begin caring for aging grandparents. Stress tends to rise when a family remains rigid at a transition point.
References: Gladding (Counseling: A Comprehensive Profession) — Family life cycle stages and key emotional tasks (Carter & McGoldrick)

192. nce-s-clf-096 · Human Growth and Development · couples and family concerns · medium · In review

A divorcing couple with children aged 6 and 10 asks a counselor what will MOST affect how well their children adjust over the coming years. Based on research on divorce and child outcomes, which factor should the counselor highlight?
A. Whether the children are told about the divorce at the same time
How the news is shared matters, but a single conversation is not a major long-term predictor of adjustment.
B. Whether a sole or a joint legal custody arrangement is chosen
Custody type alone predicts outcomes less than the quality of parenting and the level of conflict children face.
C. Whether either parent remarries within the first year after
Remarriage brings its own adjustments, but it is not the strongest predictor of children's post-divorce adjustment.
D. How much ongoing conflict between parents the children see ✔ KEY
Exposure to persistent, hostile interparental conflict is among the strongest predictors of poorer child adjustment after divorce.
Rationale: Research by Hetherington, Amato and others shows that most children of divorce adjust well within a few years. Poorer outcomes are most strongly linked to ongoing interparental conflict, especially when children are caught in the middle, along with declines in parenting quality and economic hardship. Counselors help parents shield children from conflict and keep warm, consistent parenting.
References: Berk (Development Through the Lifespan) — Divorce: factors in children's adjustment, interparental conflict and parenting quality

193. nce-s-clf-097 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · couples and family concerns · easy · In review

During a heated discussion in couples counseling, a husband pauses, smiles, and says, "Okay, I'm getting defensive. Can I try saying that again?" His wife's tone softens. In Gottman's research, what is the husband's statement called?
A. Stonewalling
Stonewalling is withdrawing from the interaction, one of the Four Horsemen; he is re-engaging, not shutting down.
B. A repair attempt ✔ KEY
A repair attempt is any statement or action that de-escalates conflict and keeps negativity from spiraling.
C. A harsh startup
A harsh startup is opening a discussion with criticism or contempt; his comment de-escalates.
D. Flooding
Flooding is physiological overwhelm during conflict; his calm self-correction shows the opposite.
Rationale: Gottman found that stable couples argue too, but they make and accept repair attempts, such as humor, apologies or requests to start over, that stop negativity from escalating. Whether repairs succeed depends largely on the strength of the couple's friendship. Counselors teach partners to offer and recognize repair attempts.
References: Gladding (Counseling: A Comprehensive Profession) — Couples counseling: Gottman's research on repair attempts and the Four Horsemen
⚑ Check: Gottman's own texts are not on the approved source list; confirm the cited Gladding edition covers repair attempts.

194. nce-s-clf-098 · Social and Cultural Diversity · spiritual and religious concerns · medium · In review

A 20-year-old college student raised in a close-knit congregation tells her counselor she has begun questioning teachings she once accepted without thought. She is reading widely, testing beliefs against her own reasoning, and wants a faith she has chosen for herself. Which of Fowler's stages of faith does this BEST describe?
A. Synthetic-conventional
Synthetic-conventional faith conforms to the beliefs of one's group without critical examination, which she is now moving beyond.
B. Mythic-literal
Mythic-literal faith, typical of school-age children, takes stories and symbols literally.
C. Individuative-reflective ✔ KEY
This stage involves critically examining inherited beliefs and taking personal responsibility for one's own commitments.
D. Conjunctive
Conjunctive faith, usually in midlife or later, embraces paradox and other traditions after the reflective stage.
Rationale: Fowler proposed stages of faith development: primal (undifferentiated), intuitive-projective, mythic-literal, synthetic-conventional, individuative-reflective, conjunctive and universalizing. The shift from synthetic-conventional to individuative-reflective often occurs in late adolescence or young adulthood, when people move from group-held belief to a self-examined faith. Counselors can normalize this questioning rather than treat it as a crisis.
References: Gladding (Counseling: A Comprehensive Profession) — Spirituality in counseling: Fowler's stages of faith development

195. nce-s-clf-099 · Social and Cultural Diversity · spiritual and religious concerns · hard · In review

After a long illness in his family, a 63-year-old deacon says he has lost his sense that God listens and feels unmoored from his faith. He is sad at times but sleeps, eats and works normally, and his distress is in proportion to his experience. He meets criteria for no mental disorder. How should the counselor document the focus of treatment?
A. Adjustment disorder with depressed mood after the stressor
Adjustment disorder requires distress out of proportion to the stressor or significant impairment; neither is present.
B. Persistent depressive disorder, given his low periods
He has occasional sadness with normal functioning, not depressed mood most days for two years.
C. No code, since spiritual concerns fall outside counseling
Spiritual concerns can be a legitimate focus of counseling and have a code for documentation.
D. Religious or spiritual problem as a focus of attention ✔ KEY
DSM-5-TR lists religious or spiritual problem (Z65.8) for distress such as loss or questioning of faith that is a focus of care.
Rationale: DSM-5-TR includes Religious or Spiritual Problem (Z65.8) among Other Conditions That May Be a Focus of Clinical Attention. It covers distressing experiences such as loss or questioning of faith, conversion, or conflicts with a religious institution. It lets counselors document a legitimate focus of care without pathologizing a spiritual struggle that does not meet criteria for a mental disorder.
References: DSM-5-TR — Other Conditions That May Be a Focus of Clinical Attention: Religious or Spiritual Problem (Z65.8)

196. nce-s-clf-100 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · child abuse and neglect · medium · In review

A mother tells her counselor that her 11-year-old's new volunteer tutor is "amazing." He buys her daughter small gifts, offers rides so the mother can rest, texts the girl privately at night, and recently told her some of their talks are "just between us." Which feature MOST strongly suggests possible grooming?
A. Private contact paired with a request to keep secrets ✔ KEY
Grooming typically moves toward private access and secrecy, which isolate the child and lower the chance of disclosure.
B. His popularity with the parents in the neighborhood
Groomers may win over parents, but being well liked is common among safe adults and is not a specific warning sign.
C. His strong interest in the girl's grades and reading
Academic interest is expected of a tutor and is not by itself a warning sign.
D. His volunteering without pay for a nonprofit program
Unpaid volunteering is ordinary and does not distinguish grooming from safe mentoring.
Rationale: Grooming is a gradual process in which an offender builds trust with a child and often the family, gives special attention or gifts, tests boundaries, increases private access and introduces secrecy. Secret-keeping and isolated contact are the most telling signs. Counselors educate parents about these patterns and follow mandated reporting law when abuse is reasonably suspected.
References: Gladding (Counseling: A Comprehensive Profession) — Child sexual abuse: grooming patterns and warning signs
⚑ Check: Confirm the cited source covers grooming; the concept itself is standard in child-protection literature.

197. nce-s-clf-101 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · child abuse and neglect · easy · In review

A school counselor notices bruises on an active 6-year-old who plays rough sports at recess. Which bruise location should raise the GREATEST concern about possible physical abuse?
A. Scattered bruises over both shins and knees
Shins and knees are bony areas that are commonly bruised in normal childhood play.
B. A bruise on the forehead after a playground fall
The forehead is a common site of accidental injury in falls, especially with a matching account.
C. Bruises on the ears, cheeks and the upper back ✔ KEY
Ears, cheeks and the back are protected, soft areas rarely bruised by accident, so injuries there suggest inflicted harm.
D. Bruises on the elbows of both arms from falling
Elbows are bony prominences that often take the impact of accidental falls.
Rationale: Accidental bruises in children usually appear over bony prominences such as the shins, knees, elbows and forehead. Bruises on soft or protected areas such as the ears, cheeks, neck, torso, back, buttocks or upper arms, or bruises in the shape of an object or hand, are more concerning for abuse. Reasonable suspicion triggers a mandated report.
References: Gladding (Counseling: A Comprehensive Profession) — Recognizing physical indicators of child abuse · State law — Child abuse mandated reporting statutes: reasonable-suspicion standard
⚑ Check: Confirm the cited Gladding edition lists physical indicators of abuse; the injury-location pattern is standard pediatric guidance.

198. nce-s-clf-102 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · school and academic concerns · easy · In review

A middle school counselor is preparing a parent workshop on cyberbullying. Which feature MOST clearly sets cyberbullying apart from traditional in-person bullying?
A. It involves a real or perceived imbalance of power
A power imbalance is part of the definition of all bullying, not a distinguishing feature of cyberbullying.
B. It can reach the child at home, at any hour, by many ✔ KEY
Online bullying follows children home around the clock and can be seen and shared by large audiences, often anonymously.
C. It is intentional and is repeated over a period of time
Intent and repetition define bullying in general, both online and in person.
D. It harms the target's mood and school performance
Both forms of bullying are linked to depression, anxiety and academic problems.
Rationale: Bullying is unwanted aggressive behavior that is intentional, repeated or likely to be repeated, and involves a power imbalance. Cyberbullying shares these features but differs in that it can occur at any time and place, reach a wide audience, persist as a permanent record, and allow anonymity, so home is no longer a refuge.
References: Gladding (Counseling: A Comprehensive Profession) — School counseling: bullying and cyberbullying

199. nce-s-clf-103 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · school and academic concerns · medium · In review

For several weeks, two larger eighth graders have mocked a smaller classmate, taken his lunch, and posted edited photos of him. The principal asks the school counselor to hold a peer mediation session so the three boys can "work it out together." What is the counselor's BEST response?
A. Agree, and set clear ground rules so each boy can share equally
Ground rules do not remove the power imbalance that makes mediation unsafe and unfair for a bullied student.
B. Agree, but ask the target to suggest ways he could avoid the boys
This places responsibility on the target and implies he shares blame for being bullied.
C. Decline, and leave the matter to discipline by the administration
Discipline may be needed, but the counselor still has a role in supporting the target and working with the students who bully.
D. Explain why mediation fits poorly and meet with the boys separately ✔ KEY
Because bullying involves a power imbalance, separate meetings that support the target and hold the others accountable are preferred.
Rationale: Peer mediation assumes two parties with roughly equal power and shared responsibility for a conflict. Bullying is repeated, intentional aggression with a power imbalance, so mediation can retraumatize the target and send the message that both are to blame. Recommended practice is to support the target, address the behavior of those who bully separately, and involve families and school-wide prevention.
References: Gladding (Counseling: A Comprehensive Profession) — School counseling: responding to bullying; limits of peer mediation
⚑ Check: Confirm the cited source states that peer mediation is not recommended for bullying (a position also taken by federal StopBullying guidance).

200. nce-s-clf-104 · Human Growth and Development · aging and older adults · easy · In review

A counselor reviewing a new referral for a 77-year-old with low mood, poor sleep and withdrawal thinks, "Anyone her age would feel down. Therapy probably won't change much at this point." What does the counselor's reaction BEST reflect?
A. Ageist bias that risks undertreating treatable depression ✔ KEY
Treating depression as a normal part of aging, and assuming older adults cannot change, is ageism and leads to undertreatment.
B. Sound clinical judgment about late-life treatment outcomes
Older adults respond well to psychotherapy for depression; the assumption is not supported.
C. Appropriate humility about his limits with older clients
Humility would lead to consultation or training, not a belief that therapy is futile.
D. A correct view of depression as part of normal aging
Depression is common in later life but is not a normal part of aging.
Rationale: Ageism includes stereotypes that older adults are rigid, unable to change, or naturally depressed. In clinicians it can show up as therapeutic nihilism and missed diagnoses. Depression is not a normal part of aging, and evidence-based therapies such as CBT and problem-solving therapy work well for older adults.
References: Berk (Development Through the Lifespan) — Late adulthood: ageism, stereotypes of aging and late-life depression

201. nce-s-clf-105 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · aging and older adults · medium · In review

An 84-year-old client mentions that her nephew, who now manages her finances, has her sign blank checks, and that her utilities were shut off last month. She seems anxious when he is mentioned and asks the counselor not to "make trouble." What should the counselor do?
A. Wait to act until she gives proof that funds were taken
Reporting standards rest on reasonable suspicion; counselors do not need proof before acting.
B. Call the nephew to ask him to explain how he uses the money
Confronting a suspected abuser can raise the risk to the client and breaches confidentiality.
C. Follow state law and report suspected exploitation to APS ✔ KEY
Financial exploitation of an older adult is reportable abuse in many states; counselors follow their state's law and report to Adult Protective Services.
D. Respect her wish and only document what she has disclosed
Her wishes matter, but many states mandate reports of suspected elder abuse regardless of consent.
Rationale: Elder abuse includes physical, emotional, sexual and financial abuse and neglect; perpetrators are often family members or trusted caregivers. Signs of financial exploitation include unpaid bills despite adequate income and unusual control of money. Reporting laws vary, so counselors know their state's requirements, report suspected abuse to Adult Protective Services when required, and involve the client as much as possible.
References: State law — Elder and vulnerable adult abuse reporting statutes (requirements vary by state) · ACA Code of Ethics — B.2.a Serious and Foreseeable Harm and Legal Requirements

202. nce-s-clf-106 · Social and Cultural Diversity · LGBTQ+ clients and minority stress · hard · In review

A 29-year-old nonbinary client, assigned male at birth, has lived in line with their gender identity for years and feels settled and content with it. They seek counseling for work stress after a promotion. Which statement about a gender dysphoria diagnosis is MOST accurate?
A. It applies, since gender identity differs from assigned sex
Incongruence alone is not a disorder; DSM-5-TR also requires clinically significant distress or impairment.
B. It does not apply without related distress or impairment ✔ KEY
Gender dysphoria requires clinically significant distress or impairment linked to the incongruence, which this client does not report.
C. It applies, since the incongruence has lasted over 6 months
Duration is only part of the criteria; distress or impairment is also required.
D. It does not apply, since nonbinary identities are excluded
DSM-5-TR criteria include a desire to be or be treated as an alternative gender, which covers nonbinary identities.
Rationale: In DSM-5-TR, gender dysphoria requires a marked incongruence between experienced or expressed gender and assigned gender lasting at least 6 months, plus clinically significant distress or impairment. The diagnosis centers on distress, not identity; being transgender or nonbinary is not itself a mental disorder. Counselors avoid diagnosing identity and focus on the client's presenting concerns.

203. nce-s-clf-107 · Social and Cultural Diversity · LGBTQ+ clients and minority stress · easy · In review

In the third session, a 17-year-old client tells the counselor that he is transgender, goes by Eli, and uses he/him pronouns. He has not told his parents. Which counselor response is MOST affirming?
A. Ask him to wait a few months before changing his name
Delaying use of his name invalidates his identity and can weaken trust in the relationship.
B. Use his legal name in session until his parents know
Withholding his name in session is not affirming; safety concerns apply to records and outside contacts, not to how the counselor addresses him.
C. Explore whether this is a phase before using his name
Treating his identity as a phase to be tested is not affirming practice.
D. Use Eli and he/him, and ask where it is safe to do so ✔ KEY
Affirming care uses the client's name and pronouns and plans together where they are used, such as in records or with family, to avoid outing him.
Rationale: Affirming counseling with transgender clients means using their chosen name and pronouns, treating gender identity as valid, and collaborating on safety, including when names appear in records or communications that parents or others may see. This respects the client's autonomy and protects against unwanted disclosure.

204. nce-s-clf-108 · Social and Cultural Diversity · acculturation and immigration stress · hard · In review

A 45-year-old Somali refugee who resettled two years ago works steadily and cares for his children. He grieves the loss of his land, his community's rituals, and his parents' graves, feels guilty for leaving, and sometimes dreams that his ancestors are calling him. Which concept BEST describes his experience?
A. Cultural bereavement after losing homeland and community ✔ KEY
Cultural bereavement is grief over the loss of one's homeland, social structures and cultural identity, a normal response to uprooting.
B. Culture shock from early contact with an unfamiliar setting
Culture shock is the early disorientation of contact with a new culture; his grief centers on what was lost.
C. Prolonged grief disorder after the death of a close person
Prolonged grief disorder requires the death of someone close and impairing grief; his loss is of culture and place, and he functions well.
D. Posttraumatic stress disorder with prominent survivor guilt
He reports no intrusion, avoidance or arousal symptoms; guilt and longing alone do not indicate PTSD.
Rationale: Eisenbruch described cultural bereavement as the grief of uprooted people for lost land, culture and social bonds, which can include guilt over leaving, longing, and dreams or visits from ancestors. He cautioned that such experiences may be misread as pathology. Counselors validate the loss and draw on cultural and community resources for mourning.
References: Sue & Sue (Counseling the Culturally Diverse) — Refugees and immigrants: cultural bereavement and migration-related loss
⚑ Check: Verify the Eisenbruch (1991) attribution and that the cited Sue & Sue edition discusses cultural bereavement.

205. nce-s-clf-109 · Social and Cultural Diversity · acculturation and immigration stress · easy · In review

A 12-year-old regularly interprets for her Spanish-speaking parents at medical visits and the bank. At the start of a family counseling session about the father's job loss, the parents ask her to interpret for them. What should the counselor do?
A. Let her interpret, since the family trusts her the most
Trust does not offset the role reversal and exposure to adult concerns that interpreting places on a child.
B. Hold the session in English and simplify the language
Simplifying English does not give the parents full access to the session.
C. Arrange a qualified interpreter so she can be a child ✔ KEY
A qualified interpreter gives the parents full access and frees the child from a parental role in adult matters.
D. Ask her to sum up each part instead of translating it
Summaries still make the child the gatekeeper of adult content and risk distortion.
Rationale: Language brokering, in which children of immigrants interpret for parents, can build skills but also brings role reversal, stress and exposure to adult worries. In counseling, using a child as interpreter compromises accuracy and confidentiality and burdens the child. The ACA Code directs counselors to arrange qualified interpreters when clients have difficulty understanding the language used.
References: ACA Code of Ethics — A.2.c Developmental and Cultural Sensitivity (qualified interpreters) · Sue & Sue (Counseling the Culturally Diverse) — Language barriers, language brokering and the use of interpreters
