206. nce-s-clf-110 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · self-injury · hard · In review

A 19-year-old with a three-year history of superficial cutting to "calm down" tells her counselor that last weekend she swallowed a bottle of pills because she "wanted to never wake up." She slept for 12 hours and woke without treatment. Which statement is MOST accurate?
A. The overdose is part of her NSSI pattern, given her history
NSSI is defined by the absence of suicidal intent; she clearly stated she wanted to die.
B. The overdose was a suicide attempt, and NSSI raises her risk ✔ KEY
Intent to die makes the overdose a suicide attempt, and a history of NSSI is a known risk factor for suicidal behavior.
C. Her low-lethality outcome shows the act was mainly a gesture
Outcome does not define intent; calling it a gesture minimizes a real suicide attempt.
D. Her NSSI lowers suicide risk because it works as coping for her
NSSI may regulate emotion short term, but it is associated with higher, not lower, suicide risk.
Rationale: NSSI and suicidal behavior are distinguished mainly by intent: NSSI is self-inflicted harm without intent to die, usually to relieve distress, whereas a suicide attempt involves at least some intent to die. The two often co-occur, and NSSI history is a robust predictor of later suicide attempts. Each act should be assessed for intent, and safety planning updated.
References: DSM-5-TR — Conditions for Further Study: Nonsuicidal Self-Injury Disorder and Suicidal Behavior Disorder · C-SSRS — Distinguishing suicidal from non-suicidal self-injurious behavior by intent

207. nce-s-clf-111 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · self-injury · easy · In review

The DSM-5-TR proposed criteria for nonsuicidal self-injury disorder, a condition for further study, require intentional self-inflicted injury without suicidal intent on how many days in the past year?
A. At least 2 days
Two days is below the proposed threshold.
B. At least 12 days
Twelve days exceeds the proposed threshold.
C. At least 30 days
Thirty days is far above the proposed threshold.
D. At least 5 days ✔ KEY
The proposed criteria require self-injury on 5 or more days in the past year.
Rationale: In DSM-5-TR Section III, the proposed nonsuicidal self-injury disorder requires intentional self-inflicted damage to the body on 5 or more days in the past year, with the expectation of only minor or moderate harm. 208. nce-s-clf-112 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · sleep-wake concerns · medium · In review

A 16-year-old cannot fall asleep before 2 a.m. on school nights and is exhausted in morning classes. On weekends he sleeps soundly from 2 a.m. to 11 a.m. and wakes refreshed. Months of sleep hygiene tips such as no caffeine and a cool, dark room have not helped. Which explanation BEST fits?
A. A circadian rhythm disorder of the delayed sleep phase type ✔ KEY
Sleep that is normal in quality but shifted later, causing conflict with required schedules, fits delayed sleep phase type.
B. An insomnia disorder needing more strict sleep hygiene rules
He sleeps well when allowed his own schedule, so the problem is timing, and sleep hygiene alone has not helped.
C. A hypersomnolence disorder marked by excessive sleep need
His total sleep is normal and he wakes refreshed on weekends, which argues against hypersomnolence.
D. Narcolepsy with sleep attacks in his early morning classes
Narcolepsy involves recurrent irresistible sleep episodes regardless of schedule, not a simple shift in timing.
Rationale: Circadian rhythm sleep-wake disorders arise from a mismatch between the internal clock and required schedules. Delayed sleep phase type, common in adolescents, causes trouble falling asleep and waking at conventional times, while sleep at the preferred time is normal. Sleep hygiene alone rarely fixes a timing problem; approaches such as a consistent wake time and morning light, coordinated with a medical provider, target the clock itself.
References: DSM-5-TR — Circadian Rhythm Sleep-Wake Disorders: delayed sleep phase type
☐ Approve☐ Revise☐ RetireNotes:

209. nce-s-clf-113 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · psychopharmacology basics for counselors · medium · In review

A 58-year-old client with schizophrenia has taken a first-generation antipsychotic for many years. The counselor notices repetitive lip smacking, tongue thrusting and facial grimacing that the client does not seem aware of. Which side effect do these signs MOST likely reflect?
A. Akathisia, an inner restlessness and urge to keep moving
Akathisia is subjective restlessness with pacing or fidgeting, not involuntary oral and facial movements.
B. Acute dystonia, with sudden and sustained muscle spasms
Acute dystonia appears early in treatment as painful sustained contractions, not repetitive movements after years.
C. Tardive dyskinesia from long-term antipsychotic use ✔ KEY
Involuntary repetitive movements of the mouth, tongue and face after long-term antipsychotic use are classic tardive dyskinesia.
D. Neuroleptic malignant syndrome, a fever-and-rigidity crisis
NMS is an acute emergency with high fever, severe rigidity and confusion, not chronic oral-facial movements.
Rationale: Tardive dyskinesia is a late-emerging, sometimes irreversible movement disorder linked to long-term dopamine-blocking medications, especially first-generation antipsychotics. Counselors who notice possible signs should promptly inform the prescriber. Second-generation antipsychotics carry lower TD risk but greater metabolic risks, such as weight gain, diabetes and lipid changes, which also warrant medical monitoring.
References: DSM-5-TR — Medication-Induced Movement Disorders: Tardive Dyskinesia
☐ Approve☐ Revise☐ RetireNotes:

210. nce-s-clf-114 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · psychopharmacology basics for counselors · easy · In review

A client who takes phenelzine, a monoamine oxidase inhibitor, mentions he is planning a celebration dinner of aged cheeses, cured salami and draft beer. The counselor encourages him to check with his prescriber first. Which reaction is the main concern?
A. Agranulocytosis
Agranulocytosis is a blood disorder linked mainly to clozapine, not to diet with MAOIs.
B. Hypertensive crisis ✔ KEY
Tyramine in aged, cured and fermented foods can trigger a dangerous blood pressure spike in people taking MAOIs.
C. Lithium toxicity
Lithium toxicity relates to lithium levels, not to tyramine or MAOIs.
D. Tardive dyskinesia
Tardive dyskinesia is a movement disorder from long-term antipsychotics, not a dietary reaction.
Rationale: MAOIs block the breakdown of tyramine, found in aged cheeses, cured meats, some tap or draft beers and other fermented foods. Tyramine buildup can cause a hypertensive crisis with severe headache, stiff neck and rapid blood pressure rise. MAOIs also interact dangerously with many drugs, including other antidepressants, raising the risk of serotonin syndrome. Counselors reinforce prescriber guidance rather than giving medical advice.
References: Gladding (Counseling: A Comprehensive Profession) — Psychopharmacology for counselors: MAOIs and tyramine dietary restrictions
⚑ Check: Confirm the cited source covers MAOI dietary restrictions; the pharmacology is standard.
☐ Approve☐ Revise☐ RetireNotes:

211. nce-s-clf-115 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · psychopharmacology basics for counselors · medium · In review

A 20-year-old college student without an ADHD diagnosis tells her counselor she takes a friend's prescription stimulant before exams to "focus better." She says it is safe "because doctors prescribe it to kids." What is the counselor's BEST response?
A. Tell her she must stop now or the counselor will alert the school
Threats damage the alliance, and confidential disclosure of past use does not by itself permit a report.
B. Agree it is low risk if used only during the exam period
Nonmedical stimulant use carries real risks even when occasional, so this response gives false reassurance.
C. Advise her to seek her own prescription so she can use it safely
Steering her to obtain a stimulant without a clinical need endorses misuse and is outside the counselor's role.
D. Explore her use nonjudgmentally and discuss its health risks ✔ KEY
A nonjudgmental, MI-consistent discussion of risks such as dependence and cardiac strain respects autonomy and invites change.
Rationale: Nonmedical use of prescription stimulants is common among college students and carries risks such as elevated heart rate and blood pressure, anxiety, insomnia and stimulant use disorder. Counselors respond with motivational interviewing: exploring the student's reasons, offering accurate information with permission, and addressing underlying academic stress, rather than lecturing or threatening.
References: Miller & Rollnick (MI) — Exchanging information with permission (elicit-provide-elicit) and avoiding the righting reflex · DSM-5-TR — Stimulant Use Disorder

212. nce-s-clf-116 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · military and veteran populations · medium · In review

Two months after her husband returned from a 9-month deployment, a military spouse says he keeps overriding household routines she ran on her own, the children test his rules, and they feel like "strangers sharing a house." In the emotional cycle of deployment, which phase is the family in?
A. Post-deployment, as the family renegotiates its roles ✔ KEY
Post-deployment (reintegration) involves renegotiating roles, routines and intimacy after the service member returns.
B. Sustainment, as the family settles into new routines
Sustainment occurs during deployment, when the family at home builds new routines and support.
C. Redeployment, as the family anticipates his return
Redeployment is the period just before return, marked by excitement and apprehension.
D. Pre-deployment, as the family prepares for the leave
Pre-deployment covers preparation and anticipatory loss before departure.
Rationale: The emotional cycle of deployment is often described in phases: pre-deployment, deployment, sustainment, redeployment and post-deployment. Post-deployment reintegration can take months as families renegotiate roles the at-home partner took on, rebuild intimacy and adjust parenting. Counselors normalize this friction and help families communicate about changed roles.
References: Gladding (Counseling: A Comprehensive Profession) — Counseling military families: emotional cycle of deployment and reintegration

A 38-year-old Air Force veteran discloses that she was sexually assaulted by a fellow service member during her enlistment. She never reported it, has no VA disability rating, and assumes VA will not help her without proof. Which statement BEST guides the counselor's response?
A. She must first file a report so the assault can be verified
VA does not require that the assault was reported or documented to provide MST-related care.
B. She can get care only after a service-connection rating
MST-related care does not require a service-connected disability rating.
C. VA offers free MST care without a report or a rating ✔ KEY
VA provides free care for mental and physical conditions related to military sexual trauma without requiring a prior report or a rating.
D. MST must first be diagnosed as a disorder to get services
MST is an experience, not a diagnosis; care addresses conditions related to it.
Rationale: Military sexual trauma (MST) is the VA term for sexual assault or threatening sexual harassment during military service. It is an experience, not a diagnosis. 214. nce-s-clf-118 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · chronic illness and disability · medium · In review

The mother of a 9-year-old with cerebral palsy says she accepted her son's diagnosis long ago and loves their life, yet waves of sadness return each time his classmates reach milestones he cannot, such as riding a bike. She asks whether something is wrong with her. Which concept BEST frames her experience?
A. Prolonged grief disorder from an unresolved earlier loss
Prolonged grief disorder follows a death and involves persistent, impairing grief; her sadness is episodic and she functions well.
B. Chronic sorrow, a normal and recurring grief response ✔ KEY
Chronic sorrow describes periodic, recurring grief triggered by milestones in parents of children with lasting disabilities; it is not pathological.
C. Denial, the first stage that she has not yet moved beyond
She openly acknowledges the disability; renewed sadness is not denial, and stage models are not strictly linear.
D. A major depressive episode with a seasonal recurring pattern
Brief, triggered waves of sadness with good functioning do not meet criteria for a depressive episode.
Rationale: Olshansky introduced chronic sorrow to describe the recurring sadness of parents of children with lifelong disabilities. Grief resurfaces at milestones, transitions and reminders of the gap between expected and actual life, while day-to-day functioning and love for the child remain intact. Counselors normalize this pattern instead of framing it as failed acceptance.
References: Gladding (Counseling: A Comprehensive Profession) — Counseling families of people with disabilities: chronic sorrow
⚑ Check: Confirm the cited Gladding edition discusses chronic sorrow (Olshansky, 1962).

215. nce-s-trt-001 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · SMART goals and measurable objectives · easy · In review

A counselor is writing a treatment plan for a 34-year-old client with social anxiety. Which objective is BEST written according to SMART criteria?
A. Client will feel much more confident in social settings by the end of treatment.
Feeling "more confident" is not observable or measurable, and "end of treatment" is not a specific time frame.
B. Client will start one conversation with a coworker at least 3 days a week within 6 weeks. ✔ KEY
This is specific, measurable (frequency count), achievable, relevant to social anxiety, and time-bound (6 weeks).
C. Client will understand the cognitive roots of social anxiety and work through them fully.
"Understand" and "work through" are vague internal processes with no measurable criterion or deadline.
D. Client will attend weekly counseling sessions and complete homework as the counselor assigns.
Attendance and compliance describe the treatment process, not a measurable change in the presenting problem.
Rationale: SMART objectives are Specific, Measurable, Achievable, Relevant and Time-bound. A good objective names an observable behavior, a criterion for success, and a deadline, so progress can be tracked and the plan revised. Vague feeling states and process statements (attendance) do not meet the standard.
References: Wiger (Documentation) — Treatment plans: behaviorally measurable goals and objectives
⚑ Check: Wiger reference detail describes the concept, not an exact chapter title.

216. nce-s-trt-002 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · matching level of care to need · medium · In review

A 41-year-old client with an alcohol use disorder has relapsed twice while attending weekly outpatient sessions. There is no withdrawal risk, no acute medical or psychiatric concern, and the client has stable housing and a supportive partner. Which level of care is MOST appropriate?
A. Medically managed intensive inpatient treatment in a hospital setting
Hospital-level care is for severe, unstable withdrawal or acute medical/psychiatric needs, none of which are present.
B. Long-term residential treatment with 24-hour structure and support
Residential care is more restrictive than needed for a client with stable housing and a supportive recovery environment.
C. An intensive outpatient program with several sessions per week ✔ KEY
Repeated relapse despite weekly outpatient care, with no withdrawal, medical or housing barriers, points to a step up to the next more intensive outpatient level.
D. Continue weekly outpatient sessions with no change to the treatment plan
Repeated relapse at the current intensity signals that the present level is not meeting the client's needs.
Rationale: Placement criteria such as the ASAM Criteria match clients to the least restrictive level of care that safely meets their needs across multiple dimensions (withdrawal, medical, emotional, readiness, relapse potential, recovery environment). When a client is not progressing at one level and has no acute risks, the usual step is to the next more intensive level, not a jump to residential or hospital care.
References: ASAM Criteria — Multidimensional assessment and least restrictive level of care; outpatient vs intensive outpatient
⚑ Check: ASAM Criteria 4th ed. (2023) renamed some levels; confirm the level-of-care wording is current.

217. nce-s-trt-003 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · evidence-based treatment selection · medium · In review

A 29-year-old veteran meets criteria for posttraumatic stress disorder after a combat deployment. According to major clinical practice guidelines, which psychotherapy approach has the strongest research support as a first-line treatment?
A. Trauma-focused therapy such as cognitive processing therapy or prolonged exposure ✔ KEY
APA and VA/DoD guidelines give their strongest recommendations to trauma-focused therapies such as CPT and PE.
B. A single session of psychological debriefing held soon after returning home
Single-session debriefing is not recommended; it has not been shown to prevent or treat PTSD and may be harmful.
C. Open-ended nondirective supportive counseling focused on current life stressors
Nondirective supportive counseling is often used as a control condition and is not a first-line PTSD treatment.
D. Progressive muscle relaxation training used alone as the primary treatment
Relaxation may help with arousal, but used alone it lacks the support of trauma-focused treatments for PTSD.
Rationale: Evidence-based treatment selection integrates the best research evidence with clinical expertise and client preferences. For PTSD, the APA (2017) and VA/DoD guidelines recommend trauma-focused psychotherapies such as cognitive processing therapy and prolonged exposure as first-line treatments.
References: APA CPG — Clinical Practice Guideline for the Treatment of PTSD (2017): CPT, PE, CBT and CT strongly recommended · VA/DoD CPG — Management of PTSD and Acute Stress Disorder: trauma-focused psychotherapy as first-line treatment

A counselor has completed an intake with a 52-year-old Korean American client who reports grief and insomnia after her husband's death. What is the BEST way for the counselor to develop the treatment plan?
A. Write the plan alone based on the diagnosis, then review it with the client next week
Presenting a finished plan treats the client as a recipient rather than a partner and misses her priorities.
B. Use a standard grief protocol so the client receives the same plan as other clients
A one-size-fits-all plan ignores individual goals, cultural values and preferences.
C. Ask the client's adult children what goals they would like their mother to work on
Family input may be welcome with consent, but the client herself is the primary partner in setting her goals.
D. Work with the client to identify her goals and the methods she finds acceptable ✔ KEY
Collaborative planning engages the client in choosing goals and methods, respecting her values and culture.
Rationale: Treatment plans should be developed jointly with the client. Collaboration respects autonomy, incorporates cultural values and preferences, and increases engagement and follow-through. 219. nce-s-trt-005 · Assessment and Testing · progress monitoring and outcome measures · medium · In review

A counselor gives a 23-year-old client the PHQ-9 at every session. After eight sessions of treatment for depression, the client's scores have not meaningfully changed from intake. What should the counselor do NEXT?
A. Stop giving the PHQ-9, since repeated testing seems to be discouraging the client
Dropping the measure removes the very data that revealed the lack of progress.
B. Review the scores with the client and revise the treatment plan together ✔ KEY
Measurement-based care uses nonresponse as a signal to discuss progress with the client and adjust the plan.
C. Continue the same approach unchanged, since depression often improves slowly
Ignoring eight sessions of flat scores delays a needed change in treatment.
D. Refer the client out at once, since the lack of change shows a poor counselor fit
Flat scores call for review and adjustment first; immediate referral is premature.
Rationale: Routine outcome monitoring with a standardized measure lets the counselor detect clients who are not responding. Feedback on scores should be shared with the client and used to revisit goals, interventions or level of care. Measurement-based care has been shown to improve outcomes, especially for clients at risk of treatment failure.
References: Hays (Assessment) — Outcome assessment and progress monitoring with standardized measures

A. Call the psychiatrist right away to share the intake findings and diagnosis
Sharing information without the client's authorization breaches confidentiality.
B. Ask the client to stop seeing the psychiatrist to avoid conflicting treatment
Medication management and counseling are complementary; there is no basis for asking her to stop.
C. Request the client's written consent to inform and consult with the psychiatrist ✔ KEY
The ACA Code directs counselors to request a release so they can inform the other professional and collaborate.
D. Note the psychiatrist in the file and leave all contact entirely up to the client
This is passive; counselors are expected to seek permission to coordinate with other providers.

A. At intake, revisiting the plan throughout the course of treatment ✔ KEY
Planning for discharge from the start keeps treatment goal-directed and prepares the client for transition.
B. In the final session, once the client has met every treatment goal
Waiting until the last session leaves too little time to arrange supports and aftercare.
C. When the insurer signals that authorized sessions are running out
Letting payer limits drive discharge timing is reactive and can leave gaps in care.
D. Only after the client asks to end treatment or stops attending
This leaves aftercare to chance and misses the chance to plan termination together.

222. nce-s-trt-008 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · safety planning · medium · In review

A counselor is completing a Stanley-Brown Safety Planning Intervention with a 19-year-old college student who has recent suicidal ideation. Which step does the safety plan address FIRST?
A. Listing professionals and crisis lines to contact in an emergency
Professional and crisis contacts come later in the plan, after self-directed and social steps.
B. Making the environment safe by limiting access to lethal means
Means safety is a key part of the plan but is addressed as the final step.
C. Naming people the student can ask directly for help in a crisis
Reaching out to trusted people for help follows warning signs and internal coping.
D. Recognizing personal warning signs that a crisis may be developing ✔ KEY
The plan begins with identifying warning signs so the client knows when to use the plan.
Rationale: The Stanley-Brown Safety Plan proceeds in order: (1) warning signs, (2) internal coping strategies, (3) people and social settings that provide distraction, (4) people to ask for help, (5) professionals and agencies to contact, and (6) making the environment safe. Recognizing warning signs comes first because it tells the client when to begin using the plan.

223. nce-s-trt-009 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · stages of change and treatment readiness · easy · In review

A 38-year-old client says, "I know my drinking is hurting my marriage, and part of me wants to cut back, but I'm just not sure I'm ready to give it up." Which stage of change does this statement BEST reflect?
A. Precontemplation
In precontemplation the person does not see a problem or intend to change; this client recognizes the problem.
B. Contemplation ✔ KEY
Acknowledging the problem while feeling ambivalent about changing is the hallmark of contemplation.
C. Preparation
Preparation involves a commitment to act soon and small steps toward change, which are absent here.
D. Action
Action involves actively modifying the behavior, which this client has not begun.
Rationale: In the Transtheoretical Model, contemplation is marked by awareness that a problem exists and serious thought about change, combined with ambivalence and no firm commitment. Counselors often use motivational interviewing to explore and resolve that ambivalence.

224. nce-s-trt-010 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · stages of change and treatment readiness · hard · In review

A 60-year-old client stopped smoking four months ago. He uses nicotine gum, avoids places where he used to smoke, and calls a friend when cravings hit. According to Prochaska and DiClemente, which stage is he in?
A. Preparation
Preparation precedes the behavior change; this client has already quit.
B. Maintenance
Maintenance generally begins after about six months of sustained change; he is at four months.
C. Action ✔ KEY
He has changed the behavior within the past six months and is actively using change strategies.
D. Contemplation
Contemplation involves thinking about change without acting; he has already acted.
Rationale: The action stage covers roughly the first six months after a person has made an overt change in behavior, when effort and relapse risk are highest. Maintenance begins once the change has been sustained for more than about six months and focuses on preventing relapse.

225. nce-s-trt-011 · Research and Program Evaluation · program evaluation and needs assessment · medium · In review

Three months into a new school-based anger management group, a counselor gathers feedback from students and teachers so she can refine the sessions while the program is still running. This is an example of which type of evaluation?
A. Formative evaluation ✔ KEY
Formative evaluation occurs during implementation to improve the program as it runs.
B. Summative evaluation
Summative evaluation judges overall effectiveness or outcomes after the program is completed.
C. Needs assessment
A needs assessment happens before a program is designed, to identify gaps and priorities.
D. Cost-benefit analysis
Cost-benefit analysis compares program costs with monetary value of outcomes, not process feedback.
Rationale: Formative evaluation is conducted while a program is under way to give feedback for improvement. Summative evaluation is conducted at the end to judge whether the program achieved its goals and should be continued, expanded or ended.

226. nce-s-trt-012 · Research and Program Evaluation · program evaluation and needs assessment · easy · In review

Before creating any new services, a rural counseling center surveys residents, local physicians and school staff to find out which mental health concerns are most common and which services are missing. What is the center conducting?
A. An outcome evaluation
Outcome evaluation measures the effects of an existing program; no program exists yet.
B. A process evaluation
Process evaluation examines how an existing program is being delivered.
C. A fidelity review
A fidelity review checks whether a program is delivered as designed, which requires a program in place.
D. A needs assessment ✔ KEY
Gathering data on community problems and service gaps before planning services is a needs assessment.

227. nce-s-trt-013 · Research and Program Evaluation · research design basics · easy · In review

A school counselor records a student's disruptive outbursts during baseline, introduces a token system, removes it, and then reintroduces it (an ABAB design). What is the main purpose of the withdrawal phase?
A. To let the student rest so the token system does not lose its appeal
Withdrawal is not about preventing satiation; it is a test of experimental control.
B. To show that behavior changes when and only when the treatment changes ✔ KEY
A return toward baseline when treatment is removed, and improvement when it returns, supports a causal link.
C. To compare the student's behavior with a randomly assigned control group
Single-subject designs use the person as his or her own control; there is no separate group.
D. To produce enough data points to compute a reliable correlation value
The goal of the reversal is to demonstrate experimental control, not to compute a correlation.
Rationale: In an ABAB (reversal or withdrawal) single-subject design, behavior is measured at baseline (A), during treatment (B), after treatment is withdrawn (A), and after it is reinstated (B). If behavior worsens on withdrawal and improves again on reinstatement, the counselor has stronger evidence that the intervention, not some other factor, caused the change.
References: Erford (Research and Evaluation in Counseling) — Single-subject designs: AB and ABAB (withdrawal) designs

228. nce-s-trt-014 · Research and Program Evaluation · research design basics · medium · In review

A researcher delivers a new career-readiness curriculum to one intact ninth-grade class and uses a second existing class at the same school as a comparison. Both classes complete pretests and posttests. Which design is this?
A. A true experimental pretest-posttest control group design
A true experiment requires random assignment of participants to conditions, which did not occur.
B. A single-subject multiple-baseline design across settings
Single-subject designs track individuals over repeated phases, not two classes compared once.
C. A quasi-experimental nonequivalent control group design ✔ KEY
Using intact groups without random assignment, with pre- and posttests, is a nonequivalent group quasi-experiment.
D. A correlational design examining two naturally linked variables
The researcher manipulated an independent variable (the curriculum), so this is not correlational.
Rationale: The defining feature of a true experiment is random assignment to conditions. When a researcher manipulates an independent variable but uses pre-existing groups, the design is quasi-experimental. Pretests help assess initial group differences, but selection remains a threat to internal validity.
References: Erford (Research and Evaluation in Counseling) — Experimental vs quasi-experimental designs; random assignment

229. nce-s-trt-015 · Research and Program Evaluation · research design basics · hard · In review

A counselor selects the 15 students with the highest scores on a test anxiety scale for a six-week group. There is no comparison group, all students complete the group, and nothing notable happens at school during that time. Posttest scores are lower. Which threat to internal validity MOST likely accounts for part of this drop?
A. Experimental mortality, or attrition of group members
All students completed the group, so loss of participants cannot explain the change.
B. History, or outside events occurring during the study period
The stem rules out notable outside events during the six weeks.
C. Diffusion of treatment between groups being compared
Diffusion requires a comparison group that is exposed to the treatment; there is none.
D. Statistical regression of extreme scores toward the mean ✔ KEY
Participants selected for extreme scores tend to score closer to the mean on retest even without treatment.
Rationale: Statistical regression is a threat to internal validity whenever participants are chosen because of extreme scores. Because no measure is perfectly reliable, extreme scorers tend to move toward the mean on retesting, which can be mistaken for a treatment effect. A comparison group selected the same way helps rule this out.
References: Erford (Research and Evaluation in Counseling) — Threats to internal validity: statistical regression
☐ Approve☐ Revise☐ RetireNotes:

230. nce-s-trt-016 · Research and Program Evaluation · statistics used in outcome research · hard · In review

A researcher sets alpha at .05 and finds p = .03 when comparing a new intervention with treatment as usual, so she rejects the null hypothesis. If the intervention in fact has no real effect, what has occurred?
A. A Type I error, a false positive finding ✔ KEY
Rejecting a null hypothesis that is actually true is a Type I error; its probability is set by alpha.
B. A Type II error, a false negative finding
A Type II error is failing to reject a false null, missing a real effect; here the null was rejected.
C. A correct decision, since p fell below alpha
A p-value below alpha does not guarantee a true effect; if there is none, rejecting the null was an error.
D. A power failure caused by too small a sample
Low power raises the risk of Type II errors, not of falsely detecting an effect that does not exist.
Rationale: A Type I error (alpha) is rejecting a true null hypothesis, concluding an effect exists when it does not. A Type II error (beta) is failing to reject a false null. The p-value is the probability of results at least this extreme if the null is true; it is not the probability that the null is true, so a significant p can still be a false positive.
References: Erford (Research and Evaluation in Counseling) — Hypothesis testing: Type I and Type II errors, alpha and p-values
☐ Approve☐ Revise☐ RetireNotes:

231. nce-s-trt-017 · Research and Program Evaluation · statistics used in outcome research · medium · In review

A study of 4,000 clients finds that a brief online module reduces anxiety scores compared with a waitlist, p < .001, with Cohen's d = 0.08. How should a counselor BEST interpret these results?
A. The module has a large effect, as shown by the very small p-value
The p-value reflects sample size as well as effect; it does not indicate magnitude.
B. The difference is statistically significant but practically trivial ✔ KEY
With a very large sample, tiny effects reach significance; d = 0.08 is well below even a small effect (0.2).
C. The result is likely a Type II error because the sample was so large
A Type II error is missing a real effect; here an effect was detected, and large samples reduce Type II risk.
D. The module causes anxiety to drop by about 8 percent for most clients
Cohen's d is a difference in standard deviation units, not a percentage reduction.
Rationale: Statistical significance tells whether an effect is unlikely under the null hypothesis; effect size tells how large it is. Cohen's guidelines for d are about 0.2 small, 0.5 medium and 0.8 large. Large samples can make trivial differences significant, so counselors should look at effect size to judge clinical or practical importance.
References: Erford (Research and Evaluation in Counseling) — Effect size (Cohen's d) vs statistical significance
☐ Approve☐ Revise☐ RetireNotes:

232. nce-s-trt-018 · Research and Program Evaluation · research design basics · easy · In review

A survey of 600 adolescents finds that hours spent on social media correlate r = .45 with scores on a depressive symptom scale. Which conclusion is BEST supported by this finding?
A. Heavy social media use causes depressive symptoms in teenagers
A correlation cannot establish causation; depression could drive use, or a third variable could affect both.
B. Teens who use social media more tend to report more depressive symptoms ✔ KEY
A positive correlation means higher values on one variable tend to go with higher values on the other, without implying cause.
C. About 45 percent of depressive symptoms are explained by social media use
Shared variance is r squared (.45 x .45, about 20 percent), and even that describes association, not explanation by cause.
D. Reducing social media time will lower depression scores for most teens
Predicting the effect of changing one variable is a causal claim that requires an experimental design.
Rationale: Correlational designs show whether and how strongly two variables move together, but not why. The direction of influence is unknown, and a third variable (such as loneliness or poor sleep) may account for both. The coefficient of determination (r squared) gives the proportion of shared variance; here about 20 percent.
References: Erford (Research and Evaluation in Counseling) — Correlational research: correlation vs causation and the coefficient of determination
☐ Approve☐ Revise☐ RetireNotes:

233. nce-s-trt-019 · Research and Program Evaluation · statistics used in outcome research · medium · In review

A statistics instructor asks counseling students to classify several variables by scale of measurement. Which variable is measured on an interval scale?
A. Clients' ranked order of improvement
Ranks show order but not equal distances between positions, which makes them ordinal.
B. Number of sessions each client attended
A count has equal intervals and a true zero (no sessions), which makes it a ratio scale.
C. Temperature in degrees Fahrenheit ✔ KEY
Fahrenheit has equal intervals but an arbitrary zero point, the defining feature of an interval scale.
D. Clients' primary diagnostic category
Diagnostic categories are named groups with no inherent order, which makes them nominal.
Rationale: The four scales build on each other: nominal (categories), ordinal (rank order), interval (equal units but no true zero), and ratio (equal units plus a true zero). Because interval data lack a true zero, ratios are not meaningful; 80 degrees Fahrenheit is not twice as hot as 40 degrees.
References: Erford (Research and Evaluation in Counseling) — Scales of measurement: nominal, ordinal, interval and ratio
☐ Approve☐ Revise☐ RetireNotes:

234. nce-s-trt-020 · Research and Program Evaluation · statistics used in outcome research · medium · In review

Annual household incomes of clients at a community counseling agency are mostly low, but a small number of clients report very high incomes. Which statement about this distribution is accurate?
A. It is positively skewed, and the mean is higher than the median ✔ KEY
A tail of a few very high values is a positive skew, and the mean is pulled toward that tail, above the median.
B. It is negatively skewed, and the mean is lower than the median
A negative skew has its tail toward low values; here the tail is toward high incomes.
C. It is positively skewed, and the mode is higher than the mean
In a positive skew the mode sits at the peak on the low end, below both the median and the mean.
D. It is symmetrical, so the mean, median and mode are about equal
A few extreme high values make the distribution asymmetrical, so the three measures separate.
Rationale: Skew is named for the direction of the tail. In a positively skewed distribution, the typical order is mode < median < mean, because the mean is sensitive to extreme scores. For skewed data such as income, the median is usually the best single measure of central tendency.
References: Erford (Research and Evaluation in Counseling) — Descriptive statistics: measures of central tendency and skewed distributions
☐ Approve☐ Revise☐ RetireNotes:

235. nce-s-trt-021 · Research and Program Evaluation · statistics used in outcome research · medium · In review

Scores on a depression inventory are normally distributed with a mean of 50 and a standard deviation of 10. A client scores 60. Approximately what percentage of the norm group scored at or below this client?
A. 34 percent
About 34 percent of scores fall between the mean and +1 SD; this omits the 50 percent below the mean.
B. 50 percent
Fifty percent falls at or below the mean of 50, not at a score one SD above it.
C. 68 percent
About 68 percent fall within plus or minus 1 SD of the mean, which is a band, not a cumulative percentage.
D. 84 percent ✔ KEY
A score of 60 is z = +1; 50 percent fall below the mean plus about 34 percent between the mean and +1 SD.
Rationale: The standard deviation describes how spread out scores are around the mean. In a normal distribution, about 68 percent of scores fall within 1 SD of the mean, 95 percent within 2 SD, and 99.7 percent within 3 SD. A score 1 SD above the mean (z = +1, T = 60) sits at about the 84th percentile.
References: Erford (Research and Evaluation in Counseling) — Standard deviation, the normal curve and percentile ranks
☐ Approve☐ Revise☐ RetireNotes:

236. nce-s-trt-022 · Research and Program Evaluation · statistics used in outcome research · medium · In review

A researcher randomly assigns 90 clients to one of three conditions (individual CBT, a mindfulness group, or a waitlist) and compares the groups on posttest anxiety scores measured on an interval scale. Which statistical test is MOST appropriate?
A. An independent-samples t-test
A t-test compares only two group means; running several t-tests inflates the Type I error rate.
B. A one-way analysis of variance ✔ KEY
One categorical independent variable with three levels and one interval dependent variable calls for a one-way ANOVA.
C. A chi-square test of independence
Chi-square tests relationships between categorical variables (frequencies), not differences in mean scores.
D. A Pearson product-moment correlation
Pearson r measures the linear relationship between two continuous variables, not group differences.
Rationale: Here the independent variable is treatment condition (three levels) and the dependent variable is the anxiety score. A one-way ANOVA compares three or more group means in a single test while holding the Type I error rate at alpha; post hoc tests then show which groups differ. A t-test is used for two groups, chi-square for categorical outcomes, and Pearson r for relationships between two continuous variables.
References: Erford (Research and Evaluation in Counseling) — Selecting inferential statistics: t-test, ANOVA, chi-square and correlation
☐ Approve☐ Revise☐ RetireNotes:

237. nce-s-trt-023 · Research and Program Evaluation · statistics used in outcome research · hard · In review

A counselor-researcher is planning a study comparing two interventions and wants to reduce the risk of missing a true but modest difference between them. Which change would MOST directly increase statistical power?
A. Lowering alpha from .05 to .01 to guard against false positives
A stricter alpha makes it harder to reject the null, which lowers power and raises Type II risk.
B. Using a smaller sample so each participant gets more attention
Smaller samples increase sampling error and reduce power to detect a real effect.
C. Increasing the number of participants assigned to each condition ✔ KEY
Larger samples reduce standard error, making a true effect of a given size more likely to be detected.
D. Switching from a one-tailed test to a two-tailed test of the effect
Splitting alpha across two tails lowers power for an effect in the predicted direction.
Rationale: Power (1 - beta) is the probability of correctly rejecting a false null hypothesis. It rises with larger sample size, larger effect size, a more lenient alpha, a one-tailed test (when the direction is correct), and more reliable measures. Researchers run a power analysis before a study, often targeting .80, to choose a sample size that can detect the expected effect.
References: Erford (Research and Evaluation in Counseling) — Statistical power, sample size and Type II error
☐ Approve☐ Revise☐ RetireNotes:

238. nce-s-trt-024 · Research and Program Evaluation · research design basics · hard · In review

A trial finds that a new anxiety program works well. Participants knew they were in a closely watched study, received extra attention from research staff, and liked being part of something new. Critics doubt that the results will hold in routine agency practice. Which threat are the critics MOST likely describing?
A. Reactive effects of the experimental arrangements ✔ KEY
Changes caused by knowing one is studied (a Hawthorne-type effect) limit generalization to ordinary settings, a threat to external validity.
B. Statistical regression of extreme scores toward the mean
Regression is an internal validity threat tied to selecting extreme scorers, which the stem does not describe.
C. Instrumentation changes in how outcomes were measured
Instrumentation is an internal validity threat involving shifts in measurement, not generalizability.
D. Selection differences between the groups being compared
Selection is an internal validity threat about nonequivalent groups, not about generalizing to practice.
Rationale: External validity concerns whether results generalize to other people, settings and times. Reactive arrangements, such as the Hawthorne effect, occur when the research situation itself (attention, novelty, awareness of being studied) contributes to the outcome, so effects may shrink in routine care. Internal validity threats such as history, regression, selection and instrumentation instead question whether the treatment caused the change at all.
References: Erford (Research and Evaluation in Counseling) — Threats to external validity: reactive effects of experimental arrangements
☐ Approve☐ Revise☐ RetireNotes:

239. nce-s-trt-025 · Research and Program Evaluation · research design basics · hard · In review

A school counselor wants to show that a social skills intervention caused improvement for three elementary students on the autism spectrum. Once learned, the skills are not expected to fade, and withdrawing the intervention would be ethically questionable. Which design BEST fits these conditions?
A. An AB design with a single baseline and treatment phase
An AB design lacks replication, so it cannot rule out other explanations such as maturation or history.
B. An ABAB reversal design that withdraws the intervention
Reversal requires withdrawing treatment and expects behavior to return to baseline, neither of which fits here.
C. A one-group pretest-posttest design with all three students
This pre-experimental design has no control for threats such as maturation or history.
D. A multiple-baseline design staggered across the students ✔ KEY
Starting the intervention at different times for each student shows control without withdrawing treatment.
Rationale: In a multiple-baseline design, baselines begin together but the intervention is introduced at staggered points across participants, behaviors or settings. If each behavior changes only when the intervention starts for it, the counselor gains evidence of causation without a reversal. It is the design of choice when behavior is not reversible or withdrawal would be unethical; a simple AB design shows change but not experimental control.
References: Erford (Research and Evaluation in Counseling) — Single-subject designs: AB vs multiple-baseline designs
☐ Approve☐ Revise☐ RetireNotes:

240. nce-s-trt-026 · Research and Program Evaluation · research design basics · medium · In review

A researcher conducts in-depth interviews with 12 parents who have lost a child to overdose. Her goal is to describe the essence of how parents live through this kind of grief, setting aside her own assumptions as she analyzes the data. Which qualitative tradition does this study reflect?
A. Grounded theory
Grounded theory aims to generate a theory of a process through constant comparison and theoretical sampling.
B. Phenomenology ✔ KEY
Describing the essence of a lived experience and bracketing researcher assumptions are hallmarks of phenomenology.
C. Ethnography
Ethnography studies the shared patterns of a culture-sharing group through prolonged fieldwork and observation.
D. Case study
A case study examines one or a few bounded systems in depth using multiple sources of data.
Rationale: Phenomenology seeks the essential structure of a lived experience shared by several people, typically through in-depth interviews, with the researcher bracketing (epoche) personal assumptions. Grounded theory builds theory from data, ethnography describes a culture, and a case study explores a bounded case in its context.
References: Erford (Research and Evaluation in Counseling) — Qualitative traditions: phenomenology, grounded theory, ethnography and case study
☐ Approve☐ Revise☐ RetireNotes:

241. nce-s-trt-027 · Research and Program Evaluation · research design basics · easy · In review

After coding interviews with first-generation college students, a counselor-researcher shares her preliminary themes with the participants and asks whether the themes accurately capture their experiences. Which trustworthiness strategy is she using?
A. Triangulation
Triangulation compares multiple data sources, methods, investigators or theories, not participant review of findings.
B. Peer debriefing
Peer debriefing involves a colleague, not the participants, reviewing and questioning the analysis.
C. Member checking ✔ KEY
Returning interpretations to participants to confirm their accuracy is member checking.
D. Audit trail
An audit trail documents decisions throughout the study so an outside reviewer can follow them.
Rationale: Trustworthiness is the qualitative counterpart of validity and reliability, with criteria such as credibility, transferability, dependability and confirmability (Lincoln and Guba). Member checking is a key credibility strategy; triangulation, peer debriefing, prolonged engagement, thick description and audit trails support other criteria.
References: Erford (Research and Evaluation in Counseling) — Trustworthiness in qualitative research: member checking and triangulation
☐ Approve☐ Revise☐ RetireNotes:

242. nce-s-trt-028 · Research and Program Evaluation · research design basics · medium · In review

A state counseling association plans to survey its members and wants school, clinical mental health and rehabilitation counselors represented in the sample in the same proportions as in the full membership, with members chosen at random. Which sampling method BEST meets this goal?
A. Stratified random sampling ✔ KEY
Dividing the population into subgroups and randomly sampling from each guarantees proportional representation.
B. Simple random sampling
Simple random sampling gives everyone an equal chance but does not guarantee each subgroup's proportion.
C. Convenience sampling
Convenience sampling uses whoever is easily available and is neither random nor proportional.
D. Snowball sampling
Snowball sampling recruits through referrals from participants and is used for hard-to-reach groups.
Rationale: Stratified random sampling divides a population into strata on a key characteristic and then randomly selects from each stratum, often in proportion to its size. It improves representativeness on that characteristic compared with simple random sampling, while convenience and snowball samples are nonprobability methods that limit generalization.
References: Erford (Research and Evaluation in Counseling) — Probability vs nonprobability sampling: stratified random sampling

243. nce-s-trt-029 · Research and Program Evaluation · statistics used in outcome research · easy · In review

A researcher locates 48 published and unpublished studies of group counseling for adolescent depression and statistically combines their effect sizes to estimate an overall effect. What type of study is this?
A. A narrative literature review
A narrative review summarizes studies in words without statistically pooling their results.
B. A longitudinal cohort study
A cohort study follows one group of participants over time and collects new data.
C. A randomized controlled trial
An RCT randomly assigns its own participants to conditions rather than combining prior studies.
D. A meta-analysis ✔ KEY
Quantitatively pooling effect sizes across many studies to estimate an overall effect is meta-analysis.
Rationale: Meta-analysis converts the results of many studies to a common effect size metric (such as Cohen's d) and combines them, weighting larger studies more heavily. Including unpublished studies helps reduce publication bias. Meta-analyses of randomized trials are often placed at the top of evidence hierarchies used in evidence-based practice.
References: Erford (Research and Evaluation in Counseling) — Meta-analysis and effect sizes

244. nce-s-trt-030 · Research and Program Evaluation · program evaluation and needs assessment · medium · In review

A college counseling center builds a logic model for a new peer-support program. Which entry belongs in the outcomes column of the model rather than in the outputs column?
A. Number of peer-support sessions held each semester
Sessions delivered are an output, a count of activity, not a change in participants.
B. Number of students who attended at least one session
Attendance counts are outputs that show reach, not program effects.
C. Lower loneliness scores among participating students ✔ KEY
A change in participants' loneliness is an outcome, the intended effect of the program.
D. Number of peer supporters who finished the training course
Trained supporters are a product of program activities, which makes this an output.
Rationale: A logic model links inputs (resources) to activities, outputs (direct products such as sessions held and people served), and outcomes (changes in knowledge, behavior or well-being). Process evaluation examines whether activities and outputs are delivered as planned; outcome evaluation examines whether the intended changes occurred.
References: Erford (Research and Evaluation in Counseling) — Program evaluation: logic models, process vs outcome evaluation

245. nce-s-trt-031 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · SMART goals and measurable objectives · easy · In review

The parents and teacher of an 8-year-old boy report frequent tantrums when classwork becomes frustrating. Which objective for his treatment plan is MOST measurable?
A. He will use his calm-down card instead of a tantrum in 4 of 5 hard tasks by March ✔ KEY
This names an observable replacement behavior, a success criterion (4 of 5 tasks) and a deadline.
B. He will gain better control of his emotions and feel happier at school this year
Emotional control and happiness are not defined in observable terms and lack a success criterion.
C. He will learn why tantrums upset classmates and grow more empathy toward them
Understanding and empathy are internal states with no observable measure or time frame.
D. He will meet with the school counselor every week to talk through angry feelings
Attending sessions describes the intervention, not a measurable change in the problem behavior.
Rationale: Objectives for children should describe a specific, observable behavior that teachers or parents can count, a criterion for success, and a target date. Framing the objective as a replacement behavior (using a coping tool) rather than only the absence of tantrums also gives the child something concrete to practice and adults something to reinforce.
References: Wiger (Documentation) — Treatment plans: observable, measurable objectives
⚑ Check: Wiger reference detail describes the concept, not an exact chapter title.

246. nce-s-trt-032 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · matching level of care to need · medium · In review

A 26-year-old man with schizophrenia was discharged from a psychiatric unit two weeks ago. He is taking his medication and has no suicidal or homicidal ideation, but he has missed several outpatient appointments and struggles to manage daily tasks on his own. Consistent with the least restrictive environment, which plan is MOST appropriate?
A. Readmit him to the inpatient unit until his attendance becomes consistent
He has no acute risk; hospitalizing him for missed appointments is far more restrictive than needed.
B. Add assertive community treatment or case management to support him at home ✔ KEY
Bringing intensive supports to him in the community addresses engagement and daily functioning in the least restrictive setting.
C. Arrange long-term placement in a locked residential facility for his safety
A locked setting is unjustified for a client who is stable and not dangerous to himself or others.
D. Continue monthly outpatient visits and close the case if he misses another
This ignores clear signs that his current level of support is not enough and risks disengagement.
Rationale: The least restrictive environment principle calls for providing care in the most integrated, least confining setting that can safely meet the client's needs. When a client is stable but struggling to engage and function, the first step is to intensify community-based supports, such as assertive community treatment or case management, rather than move him to a more restrictive setting.
References: Gladding (Counseling: A Comprehensive Profession) — Clinical mental health counseling: community-based care and the least restrictive setting
⚑ Check: Gladding reference detail is a concept paraphrase; confirm a better-matched source if available.

247. nce-s-trt-033 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · discharge and aftercare planning · medium · In review

A 33-year-old client finishing treatment for alcohol use disorder is building a relapse prevention plan with her counselor. Based on the cognitive-behavioral relapse prevention model, which element is MOST central to the plan?
A. Agreeing that any single lapse means treatment failed and must start over
This all-or-nothing view feeds the abstinence violation effect, which turns lapses into full relapses.
B. Avoiding all talk of cravings so that the urges lose their power over time
Avoiding discussion of cravings leaves her unprepared; the model teaches recognizing and coping with urges.
C. Focusing the plan mainly on the past events that first led her to drink
Relapse prevention is present- and future-focused, centered on upcoming risks and skills.
D. Identifying her high-risk situations and rehearsing coping responses ✔ KEY
Spotting personal high-risk situations and practicing coping skills for them is the core of relapse prevention.
Rationale: Marlatt and Gordon's relapse prevention model holds that relapse risk rises when a person meets a high-risk situation without an effective coping response. Plans therefore identify triggers, build and rehearse coping skills, increase self-efficacy, and treat a lapse as a learning opportunity rather than a failure, countering the abstinence violation effect.
References: Corey (Theory & Practice) — Cognitive behavioral approaches: relapse prevention, high-risk situations and coping skills
⚑ Check: Confirm the Corey edition in use covers relapse prevention; Marlatt & Gordon is the primary source but is not on the approved list.

248. nce-s-trt-034 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · coordination of care and referral · easy · In review

An interdisciplinary team at an adolescent residential program decides that clients who miss group will lose family phone calls for a week. The counselor on the team believes this decision raises ethical concerns. What should the counselor do FIRST?
A. Report the team to the state licensing board for unethical practice
Outside avenues are pursued only after attempts to resolve the concern within the team have failed.
B. Tell the clients' families privately that she disagrees with the team
Going around the team to families undermines the team and does not address the decision itself.
C. Raise the concern with the team and try to resolve it within the team ✔ KEY
The ACA Code directs counselors to first attempt to resolve ethical concerns about team decisions within the team.
D. Follow the decision without comment, since team consensus outranks her
Counselors keep their own ethical obligations on a team and may not simply defer to consensus.
Rationale: Counselors on interdisciplinary teams keep the focus on how best to serve clients and clarify the professional and ethical obligations of the team and its members. When a team decision raises ethical concerns, they first try to resolve the concern within the team; if that fails, they pursue other avenues consistent with client well-being.
References: ACA Code of Ethics — D.1.d Establishing Professional and Ethical Obligations
⚑ Check: Confirm the section number D.1.d for resolving team ethical concerns within the team (vs D.1.c Interdisciplinary Teamwork).

249. nce-s-cou-001 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · psychodynamic and psychoanalytic theory · easy · In review

After a tense meeting in which she felt belittled by her supervisor, a client goes home and yells at her teenage son over a minor chore. Which defense mechanism does this BEST illustrate?
A. Projection
Projection attributes one's own unacceptable impulses to someone else; she is not accusing her son of her feelings.
B. Displacement ✔ KEY
Displacement redirects an impulse from a threatening target (the supervisor) to a safer one (the son).
C. Reaction formation
Reaction formation expresses the opposite of the true impulse, such as excessive friendliness toward the supervisor.
D. Sublimation
Sublimation channels an impulse into a socially valued outlet, such as vigorous exercise; yelling at her son is not that.
Rationale: In Freudian theory the ego uses defense mechanisms to reduce anxiety. Displacement shifts feelings away from the person who aroused them toward a less threatening target, as when anger at a boss is discharged at a family member.
References: Corey (Theory & Practice) — Psychoanalytic Therapy: ego-defense mechanisms (displacement)
☐ Approve☐ Revise☐ RetireNotes:

250. nce-s-cou-002 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · psychodynamic and psychoanalytic theory · medium · In review

Several months into treatment, a client begins reacting to the counselor's neutral comments with hurt and anger, saying, "You sound just like my father, always judging me." From a psychoanalytic perspective, the counselor should view this reaction as:
A. Countertransference that the counselor ought to disclose to the client
Countertransference is the counselor's own emotional reaction to the client; here the reaction is the client's.
B. Resistance that the counselor should confront firmly and directly
Analysts explore resistance rather than confront it firmly, and this reaction is better understood as transference.
C. A realistic response to the counselor's own critical style
The comments were neutral, so the client is reacting to a figure from the past rather than to the counselor's actual behavior.
D. Transference that offers material for analysis and interpretation ✔ KEY
The client is redirecting feelings about a significant past figure onto the counselor, which analysts treat as central therapeutic material.
Rationale: Transference occurs when a client unconsciously shifts feelings and attitudes from significant early figures onto the therapist. In psychoanalytic work, analyzing and interpreting transference helps clients gain insight into unresolved conflicts and how they repeat in current relationships.
References: Corey (Theory & Practice) — Psychoanalytic Therapy: transference and its interpretation
☐ Approve☐ Revise☐ RetireNotes:

251. nce-s-cou-003 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · Adlerian therapy · medium · In review

An Adlerian counselor tells a client that his frequent helplessness seems to get his siblings to handle his responsibilities for him. Once the hidden payoff is named, the behavior becomes less appealing to the client. This technique is known as:
A. Spitting in the client's soup ✔ KEY
Exposing the purpose or payoff of a behavior "spoils" it, so the client can no longer use it as innocently as before.
B. Acting as if
Acting as if asks the client to behave as though already the person he wishes to be; it does not expose a payoff.
C. Catching oneself
Catching oneself has the client notice self-defeating behavior in the moment; here the counselor names its purpose.
D. The push-button technique
The push-button technique has clients alternate pleasant and unpleasant images to see that they create their feelings.
Rationale: Adlerian therapy assumes behavior is purposeful. "Spitting in the client's soup" means pointing out the hidden goal or payoff of a self-defeating behavior; the client may continue it, but it no longer tastes as good.
References: Corey (Theory & Practice) — Adlerian Therapy: techniques of the reorientation phase

252. nce-s-cou-004 · Human Growth and Development · Adlerian therapy · medium · In review

In Adler's view of psychological birth order, which child is MOST associated with the experience of being "dethroned" when a new sibling arrives?
A. The second-born child
Adler described the second child as racing to catch up with the older sibling, not as dethroned.
B. The youngest child
The youngest is described as the "baby" who may be pampered; no later sibling displaces them.
C. The oldest child ✔ KEY
The firstborn enjoys undivided attention until a sibling is born and must then adjust to losing that favored position.
D. The only child
An only child is never displaced by a sibling, although Adler noted they may struggle with sharing.
Rationale: Adler emphasized psychological (not merely ordinal) birth position within the family constellation. The oldest child is initially the center of attention and is "dethroned" by a sibling's birth, which can shape a lifestyle focused on responsibility, achievement, or regaining status.
References: Corey (Theory & Practice) — Adlerian Therapy: birth order and the family constellation

253. nce-s-cou-005 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · person-centered therapy · easy · In review

Carl Rogers proposed that clients move toward growth when the counselor offers congruence, accurate empathic understanding, and which other core condition?
A. Interpretation of defenses
Interpreting defenses is a psychoanalytic technique, not a Rogerian core condition.
B. Structured homework assignments
Homework is typical of cognitive and behavioral approaches, not person-centered therapy.
C. Unconditional positive regard ✔ KEY
Rogers named unconditional positive regard, nonjudgmental acceptance of the client, as a core condition.
D. Confrontation of irrational beliefs
Disputing irrational beliefs is central to REBT, not to the person-centered approach.
Rationale: Rogers held that congruence (genuineness), unconditional positive regard, and accurate empathic understanding are necessary and sufficient conditions for therapeutic change. The counselor's attitudes, rather than techniques, release the client's actualizing tendency.
References: Corey (Theory & Practice) — Person-Centered Therapy: core therapeutic conditions

254. nce-s-cou-006 · Human Growth and Development · person-centered therapy · hard · In review

A college student tells her counselor she feels worthwhile only when she earns top grades, the one achievement her parents consistently praised while growing up. From a person-centered perspective, her distress MOST likely stems from:
A. Internalized conditions of worth that distort her self-concept ✔ KEY
Rogers held that conditional acceptance from significant others leads people to value themselves only when meeting those conditions, creating incongruence.
B. An unresolved conflict between her id impulses and her superego
Id-superego conflict is a psychoanalytic formulation, not a person-centered one.
C. Irrational demands that she must perform well to be acceptable
Absolutistic "musts" are the REBT explanation of disturbance, not Rogers's.
D. A mistaken private logic formed in her early family constellation
Private logic and the family constellation are Adlerian concepts.
Rationale: Rogers described how children who receive only conditional positive regard introject conditions of worth. They then deny or distort experiences that do not fit, producing incongruence between self-concept and experience, which shows up as anxiety and vulnerability.
References: Corey (Theory & Practice) — Person-Centered Therapy: conditions of worth and incongruence

255. nce-s-cou-007 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · existential therapy · easy · In review

Which theorist founded logotherapy, drawing on his experiences in Nazi concentration camps to argue that people can find meaning even in unavoidable suffering?
A. Rollo May
May was an American existential psychologist known for his work on anxiety, freedom, and love, not logotherapy.
B. Irvin Yalom
Yalom described the existential givens and group therapeutic factors but did not found logotherapy.
C. Fritz Perls
Perls founded gestalt therapy.
D. Viktor Frankl ✔ KEY
Frankl developed logotherapy, centered on the will to meaning, and wrote Man's Search for Meaning.
Rationale: Viktor Frankl's logotherapy holds that the primary human motivation is the will to meaning. Meaning can be found through creative work, experiences and relationships, and the attitude one takes toward unavoidable suffering.
References: Corey (Theory & Practice) — Existential Therapy: Frankl and the search for meaning

256. nce-s-cou-008 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · existential therapy · medium · In review

Irvin Yalom described four "ultimate concerns," or givens of existence, that are central to existential psychotherapy. Which set lists them?
A. Anxiety, guilt, shame, and existential despair
These are emotional states that may arise from facing the givens, not the givens themselves.
B. Death, freedom, isolation, and meaninglessness ✔ KEY
Yalom identified death, freedom (and responsibility), existential isolation, and meaninglessness as the ultimate concerns.
C. Survival, belonging, power, freedom, and fun
These are Glasser's five basic needs from choice theory.
D. Safety, love, esteem, and self-actualization
These resemble levels of Maslow's hierarchy of needs.
Rationale: Yalom organized existential therapy around four givens: death, freedom, existential isolation, and meaninglessness. Anxiety arises when people confront these givens, and therapy helps clients face them authentically rather than through defensive avoidance.
References: Corey (Theory & Practice) — Existential Therapy: Yalom's ultimate concerns

257. nce-s-cou-009 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · gestalt therapy · easy · In review

A client grieving her late father says she never told him how angry she was about his drinking. The counselor invites her to imagine her father sitting in an empty chair and to speak to him directly, in the present tense. This intervention is MOST characteristic of:
A. Gestalt therapy ✔ KEY
The empty chair technique is a hallmark gestalt experiment for working through unfinished business in the here and now.
B. Rational emotive behavior therapy
REBT disputes irrational beliefs; it does not rely on enactment with an empty chair.
C. Reality therapy
Reality therapy focuses on current behavior and the WDEP system, not on enacted dialogues.
D. Solution-focused brief therapy
SFBT emphasizes exceptions and preferred futures rather than expressing unfinished feelings.
Rationale: Gestalt therapy, associated with Fritz and Laura Perls, uses experiments such as the empty chair to bring unfinished business into present awareness. Speaking directly to the imagined person helps the client experience and complete blocked feelings.
References: Corey (Theory & Practice) — Gestalt Therapy: unfinished business and the empty-chair technique

258. nce-s-cou-010 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · gestalt therapy · hard · In review

When a coworker criticizes him, a client reports that he wants to shout back but instead clenches his jaw and digs his fingernails into his own palms. In gestalt terms, this pattern of turning back onto oneself what one would like to do to others is called:
A. Introjection
Introjection is uncritically swallowing others' values or beliefs without assimilating them.
B. Confluence
Confluence is blurring the boundary between self and environment so that differences are not experienced.
C. Retroflection ✔ KEY
Retroflection is doing to oneself what one wants to do to someone else, such as turning anger inward.
D. Deflection
Deflection is avoiding contact through distraction, humor, or vagueness.
Rationale: Gestalt therapy describes several contact-boundary disturbances: introjection, projection, retroflection, deflection, and confluence. Retroflection turns an impulse meant for the environment back against the self, often seen in self-harm, muscle tension, or self-blame.
References: Corey (Theory & Practice) — Gestalt Therapy: contact and resistances to contact
☐ Approve☐ Revise☐ RetireNotes:

259. nce-s-cou-011 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · behavior therapy · medium · In review

Joseph Wolpe's systematic desensitization pairs deep relaxation with gradual imagined exposure to a fear hierarchy. The technique is based on which principle?
A. Operant extinction
Operant extinction removes reinforcement for a behavior; desensitization targets a conditioned anxiety response.
B. Reciprocal inhibition ✔ KEY
Wolpe reasoned that a response incompatible with anxiety, such as relaxation, inhibits anxiety when paired with the feared stimulus.
C. Negative reinforcement
Negative reinforcement increases behavior by removing an aversive stimulus; it is not the basis of the technique.
D. Vicarious learning
Vicarious learning through modeling comes from Bandura's social learning theory.
Rationale: Systematic desensitization is a classical-conditioning procedure. The client learns relaxation, builds an anxiety hierarchy, and then imagines each step while relaxed. Because relaxation and anxiety are incompatible, the relaxed state reciprocally inhibits the fear response.
References: Corey (Theory & Practice) — Behavior Therapy: systematic desensitization and reciprocal inhibition
☐ Approve☐ Revise☐ RetireNotes:

260. nce-s-cou-012 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · behavior therapy · medium · In review

To reduce a 7-year-old's hitting, his parents remove his tablet for the evening each time he hits his sister, and the hitting decreases. Which operant procedure are they using?
A. Positive reinforcement
Positive reinforcement adds a desirable stimulus to increase a behavior.
B. Negative reinforcement
Negative reinforcement removes an aversive stimulus to increase a behavior.
C. Positive punishment
Positive punishment adds an aversive stimulus, such as extra chores, to decrease a behavior.
D. Negative punishment ✔ KEY
Removing a desired item (the tablet) to decrease a behavior is negative punishment.
Rationale: In operant conditioning, "positive" means adding and "negative" means removing a stimulus; reinforcement increases behavior and punishment decreases it. Taking away a privilege to reduce hitting is therefore negative punishment (sometimes called response cost).
References: Corey (Theory & Practice) — Behavior Therapy: operant conditioning techniques
☐ Approve☐ Revise☐ RetireNotes:

261. nce-s-cou-013 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · cognitive behavioral therapy · easy · In review

Aaron Beck's cognitive triad of depression describes a negative view of which three areas?
A. The self, others, and the past
The triad concerns the future, not the past, and the world rather than others alone.
B. Thoughts, feelings, and behaviors
This is the general CBT model of interacting domains, not the cognitive triad.
C. The past, present, and future
The triad is not a time sequence; it includes the self and the world.
D. The self, the world, and the future ✔ KEY
Beck described depressed clients as viewing themselves, their world, and their future negatively.
Rationale: Beck proposed that depression is maintained by a negative cognitive triad: "I am inadequate" (self), "Everything goes wrong" (world), and "It will never get better" (future). Cognitive therapy helps clients test these appraisals against evidence.
References: Corey (Theory & Practice) — Cognitive Behavior Therapy: Beck's cognitive triad

262. nce-s-cou-014 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · cognitive behavioral therapy · medium · In review

A mother tells her counselor that her son's soccer team lost the championship because she had to miss the game for work. In Beck's cognitive therapy, this is an example of which cognitive distortion?
A. Magnification
Magnification exaggerates the importance of an event rather than wrongly taking responsibility for it.
B. Personalization ✔ KEY
She attributes an external event to herself without a basis for making the connection.
C. Dichotomous thinking
Dichotomous thinking sorts experiences into all-or-nothing categories.
D. Selective abstraction
Selective abstraction draws conclusions from one detail while ignoring the larger context.
Rationale: Personalization is the tendency to relate external events to oneself when there is no basis for doing so. Beck catalogued distortions such as arbitrary inference, selective abstraction, overgeneralization, magnification and minimization, personalization, and dichotomous thinking.
References: Corey (Theory & Practice) — Cognitive Behavior Therapy: Beck's systematic errors in reasoning

263. nce-s-cou-015 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · rational emotive behavior therapy · easy · In review

In Albert Ellis's ABCDE model of rational emotive behavior therapy, what does the "D" represent?
A. Describing the activating event
The activating event is the "A" in the model.
B. Developing new behaviors
New behaviors may follow, but "D" is a cognitive step, not behavior development.
C. Disputing irrational beliefs ✔ KEY
D is disputing, the active challenge of the irrational beliefs ("B") that cause disturbed consequences ("C").
D. Defining emotional consequences
Emotional and behavioral consequences are the "C" in the model.
Rationale: Ellis's model holds that an Activating event does not directly cause emotional Consequences; Beliefs about the event do. The counselor Disputes irrational beliefs, producing an Effective new philosophy (E) and healthier feelings.
References: Corey (Theory & Practice) — Rational Emotive Behavior Therapy: the A-B-C framework

264. nce-s-cou-016 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · rational emotive behavior therapy · hard · In review

A client says, "I must get this promotion. If I don't, it will be awful, I won't be able to stand it, and it will prove I'm worthless." According to Ellis, which element is the CORE of this irrational belief, from which the others derive?
A. Demandingness, or absolutistic "musts" ✔ KEY
Ellis held that rigid demands ("I must") are primary, and awfulizing, low frustration tolerance, and self-downing derive from them.
B. Catastrophic misinterpretation
Catastrophic misinterpretation of bodily sensations is a cognitive model of panic, not Ellis's core belief.
C. Internalized conditions of worth
Conditions of worth are a person-centered concept.
D. Introjected parental messages
Introjection is a gestalt contact disturbance, not the REBT core of irrationality.
Rationale: REBT identifies demandingness ("must," "should," "ought" held as absolutes) as the core of irrational thinking. Awfulizing, "I-can't-stand-it-itis" (low frustration tolerance), and global self-rating are derivatives that follow from rigid demands.
References: Corey (Theory & Practice) — Rational Emotive Behavior Therapy: musturbation and derivative beliefs
