265. nce-s-cou-017 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · reality therapy and choice theory · medium · In review

Reality therapy is often taught through the WDEP system (Wants, Doing and direction, Evaluation, Planning). Who developed and expanded this system?
A. Robert Wubbolding ✔ KEY
Wubbolding developed the WDEP system as a practical framework for applying Glasser's reality therapy.
B. Albert Ellis
Ellis founded REBT and the ABCDE model.
C. Steve de Shazer
De Shazer co-developed solution-focused brief therapy.
D. Michael White
White co-developed narrative therapy.
Rationale: William Glasser founded reality therapy and choice theory; Robert Wubbolding extended it with the WDEP system. Counselors explore what clients want, what they are doing, whether it is working, and a simple, attainable plan.
References: Corey (Theory & Practice) — Reality Therapy: the WDEP system

266. nce-s-cou-018 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · reality therapy and choice theory · medium · In review

A high school student says he skips class because "nobody there likes me anyway." After exploring what he wants and what he is currently doing, the reality therapist asks, "Is skipping class helping you get the friendships you want?" This question reflects which part of WDEP?
A. Wants
Wants were explored earlier; this question judges whether behavior serves those wants.
B. Doing and direction
Doing describes current behavior; it does not ask the client to judge its effectiveness.
C. Evaluation ✔ KEY
Asking whether current behavior is getting the client what he wants is self-evaluation, the core of reality therapy.
D. Planning
Planning comes after evaluation and creates a specific, attainable course of action.
Rationale: In Wubbolding's WDEP system, Evaluation asks clients to judge whether their total behavior is meeting their needs and wants. Glasser's five basic needs are survival, love and belonging, power, freedom, and fun; here the student is pursuing belonging ineffectively.
References: Corey (Theory & Practice) — Reality Therapy: self-evaluation in the WDEP system

267. nce-s-cou-019 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · solution-focused brief therapy · easy · In review

A counselor asks a client, "Suppose that tonight, while you sleep, the problem that brought you here is solved. When you wake up, what will be the first thing you notice that tells you things are different?" This is an example of:
A. An exception question
Exception questions ask about past times the problem was absent or less severe.
B. The miracle question ✔ KEY
Imagining the problem gone overnight and describing the difference is the classic miracle question.
C. A scaling question
Scaling questions ask clients to rate progress or confidence on a numerical scale.
D. A coping question
Coping questions ask how the client has managed to keep going despite difficulties.
Rationale: The miracle question, developed within solution-focused brief therapy by Steve de Shazer and Insoo Kim Berg, invites clients to describe a preferred future in concrete, observable terms. Those details then become the basis for goals and small next steps.
References: Corey (Theory & Practice) — Postmodern Approaches: solution-focused brief therapy, the miracle question

268. nce-s-cou-020 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · solution-focused brief therapy · medium · In review

A counselor says, "On a scale from 0 to 10, where 10 means the problem is fully resolved, you said you are at a 4. What would tell you that you had moved to a 5?" This technique and the approach it comes from are MOST closely associated with:
A. Michael White and David Epston
White and Epston developed narrative therapy, known for externalizing conversations.
B. Jay Haley and Cloé Madanes
Haley and Madanes are associated with strategic family therapy and directives.
C. William Miller and Stephen Rollnick
Miller and Rollnick developed motivational interviewing; MI uses importance rulers, but this framing is SFBT scaling.
D. Steve de Shazer and Insoo Kim Berg ✔ KEY
Scaling questions focused on small next steps are a signature solution-focused technique from de Shazer and Berg.
Rationale: Solution-focused brief therapy, developed by Steve de Shazer and Insoo Kim Berg, uses scaling questions to make progress concrete and to identify the next small, observable step. The focus is on what is already working rather than on the problem's history.
References: Corey (Theory & Practice) — Postmodern Approaches: solution-focused brief therapy, scaling questions

269. nce-s-cou-021 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · narrative therapy · easy · In review

A counselor working with a 10-year-old who refuses to go to school asks, "When does the Worry show up, and how does it try to convince you to stay home?" The counselor is using which technique?
A. Reframing
Reframing offers a new meaning for a behavior; it does not separate the problem from the person.
B. Paradoxical intention
Paradoxical intention asks the client to deliberately do or wish for the feared behavior.
C. Externalizing the problem ✔ KEY
Naming the Worry as a separate entity that acts on the child externalizes the problem.
D. Cognitive restructuring
Cognitive restructuring identifies and replaces distorted thoughts rather than personifying the problem.
Rationale: Narrative therapy, developed by Michael White and David Epston, holds that "the person is not the problem; the problem is the problem." Externalizing conversations separate clients from problem-saturated identities so they can examine and resist the problem's influence.
References: Corey (Theory & Practice) — Postmodern Approaches: narrative therapy, externalizing conversations

270. nce-s-cou-022 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · narrative therapy · hard · In review

A narrative counselor learns that a client who describes himself as "a hopeless addict" turned down a drink at a family wedding last month. The counselor explores this event in detail to begin building an alternative story. In narrative therapy, such events are called:
A. Unique outcomes ✔ KEY
White used "unique outcomes" for moments that contradict the dominant problem story and seed re-authoring.
B. Exceptions
"Exceptions" is the solution-focused term for times the problem is absent; the question asks for narrative terminology.
C. Peak experiences
Peak experiences are Maslow's term for moments of intense fulfillment.
D. Corrective emotional experiences
This psychodynamic term refers to re-experiencing old conflicts with a new, healthier outcome in therapy.
Rationale: Narrative therapists listen for unique outcomes: times when the client acted outside the problem-saturated story. Thickening these accounts helps re-author an alternative, preferred identity story.

Which statement BEST reflects a central principle of feminist therapy?
A. Distress mainly reflects unresolved intrapsychic conflict
Feminist therapy looks beyond intrapsychic causes to social, political, and cultural context.
B. The personal is political, so context shapes distress ✔ KEY
"The personal is political" holds that individual problems are linked to social structures and oppression.
C. The counselor holds expert authority to direct the client
Feminist therapy emphasizes an egalitarian relationship that minimizes power differences.
D. A diagnosis is required before exploring social context
Feminist therapists are cautious about diagnostic labels and prioritize the client's context.

A. Lifestyle assessment
Lifestyle assessment is an Adlerian technique exploring family constellation and early recollections.
B. Genogram construction
A genogram maps multigenerational family patterns, a Bowenian tool.
C. Cognitive restructuring
Cognitive restructuring targets distorted thoughts without centering gender socialization.
D. Gender-role analysis ✔ KEY
Gender-role analysis examines the impact of gender-role expectations on a client's well-being.

B. Summaries ✔ KEY
Summaries collect and link what the client has said, often highlighting change talk.
C. Silence
Silence can be useful, but it is not part of the OARS acronym.
D. Self-disclosure
Self-disclosure is not one of the OARS core skills.

274. nce-s-cou-026 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · motivational interviewing · hard · In review

A client says, "I know I should cut back on drinking, but it's the only thing that helps me relax after work." Which counselor response is MOST consistent with motivational interviewing?
A. "You need to understand that drinking is harming your health."
Unsolicited warnings reflect the righting reflex and tend to elicit sustain talk.
B. "Why haven't you been able to cut back on your drinking yet?"
Asking why the client has not changed invites defense of the status quo.
C. "It sounds like you are in denial about how serious this is."
Labeling and confronting denial increases discord, which MI avoids.
D. "It helps you unwind, and you also see reasons to cut back." ✔ KEY
A double-sided reflection acknowledges both sides of ambivalence and ends on the change side.
Rationale: MI treats ambivalence as normal. A double-sided reflection captures both sustain talk and change talk, typically ending with the change side, so the client hears his own reasons for change without feeling pushed. Confrontation and advice-giving tend to strengthen sustain talk.
References: Miller & Rollnick (MI) — Responding to sustain talk and discord: double-sided reflections

275. nce-s-cou-027 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · dialectical behavior therapy · medium · In review

A DBT skills group is teaching TIPP skills for crisis survival and practicing radical acceptance of painful realities that cannot be changed right now. These skills belong to which DBT module?
A. Distress tolerance ✔ KEY
Crisis survival skills such as TIPP and reality-acceptance skills such as radical acceptance are taught in distress tolerance.
B. Emotion regulation
Emotion regulation focuses on understanding emotions, reducing vulnerability, and changing unwanted emotions.
C. Interpersonal effectiveness
Interpersonal effectiveness teaches asking for what one needs and saying no (for example, DEAR MAN).
D. Core mindfulness
Mindfulness teaches observing, describing, and participating nonjudgmentally (wise mind).
Rationale: Marsha Linehan's DBT skills training has four modules: mindfulness, distress tolerance, emotion regulation, and interpersonal effectiveness. Distress tolerance helps clients survive crises without making things worse and accept reality as it is.
References: Corey (Theory & Practice) — Behavior Therapy: dialectical behavior therapy skills modules

276. nce-s-cou-028 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · dialectical behavior therapy · easy · In review

Marsha Linehan developed dialectical behavior therapy initially for chronically suicidal clients. The central dialectic of the approach is the balance between:
A. Insight and catharsis
Insight and catharsis are associated with psychodynamic and experiential approaches.
B. Structure and spontaneity
This is not the organizing dialectic of DBT.
C. Acceptance and change ✔ KEY
DBT balances validation and acceptance of the client with behavioral change strategies.
D. Thinking and feeling
DBT integrates reason and emotion in "wise mind," but its central dialectic is acceptance and change.
Rationale: DBT integrates cognitive-behavioral change strategies with acceptance practices drawn from Zen and mindfulness. Linehan developed it for chronically suicidal clients, many meeting criteria for borderline personality disorder, who experienced pure change-focused therapy as invalidating.

277. nce-s-cou-029 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · family systems theories (Bowen, structural, strategic) · medium · In review

Two parents in a tense marriage each confide in their 15-year-old daughter about their frustrations with the other, and the daughter now feels responsible for keeping the peace. A Bowenian family counselor would MOST likely describe this pattern as:
A. Triangulation ✔ KEY
Bowen described how anxious two-person systems draw in a third person to stabilize tension, forming a triangle.
B. Emotional cutoff
Emotional cutoff is managing unresolved fusion by distancing from family, the opposite of being drawn in.
C. Sibling position
Sibling position concerns how birth order shapes functioning, not parental conflict.
D. Societal regression
Societal regression applies Bowen's concepts to society as a whole.
Rationale: In Bowen family systems theory, the triangle is the smallest stable relationship unit; dyads under stress pull in a third person. Bowenian counselors help members increase differentiation of self, often using genograms to trace multigenerational patterns, so they can stay connected without being triangled.
References: Corey (Theory & Practice) — Family Systems Therapy: Bowen's triangulation and differentiation of self

278. nce-s-cou-030 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · family systems theories (Bowen, structural, strategic) · hard · In review

Parents tell a family counselor they always agree on rules. The counselor asks them to decide, right now in session, whether their son may stay out late Saturday, then watches as the son interrupts and his mother sides with him. In Minuchin's structural family therapy, this technique is called:
A. Prescribing the symptom
Prescribing the symptom is a paradoxical directive from strategic therapy (Haley, Madanes).
B. Enactment ✔ KEY
Enactment has the family act out a transaction in session so the counselor can observe and modify its structure.
C. Detriangulation
Detriangulation is a Bowenian goal of removing a third person from a two-person conflict.
D. Unbalancing
Unbalancing is a structural technique in which the counselor temporarily sides with one member to shift power; that is not what is described.
Rationale: Minuchin's structural family therapy focuses on subsystems, boundaries, and hierarchy. In an enactment, the counselor has family members interact in session rather than report on their interactions, revealing patterns such as a cross-generational coalition that the counselor can then help restructure.
References: Corey (Theory & Practice) — Family Systems Therapy: structural family therapy, enactment

279. nce-s-cou-031 · Group Counseling and Group Work · group stages and dynamics · medium · In review

In the third session of a community anger-management group, members begin arguing about the attendance rule, and two members openly question whether the leader is qualified to run the group. According to Tuckman's model, the group is MOST likely in which stage?
A. Forming
Forming is the orientation stage of politeness, dependence on the leader and tentative participation, before open conflict emerges.
B. Storming ✔ KEY
Storming is marked by conflict, challenges to the leader and struggles over control and rules as members test the group.
C. Norming
Norming follows storming; members have settled conflicts, agreed on norms and developed cohesion.
D. Performing
Performing is the productive stage in which the group works on its goals with little energy spent on conflict.
Rationale: Tuckman (1965; adjourning added with Jensen in 1977) described forming, storming, norming, performing and adjourning. Conflict over rules and direct challenges to the leader's authority are the hallmark of storming, which leaders should treat as a normal developmental step rather than a sign the group is failing.
References: Gladding (Counseling: A Comprehensive Profession) — Group work: Tuckman stages of group development

280. nce-s-cou-032 · Group Counseling and Group Work · group stages and dynamics · hard · In review

During the transition stage of a counseling group, a member crosses his arms and says to the leader, 'Honestly, this group is a waste of my time.' Other members fall silent. Which leader response BEST fits Corey's guidance for this stage?
A. Remind him that the group rules ask members to stay positive and to avoid discouraging the others in the room.
Invoking a rule to suppress the comment shuts down a challenge the group needs to work through and models avoidance of conflict.
B. Move to a structured exercise so the silence ends quickly and the group can return to the planned topic of the day.
Changing the subject bypasses the conflict; unaddressed resistance in the transition stage tends to go underground and erode trust.
C. Ask the other members whether they agree with him so that the group can vote on whether to keep meeting each week.
Polling members turns a personal reaction into a referendum, can isolate the member and puts the group itself on trial.
D. Respond without defensiveness and invite him to say more about what isn't working for him in the group so far. ✔ KEY
Corey advises leaders to meet challenges openly and non-defensively, treating resistance as useful information and modeling direct, respectful handling of conflict.
Rationale: In Corey's model the transition stage is characterized by anxiety, defensiveness, resistance and struggles for control, including challenges to the leader. The leader's task is to recognize and explore these reactions directly and non-defensively, which builds the trust needed to move into the working stage.
References: Corey (Theory & Practice) — Group stages: transition stage, dealing with resistance and challenges to the leader

281. nce-s-cou-033 · Group Counseling and Group Work · group stages and dynamics · easy · In review

A high school counselor plans a six-week group in which students learn and practice specific study and test-anxiety management skills, with brief lessons and structured activities each week. Under the ASGW classification of group work, this is BEST described as which type of group?
A. Psychoeducation group ✔ KEY
Psychoeducation groups emphasize teaching information and skills, often to prevent problems, using structured content and activities.
B. Task group
Task and work groups focus on accomplishing an organizational goal, such as a committee or planning team, rather than member learning.
C. Counseling group
Counseling groups address interpersonal and developmental concerns through member interaction, with less didactic structure.
D. Psychotherapy group
Psychotherapy groups address more severe or chronic problems and personality reconstruction, which this skills group does not target.
Rationale: The ASGW identifies four group types: task/work, psychoeducation, counseling and psychotherapy. A time-limited, structured group whose main aim is teaching skills to a nonclinical population is a psychoeducation group.

282. nce-s-cou-034 · Group Counseling and Group Work · group leadership skills and therapeutic factors · easy · In review

After a member of a grief group shares that she still sets a place at the table for her late husband, three other members say they have done something similar. She replies, 'I thought I was the only one.' Which of Yalom's therapeutic factors is MOST evident?
A. Instillation of hope
Instillation of hope comes from seeing others improve or cope well, not from discovering that others share the same experience.
B. Altruism
Altruism is the benefit of helping others; the member here is the one receiving relief rather than giving help.
C. Universality ✔ KEY
Universality is the relief of learning that one is not alone or unique in one's problems, thoughts or feelings.
D. Catharsis
Catharsis is the release of strong emotion; the key experience described is recognizing shared experience.
Rationale: Yalom identified 11 therapeutic factors. Universality, the discovery that others share one's 'secret' struggles, often appears early in groups and reduces shame and isolation, which is why the member's statement that she thought she was the only one points to it.
References: Yalom & Leszcz (Group Psychotherapy) — Ch. 1: The therapeutic factors, universality

283. nce-s-cou-035 · Group Counseling and Group Work · group leadership skills and therapeutic factors · medium · In review

In a young adult counseling group, two members repeatedly press a quieter member to 'finally tell us what really happened' with her family, even after she says she is not ready. Which leadership skill is MOST needed at this moment?
A. Linking, by connecting her reluctance to similar feelings that others in the group have described
Linking builds connection among members but does not stop the pressure being placed on her right now.
B. Drawing out, by inviting the quiet member to share a little more so the others feel heard
Drawing her out would join the pressure; she has already stated a boundary about disclosure.
C. Blocking, by stopping the pressure and affirming her right to decide what and when to share ✔ KEY
Blocking protects members from counterproductive behavior such as coercive questioning while respecting their autonomy over disclosure.
D. Summarizing, by restating the main points of the discussion before moving to another member
Summarizing may move the group along but does not address the coercion or protect the member.
Rationale: Group leaders have a duty to protect members from undue pressure and from behaviors that threaten safety. Blocking intervenes in harmful patterns, such as pressuring, gossiping or breaking confidences, without attacking the members doing it.
References: Corey (Theory & Practice) — Group leadership skills: blocking and protecting members · ACA Code of Ethics — A.9.b Protecting Clients (group work)

284. nce-s-cou-036 · Group Counseling and Group Work · group leadership skills and therapeutic factors · hard · In review

In a long-term interpersonal group, a man reacts to the male co-leader with the same deference and hidden resentment he felt toward his father, and competes with an older member as he did with his brother. Over months he recognizes the pattern and responds to them differently. Which of Yalom's therapeutic factors does this process BEST illustrate?
A. Corrective recapitulation of the primary family group ✔ KEY
The group re-creates early family dynamics, and the member relives and then reworks those patterns in a new, corrective way.
B. Imitative behavior modeled on the co-leader and members
Imitative behavior means adopting behaviors seen in others; the core change here is reworking old family-based roles.
C. Socializing techniques learned through group role-plays
Socializing techniques are basic social skills learned in the group, not the reworking of family-of-origin patterns.
D. Existential factors, such as accepting final responsibility
Existential factors involve accepting responsibility, mortality and life's unfairness, not reliving family roles.
Rationale: Yalom describes groups as resembling a family in many ways, so members often relate to leaders and peers as they did to parents and siblings. When those patterns are not merely relived but explored and corrected, the factor is corrective recapitulation of the primary family group.
References: Yalom & Leszcz (Group Psychotherapy) — Ch. 1: Corrective recapitulation of the primary family group

285. nce-s-cou-037 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · basic attending and listening skills · easy · In review

In Egan's SOLER guideline for nonverbal attending, what does the letter 'L' stand for?
A. Listen without interrupting
Uninterrupted listening matters, but SOLER describes physical posture, and L is not listening.
B. Look at the client's face
Eye contact is the E in SOLER, not the L.
C. Limit note-taking in session
Note-taking is not one of the SOLER behaviors.
D. Lean slightly toward the client ✔ KEY
L stands for leaning toward the client, a posture that signals involvement and interest.
Rationale: SOLER stands for Squarely face the client, Open posture, Lean toward the client, Eye contact and Relaxed manner. Egan presents these as flexible guidelines; the counselor adapts them to the client's culture and comfort, since direct eye contact or close posture is not welcome in every context.
References: Gladding (Counseling: A Comprehensive Profession) — Building the counseling relationship: attending behavior (SOLER)

286. nce-s-cou-038 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · basic attending and listening skills · medium · In review

A practicum student is eager to start using confrontation and interpretation with clients. According to Ivey's microskills hierarchy, which skills should the student master FIRST?
A. Reflection of meaning and the interpretation of core themes
Reflection of meaning and interpretation sit higher in the hierarchy and rely on the listening foundation.
B. Attending behavior and the basic listening sequence ✔ KEY
Attending and the basic listening sequence are the foundation on which higher-level influencing skills are built.
C. Influencing skills such as directives and feedback
Influencing skills are later in the hierarchy and are effective only after the client feels heard.
D. Skill integration and a personal style of counseling
Skill integration and personal style form the top of the hierarchy, reached after the other skills are mastered.
Rationale: Ivey's hierarchy rests on ethics, multicultural competence and wellness, then attending behavior, then the basic listening sequence (questions, observation, encouraging, paraphrasing, summarizing, reflecting feeling). Focusing, empathic confrontation, reflection of meaning, influencing skills and skill integration build on that base.

287. nce-s-cou-039 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · reflection, paraphrasing and summarizing · easy · In review

A client says, 'My supervisor criticized my report in front of the whole team again, and I just sat there and said nothing.' Which counselor response is the BEST example of a reflection of feeling?
A. You felt embarrassed, and maybe angry with yourself for not speaking up. ✔ KEY
This names the likely emotions behind the statement, which is the defining feature of reflecting feeling.
B. Your supervisor criticized your report in front of the team once again.
This restates the content of the message, which makes it a paraphrase rather than a reflection of feeling.
C. What do you think stopped you from saying something to your supervisor?
This is an open question that explores reasons; it does not reflect the emotion being expressed.
D. Next time, it might help to ask your supervisor to talk with you privately.
This is advice-giving, an influencing response that skips past the client's feelings.
Rationale: A reflection of feeling identifies and names the emotion in the client's message, often tentatively, so the client feels understood and can explore it further. A paraphrase captures content; questions and advice are different skills.
References: Gladding (Counseling: A Comprehensive Profession) — Counseling skills: reflection of feeling versus paraphrasing

288. nce-s-cou-040 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · reflection, paraphrasing and summarizing · medium · In review

Midway through a session, a client has moved quickly among worries about money, conflict with her sister and trouble sleeping. The counselor wants to pull these threads together, check accuracy and help the client choose a focus. Which skill is MOST suited to this purpose?
A. Paraphrasing
Paraphrasing restates the content of a single statement rather than tying together several themes across the session.
B. Reflection of meaning
Reflection of meaning explores values and deeper significance, not the organization of several topics into a focus.
C. Summarizing ✔ KEY
Summarizing condenses several statements or themes, checks understanding and helps set direction or transition.
D. Encouraging
Minimal encouragers keep the client talking; they do not integrate content across topics.
Rationale: Summarizing draws together the main content and feelings from a longer stretch of talk. It is used to begin or end sessions, to make transitions and to help a client who has raised many concerns decide what to work on.
References: Gladding (Counseling: A Comprehensive Profession) — Counseling skills: summarizing

289. nce-s-cou-041 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · confrontation and immediacy · medium · In review

A client who is tearful and twisting a tissue says, 'I'm completely fine with the divorce. It's no big deal.' Which response BEST illustrates empathic confrontation?
A. You aren't being honest with me or yourself, because you are clearly upset right now.
This accuses and judges the client, which is likely to raise defensiveness rather than invite exploration.
B. Divorce is one of the most stressful life events, so being upset would be quite normal.
This normalizes and reassures, but it does not name the discrepancy between the words and the behavior.
C. Why do you think you are telling me it is no big deal when it seems to matter a lot?
"Why" questions tend to put clients on the defensive and ask for justification rather than reflection.
D. You're saying the divorce is no big deal, and you're also in tears as we talk about it. ✔ KEY
This nonjudgmentally places the verbal message beside the nonverbal one and lets the client explore the mixed message.
Rationale: Empathic confrontation identifies discrepancies, mixed messages or conflicts (often with an "on the one hand ... and on the other" format) in a supportive, nonjudgmental way. Its aim is to help the client notice and resolve the incongruity, not to win an argument.
References: Gladding (Counseling: A Comprehensive Profession) — Counseling skills: confrontation of discrepancies

290. nce-s-cou-042 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · confrontation and immediacy · medium · In review

A counselor says, 'I notice that whenever we get close to talking about your father, you change the subject and glance at me as if checking my reaction. I'm wondering what is happening between us right now.' This statement is BEST described as:
A. Self-disclosure
Self-disclosure shares information about the counselor's own life; here the focus is the current relationship.
B. Immediacy ✔ KEY
Immediacy addresses what is occurring in the here-and-now between counselor and client.
C. Interpretation
Interpretation offers a new frame for the client's behavior; this response invites discussion of the present interaction instead.
D. Reframing
Reframing recasts a situation in a different light; it does not focus on the counselor-client relationship.
Rationale: Immediacy is the counselor's direct, present-tense comment on the counseling relationship or on a pattern unfolding in the session. It is useful when trust, tension, dependence or avoidance in the relationship is blocking progress.
References: Gladding (Counseling: A Comprehensive Profession) — Counseling skills: immediacy

291. nce-s-cou-043 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · play therapy · medium · In review

In a child-centered play therapy session, a 6-year-old grows frustrated and raises a wooden block as if to throw it at the counselor. Using Landreth's ACT model of limit setting, which response is BEST?
A. If you throw that block, our playtime is over for today and we will go back out.
Leading with a consequence skips acknowledging the feeling and offering an alternative; consequences come only if limits are repeatedly broken.
B. We don't throw blocks in here. Can you please put it back on the shelf for me?
This states a limit but ignores the child's feeling and gives no acceptable outlet for it.
C. You're really mad. I'm not for throwing. You can throw the soft ball at the wall. ✔ KEY
This acknowledges the feeling, communicates the limit and targets an acceptable alternative, the three steps of ACT.
D. It sounds like you are feeling frustrated with how the game is going for you today.
Reflecting the feeling alone leaves the counselor and child unprotected; a limit is needed when safety is at stake.
Rationale: Landreth's ACT model: Acknowledge the child's feeling, Communicate the limit and Target an acceptable alternative. Limits in child-centered play therapy are few, are stated calmly and briefly, and protect physical safety and the relationship while leaving the feeling itself accepted.
References: Gladding (Counseling: A Comprehensive Profession) — Counseling children: child-centered play therapy and limit setting
⚑ Check: The key's wording is in Landreth's style, not a direct quote.

292. nce-s-cou-044 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · play therapy · hard · In review

A play therapist spends early sessions building an egalitarian relationship with a 9-year-old, then gathers information about the child's lifestyle and mistaken beliefs, helps the child gain insight, and finally uses reorientation to practice new ways of belonging. This sequence is characteristic of which approach?
A. Adlerian play therapy ✔ KEY
Kottman's Adlerian play therapy proceeds through relationship building, exploring lifestyle, gaining insight and reorientation/reeducation.
B. Child-centered play therapy
Child-centered play therapy is nondirective and does not assess lifestyle or work toward planned reeducation phases.
C. Theraplay
Theraplay is a directive, attachment-based approach involving caregivers in structured engagement and nurture activities.
D. Filial therapy
Filial therapy trains parents to conduct child-centered play sessions; it is not organized around lifestyle analysis.
Rationale: Terry Kottman's Adlerian play therapy integrates Adlerian concepts (lifestyle, mistaken beliefs, the Crucial Cs, encouragement) with play. Its four phases are building an egalitarian relationship, exploring the child's lifestyle, helping the child gain insight and reorientation/reeducation.

293. nce-s-cou-045 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · crisis intervention models · medium · In review

A college student arrives at the counseling center hours after learning his scholarship was revoked, saying he 'can't see a way forward.' Following Roberts' seven-stage crisis intervention model, what should the counselor do FIRST?
A. Help him generate options for funding and plan his next steps with financial aid
Generating and exploring alternatives is stage 5, after safety, rapport, problems and feelings have been addressed.
B. Conduct a thorough psychosocial and lethality assessment, including suicide risk ✔ KEY
Stage 1 of Roberts' model is a biopsychosocial and lethality assessment; his hopeless statement makes this urgent.
C. Explore and validate his feelings of shock, anger and shame about the revocation
Dealing with feelings and emotions is stage 4 of the model, not the first step.
D. Identify the main problems and the precipitating event that led him to come in today
Identifying major problems and precipitants is stage 3, after assessment and rapport building.
Rationale: Roberts' seven stages are: (1) plan and conduct a thorough biopsychosocial and lethality assessment, (2) establish rapport, (3) identify major problems and precipitants, (4) deal with feelings and emotions, (5) generate and explore alternatives, (6) implement an action plan and (7) follow up. Stages can overlap, but safety assessment comes first.
References: Gladding (Counseling: A Comprehensive Profession) — Crisis counseling: Roberts' seven-stage crisis intervention model

294. nce-s-cou-046 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · crisis intervention models · easy · In review

A family is seen by a crisis counselor two days after their apartment was destroyed in a fire. Which goal BEST reflects the primary aim of crisis intervention?
A. Uncover early losses that make the family vulnerable to grief and resolve them
Exploring early history is a goal of longer-term therapy and can overwhelm people in acute crisis.
B. Restructure family roles and boundaries that may have contributed to stress
Structural change in family organization is a family therapy goal, not the immediate aim of crisis work.
C. Complete a diagnostic evaluation of each member to begin long-term treatment
Crisis intervention is brief and focused on coping; a crisis reaction is not by itself a disorder requiring diagnosis.
D. Restore the family to at least its pre-crisis level of coping and functioning ✔ KEY
Crisis intervention seeks to stabilize people and restore equilibrium, at least to the pre-crisis level of functioning.
Rationale: A crisis is a time-limited state in which usual coping is overwhelmed. Crisis intervention is brief, active and present-focused, aiming to ensure safety, mobilize resources and coping and return people to at least their pre-crisis level of functioning; deeper personality or system change is left for later work if needed.
References: Gladding (Counseling: A Comprehensive Profession) — Crisis counseling: goals of crisis intervention

295. nce-s-cou-047 · Career Development · career development theories (Holland, Super, Krumboltz) · medium · In review

On Holland's RIASEC hexagon, which pair of personality types is MOST consistent, meaning the two types share the most characteristics?
A. Realistic and Investigative ✔ KEY
Realistic and Investigative are adjacent on the hexagon, so they share the most traits and form a consistent pair.
B. Realistic and Social
Realistic and Social sit opposite each other on the hexagon and are the least consistent pairing.
C. Artistic and Conventional
Artistic and Conventional are opposite types, one valuing self-expression and the other order and structure.
D. Investigative and Enterprising
Investigative and Enterprising are opposite each other on the hexagon, so they are inconsistent.
Rationale: Holland arranged the six types in the order R-I-A-S-E-C around a hexagon. Adjacent types are most consistent, alternate types are intermediate and opposite types (R-S, I-E, A-C) are least consistent. Holland's related ideas include congruence (fit between person and environment) and differentiation.
References: Sharf (Career Development Theory) — Holland's theory of types: the hexagon and consistency

296. nce-s-cou-048 · Career Development · career development theories (Holland, Super, Krumboltz) · medium · In review

A 52-year-old nurse manager tells a counselor that her main concerns are holding on to the position she has built, keeping her clinical skills current and innovating within her role. In Super's life-span, life-space theory, these concerns BEST fit which career stage?
A. Establishment
Establishment (about ages 25 to 44) centers on stabilizing in and advancing within a chosen field.
B. Exploration
Exploration (about ages 15 to 24) involves crystallizing, specifying and implementing an occupational choice.
C. Maintenance ✔ KEY
Maintenance (about ages 45 to 64) involves holding, updating and innovating to preserve one's position.
D. Disengagement
Disengagement (about 65 and older) involves decelerating, retirement planning and retirement living.
Rationale: Super's stages are growth, exploration, establishment, maintenance and disengagement, each with developmental tasks; the maintenance tasks are holding, updating and innovating. Super treated the stage ages as approximate, and he stressed that people recycle through stages and that work is one of several life roles shown in the life-career rainbow.
References: Sharf (Career Development Theory) — Super's life-span, life-space theory: maintenance stage tasks

297. nce-s-cou-049 · Career Development · career development theories (Holland, Super, Krumboltz) · medium · In review

A client is anxious because she has no clear five-year career plan. The counselor helps her see that past opportunities arose from unexpected events, and encourages curiosity, persistence, flexibility, optimism and risk taking to create and use future chance events. This approach reflects:
A. Theory of work adjustment
The theory of work adjustment (Dawis and Lofquist) addresses correspondence between worker needs and job reinforcers.
B. Planned happenstance theory ✔ KEY
Planned happenstance (Mitchell, Levin and Krumboltz) teaches clients to create and capitalize on unplanned events.
C. Circumscription and compromise
Gottfredson's theory explains how children narrow options by sex type and prestige, not how to use chance events.
D. Cognitive information processing
CIP (Sampson, Peterson and colleagues) focuses on the pyramid of information processing and the CASVE decision cycle.
Rationale: Planned happenstance grew out of Krumboltz's social learning theory of career decision making. It reframes indecision as open-mindedness and builds five skills (curiosity, persistence, flexibility, optimism and risk taking) so that clients can turn unplanned events into career opportunities.
References: Sharf (Career Development Theory) — Krumboltz's social learning theory and planned happenstance

298. nce-s-cou-050 · Human Growth and Development · human development theories (Erikson, Piaget, Kohlberg) · easy · In review

A 4-year-old eagerly plans games and starts projects, but his parents often scold him for 'making messes' when he tries new activities. In Erikson's theory, this child is working on which psychosocial crisis?
A. Autonomy vs. shame and doubt
Autonomy vs. shame and doubt is the toddler stage (about 1 to 3 years), centered on self-control and independence.
B. Industry vs. inferiority
Industry vs. inferiority is the school-age stage (about 6 to 12 years), centered on competence and productivity.
C. Trust vs. mistrust
Trust vs. mistrust is the infancy stage (birth to about 1 year), centered on reliable caregiving.
D. Initiative vs. guilt ✔ KEY
Initiative vs. guilt (about 3 to 6 years) involves planning and starting activities; harsh criticism can foster guilt.
Rationale: Erikson's eight stages are trust vs. mistrust (infancy), autonomy vs. shame and doubt (toddlerhood), initiative vs. guilt (preschool), industry vs. inferiority (school age), identity vs. role confusion (adolescence), intimacy vs. isolation (young adulthood), generativity vs. stagnation (middle adulthood) and integrity vs. despair (late adulthood).

299. nce-s-cou-051 · Human Growth and Development · human development theories (Erikson, Piaget, Kohlberg) · easy · In review

An 8-year-old correctly explains that a ball of clay has the same amount of clay after it is rolled into a long snake, but she struggles with a purely hypothetical question about what would happen if people had no need for sleep. In Piaget's theory, she is MOST likely in which stage?
A. Concrete operational ✔ KEY
The concrete operational child (about 7 to 11) masters conservation and logical operations on concrete objects but not abstract hypotheticals.
B. Preoperational
Preoperational children (about 2 to 7) typically fail conservation tasks because of centration.
C. Formal operational
Formal operational thinkers (about 11 and older) can reason about abstract and hypothetical propositions, which she cannot yet do.
D. Sensorimotor
The sensorimotor stage (birth to about 2) involves learning through senses and actions, before symbolic thought.
Rationale: Piaget's four stages are sensorimotor (birth to 2), preoperational (2 to 7), concrete operational (7 to 11) and formal operational (11 and up). Conservation marks concrete operations; abstract, hypothetico-deductive reasoning marks formal operations.
References: Berk (Development Through the Lifespan) — Piaget's cognitive-developmental theory: concrete operational stage

300. nce-s-cou-052 · Human Growth and Development · human development theories (Erikson, Piaget, Kohlberg) · medium · In review

Asked whether a man should steal an unaffordable medicine to save his wife, a 16-year-old answers, 'No, because if everyone broke the law when they felt like it, society would fall apart.' This reasoning BEST fits which Kohlberg stage?
A. Stage 2: Instrumental exchange
Stage 2 (preconventional) reasoning focuses on self-interest and fair deals, not on maintaining social order.
B. Stage 3: Interpersonal conformity
Stage 3 reasoning centers on being seen as good and winning the approval of others.
C. Stage 4: Law and order orientation ✔ KEY
Stage 4 (conventional) reasoning upholds laws and duties because they maintain the social system.
D. Stage 5: Social contract
Stage 5 (postconventional) sees laws as changeable agreements that can be set aside to protect basic rights such as life.
Rationale: Kohlberg described three levels with two stages each: preconventional (1 punishment and obedience, 2 instrumental purpose), conventional (3 interpersonal conformity, 4 social-order maintenance or law and order) and postconventional (5 social contract, 6 universal ethical principles). Stage is judged by the reasoning given, not by the yes-or-no answer.
References: Berk (Development Through the Lifespan) — Kohlberg's theory of moral development: conventional level

301. nce-s-cou-053 · Human Growth and Development · attachment theory · medium · In review

In the Strange Situation, a 14-month-old stays close to her mother and explores little. She becomes very distressed when her mother leaves, and on reunion she clings to her mother while also pushing away and crying angrily. Which attachment classification BEST fits?
A. Secure
Securely attached infants explore using the parent as a base and are readily comforted on reunion.
B. Anxious-ambivalent (resistant) ✔ KEY
Resistant infants explore little, are highly distressed at separation and both seek and resist contact on reunion.
C. Avoidant
Avoidant infants show little distress at separation and ignore or avoid the parent on reunion.
D. Disorganized/disoriented
Disorganized infants show contradictory, confused behaviors such as freezing or approaching with the head averted.
Rationale: Ainsworth's Strange Situation identified secure, avoidant and anxious-ambivalent (resistant) patterns; Main and Solomon later added disorganized/disoriented. Mixing angry resistance with contact seeking on reunion is the signature of the resistant pattern, often linked to inconsistent caregiving.
References: Berk (Development Through the Lifespan) — Attachment: Ainsworth Strange Situation classifications

302. nce-s-cou-054 · Human Growth and Development · attachment theory · hard · In review

A counselor reviewing an infant assessment reads that, on reunion with a parent, the 12-month-old approached with his face turned away, then froze with a dazed expression. Which statement about this pattern is MOST accurate?
A. It is Ainsworth's original avoidant category, linked mainly to warm but busy caregivers
Avoidant infants show organized avoidance, and the pattern is linked to rejecting, not warm-but-busy, caregiving.
B. It reflects a secure pattern in a temperamentally shy infant who needs time to warm up
Freezing and approach-avoidance are not features of secure attachment, regardless of temperament.
C. It is the resistant pattern, defined by clinging mixed with anger toward the caregiver
Resistant infants show organized ambivalence; the freezing and averted approach described here is different.
D. It fits the disorganized category added by Main and Solomon, tied to frightening care ✔ KEY
Disorganized attachment involves contradictory or dazed behavior and is associated with frightened or frightening caregiving.
Rationale: Main and Solomon identified disorganized/disoriented attachment in infants whose behavior did not fit Ainsworth's three organized patterns. The infant lacks a coherent strategy, often because the caregiver is a source of both comfort and fear, as in maltreatment or unresolved parental trauma.
References: Berk (Development Through the Lifespan) — Attachment: disorganized/disoriented attachment (Main & Solomon)

303. nce-s-cou-055 · Social and Cultural Diversity · multicultural counseling competencies · hard · In review

The original Sue, Arredondo and McDavis multicultural competencies were organized around attitudes and beliefs, knowledge and skills. Which additional aspirational competency did the Multicultural and Social Justice Counseling Competencies (MSJCC) add?
A. Action ✔ KEY
The MSJCC framework adds action (AKSA), calling counselors to engage in advocacy and intervention at multiple levels.
B. Awareness
Awareness of one's own cultural values and biases was already central to the original competencies.
C. Assessment
Culturally appropriate assessment is part of skills, not a separate aspirational competency in the MSJCC.
D. Accreditation
Accreditation is a program-level standard, not one of the aspirational competencies.
Rationale: The MSJCC (Ratts and colleagues, endorsed by ACA in 2015) uses quadrants of privileged and marginalized counselor and client identities and four developmental domains: counselor self-awareness, client worldview, the counseling relationship, and counseling and advocacy interventions. Each domain includes attitudes and beliefs, knowledge, skills and action.
References: Sue & Sue (Counseling the Culturally Diverse) — Multicultural and Social Justice Counseling Competencies (MSJCC)

304. nce-s-cou-056 · Social and Cultural Diversity · multicultural counseling competencies · easy · In review

A counselor notices she feels impatient with clients who defer major decisions to their extended families, and she begins examining how her own upbringing shapes that reaction. In the Sue, Arredondo and McDavis framework, this work MOST directly reflects which competency area?
A. Understanding the worldview of the culturally different client
Understanding the client's worldview is a separate area; her focus here is on her own conditioning.
B. Developing culturally appropriate intervention strategies
Intervention strategies concern what the counselor does in session, not examining her own reactions.
C. Awareness of her own cultural values, assumptions and biases ✔ KEY
Examining how her own background shapes reactions to clients is counselor self-awareness, the first competency area.
D. Knowledge of family structures common among collectivist cultures
Knowledge of client cultures matters, but the activity described is reflection on her own biases.
Rationale: Sue, Arredondo and McDavis (1992) described three characteristics of the culturally skilled counselor: awareness of one's own assumptions, values and biases; understanding the worldview of culturally different clients; and developing appropriate intervention strategies. Each is assessed across beliefs and attitudes, knowledge and skills.

305. nce-s-cou-057 · Social and Cultural Diversity · racial and cultural identity models · medium · In review

Months after a racist incident on campus shook her belief that race did not matter, a Black college student now surrounds herself with Black-centered organizations, studies Black history intensely and expresses anger toward White people. In Cross's Nigrescence model, she is MOST likely in which stage?
A. Pre-encounter
Pre-encounter involves low race salience or pro-White attitudes, which she has moved beyond.
B. Immersion-emersion ✔ KEY
Immersion-emersion involves intense involvement in Black culture and often anger toward White people.
C. Encounter
The encounter is the event, here the incident, that challenged her prior worldview; she has moved past it.
D. Internalization
Internalization brings a secure, less reactive Black identity and openness to other groups.
Rationale: Cross's model describes pre-encounter, encounter, immersion-emersion and internalization (with internalization-commitment for sustained action). After an encounter disrupts the old identity, the person often immerses in Black culture and withdraws from or rejects White culture before reaching a more secure, internalized identity.
References: Sue & Sue (Counseling the Culturally Diverse) — Racial/cultural identity development: Cross Nigrescence model

306. nce-s-cou-058 · Social and Cultural Diversity · racial and cultural identity models · hard · In review

After a multicultural course, a White counseling student feels guilt and confusion. She now sees racism around her, yet feels torn between loyalty to family members who make racist jokes and her new beliefs. In Helms' White racial identity model, this experience BEST fits which status?
A. Contact
Contact involves obliviousness to racism and a naive, color-blind view, which she has moved past.
B. Reintegration
Reintegration resolves the conflict by retreating to beliefs in White superiority and blaming people of color.
C. Pseudo-independence
Pseudo-independence is an intellectual commitment to change that comes after the conflict of disintegration.
D. Disintegration ✔ KEY
Disintegration involves guilt, confusion and conflict between new awareness of racism and loyalty to one's own group.
Rationale: Helms' White racial identity statuses are contact, disintegration, reintegration, pseudo-independence, immersion/emersion and autonomy. Disintegration is the uncomfortable phase in which new awareness of racism collides with prior beliefs and group loyalties, producing guilt, shame and moral dilemmas.
References: Sue & Sue (Counseling the Culturally Diverse) — White racial identity development: Helms' model

307. nce-s-cou-059 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · trauma-informed interventions · medium · In review

At an intake, a client with a history of assault becomes visibly tense when the counselor closes the office door and moves her chair to sit between the client and the exit. Which response BEST reflects trauma-informed practice?
A. Name what she noticed, explain the reason for privacy and ask how the client wants the room set up ✔ KEY
This offers transparency, voice and choice, restoring a sense of safety and control, which are core trauma-informed principles.
B. Continue the intake as planned so the client can see the office is safe and her anxiety will fade
Pressing on ignores a cue of distress and puts the counselor's agenda ahead of the client's sense of safety.
C. Begin a detailed account of the assault now so that the tension can be processed early in treatment
Pushing for trauma details at intake, before safety and stabilization, risks retraumatization.
D. Reassure her that the building is secure and that no one can come in while the door is fully closed
Reassurance alone does not give the client any control and may increase her sense of being trapped.
Rationale: SAMHSA's trauma-informed approach rests on safety; trustworthiness and transparency; peer support; collaboration and mutuality; empowerment, voice and choice; and attention to cultural, historical and gender issues. Small environmental choices, explained openly, help clients regain control and prevent retraumatization.
References: Gladding (Counseling: A Comprehensive Profession) — Trauma and crisis counseling: principles of trauma-informed care
⚑ Check: SAMHSA's six trauma-informed principles are cited via Gladding (SAMHSA TIP 57 is not on the approved list).

308. nce-s-cou-060 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · termination skills · medium · In review

A client has met her goals for managing panic after 14 sessions, and she and the counselor agree to end counseling. Which approach to the final sessions is BEST?
A. End at the next session with a brief goodbye to avoid fostering dependence on counseling
An abrupt ending skips consolidation and relapse planning and gives no room to process feelings about ending.
B. Review her gains, anticipate setbacks, plan to keep progress and discuss feelings about ending ✔ KEY
Effective termination consolidates learning, prepares for relapse, addresses feelings and clarifies how to return if needed.
C. Begin work on new goals so that she stays engaged and does not lose momentum after her progress
Adding goals once the agreed goals are met and the client no longer needs services is not in her interest.
D. Reduce session length gradually while avoiding talk about the ending so she is not made anxious
Avoiding the topic of ending denies the client a chance to process it and to plan for maintaining gains.
Rationale: Termination is a planned phase, not an event. Counselors review progress, help clients attribute change to their own efforts, anticipate challenges and relapse, explore feelings about ending and leave the door open, consistent with ACA guidance to terminate when services are no longer needed and to provide pretermination counseling.
References: ACA Code of Ethics — A.11.c Appropriate Termination · Gladding (Counseling: A Comprehensive Profession) — Termination of counseling relationships
☐ Approve☐ Revise☐ RetireNotes:

309. nce-s-cou-061 · Group Counseling and Group Work · group stages and dynamics · medium · In review

A counselor is interviewing applicants for a 10-week interpersonal process group. Under the ACA Code of Ethics, the main purpose of this pre-group screening is to:
A. confirm that each applicant has a diagnosis that the group can be billed for
Billing eligibility is an administrative matter; the Code frames screening around fit, group process and member welfare.
B. collect a detailed history so the leader can interpret members in session
Some history may be gathered, but screening exists to select suitable members, not to prepare interpretations.
C. select members whose goals fit the group and who will not be harmed by it ✔ KEY
A.9.a directs counselors to select members whose needs and goals are compatible with the group, who will not impede it and whose well-being will not be jeopardized.
D. make sure that every member shares the same cultural background and age range
Homogeneity can be useful for some groups, but it is not the ethical purpose of screening, and diversity often enriches a group.
Rationale: ACA Code A.9.a requires counselors to screen prospective group members and, to the extent possible, select those whose needs and goals are compatible with the group's goals, who will not impede the group process and whose well-being will not be jeopardized by the experience. The ASGW Best Practice Guidelines likewise treat screening and orientation as part of group planning.
References: ACA Code of Ethics — A.9.a Screening · Corey (Theory & Practice) — Group work: pre-group screening and selection of members

310. nce-s-cou-062 · Group Counseling and Group Work · group stages and dynamics · easy · In review

A counselor on an acute inpatient psychiatric unit, where the average stay is five days, is designing a daily coping-skills group. Which group format is MOST appropriate?
A. An open group that admits new members as patients arrive ✔ KEY
With rapid turnover, an open group lets patients join and leave as they are admitted and discharged, and each session can stand alone.
B. A closed group that keeps the same members for eight weeks
A closed eight-week group is impossible when patients stay about five days; most would be discharged after a few sessions.
C. A closed group with a waiting list for the next full cycle
A waiting list would keep most short-stay patients from attending at all before discharge.
D. A marathon group that meets once for a full weekend session
A single marathon session does not fit a daily skills program or the unpredictable timing of admissions.
Rationale: Open groups replace members as they leave, which suits settings with high turnover such as inpatient units, though new arrivals can slow the growth of cohesion. Closed groups keep the same members from start to finish, which supports stability and cohesion but requires members who can commit for the full duration.
References: Corey (Theory & Practice) — Group work: open versus closed groups
☐ Approve☐ Revise☐ RetireNotes:

311. nce-s-cou-063 · Group Counseling and Group Work · group stages and dynamics · medium · In review

An elementary school counselor is forming a counseling group for 7-year-olds whose parents recently divorced. Which group size is generally recommended for a group of children this age?
A. 8 to 10 members
Eight to ten is closer to the size often suggested for adult counseling groups; young children need more individual attention.
B. 3 to 5 members ✔ KEY
Young children have short attention spans and need frequent leader attention, so very small groups are recommended.
C. 12 to 15 members
A group this large would leave young children little time to talk and make the group hard to manage.
D. 20 to 25 members
This is a classroom guidance size, suited to psychoeducation lessons rather than a counseling group.
Rationale: Group size depends on members' age, the leader's experience and the type of group. Groups for young children are kept small (often about 3 to 5) and sessions short; adolescent groups are somewhat larger, and an ongoing adult counseling group of about 8 members with one leader is often cited as workable.
References: Corey (Theory & Practice) — Group work: practical considerations, group size and composition
⚑ Check: Verify the recommended size range for young children's groups in the current Corey or Gladding edition (commonly cited as about 3 to 5).

312. nce-s-cou-064 · Group Counseling and Group Work · group leadership skills and therapeutic factors · medium · In review

Two co-leaders of an adult support group notice that members have begun bringing complaints about one leader to the other, and the leaders have started to feel competitive with each other. Which step BEST addresses this co-leadership problem?
A. Divide the members so that each leader is responsible for half of the group
Splitting the group between leaders formalizes the division and leaves the competition unaddressed.
B. Let the more experienced co-leader run the sessions while the other observes
Sidelining one leader avoids the conflict instead of resolving it and wastes the benefits of co-leadership.
C. Agree to hide any differences of opinion from the members during sessions
Respectfully voiced differences can model healthy conflict; concealing them tends to feed the splitting.
D. Meet regularly between sessions to talk openly about their working relationship ✔ KEY
Regular meetings to process their relationship let co-leaders address rivalry and present a united, coordinated approach.
Rationale: Co-leadership offers shared perspective, modeling of a healthy relationship, coverage for absences and mutual support. Its main risks are poor communication, competition and members splitting the leaders. Corey stresses that co-leaders should meet before and after sessions to plan and to process their own relationship.
References: Corey (Theory & Practice) — Group leadership: advantages and disadvantages of co-leadership

313. nce-s-cou-065 · Group Counseling and Group Work · group leadership skills and therapeutic factors · medium · In review

In the fourth session of a men's support group, one member has again taken most of the time telling long stories about his week, while others glance at the clock. Which leader response is MOST appropriate?
A. "You've given us a lot to think about. I'd like to hear how others are reacting to what you said." ✔ KEY
This acknowledges the member without attacking him, blocks the monopolizing and opens the floor to the rest of the group.
B. "Let's keep listening, since he clearly needs the group's time today more than anyone else does."
Letting the pattern continue reinforces monopolizing and ignores the frustration building among other members.
C. "You talk too much in here, and it is unfair to the others. Please stay quiet for the rest of today."
A harsh, shaming confrontation can humiliate the member and makes others fear the same treatment.
D. "I'll speak with you privately after group about whether this group is a good fit for your needs."
Raising removal ignores a workable in-group intervention and treats a common group behavior as grounds for exclusion.
Rationale: Monopolizing is one of the most common problem behaviors in groups. Leaders intervene early and respectfully, blocking the behavior while protecting the member's dignity, and they often invite the group to share reactions so members learn to address the pattern directly.
References: Corey (Theory & Practice) — Group work: problem behaviors, the monopolizing member

314. nce-s-cou-066 · Group Counseling and Group Work · group leadership skills and therapeutic factors · medium · In review

In a teen group, several members begin blaming one boy for the group's lack of progress, citing his lateness and jokes, and they bring him up whenever the discussion becomes tense. Which leader response is BEST?
A. Ask the boy to explain to the group why he keeps arriving late and making jokes
Putting him on the spot joins the group's blaming and deepens his role as the scapegoat.
B. Allow the criticism to continue, since honest feedback helps him change his conduct
Collective blaming is not constructive feedback, and letting it continue fails the leader's duty to protect members.
C. Block the blaming and ask the group to look at what is happening among them now ✔ KEY
The leader protects the targeted member and turns attention to the group process, including the feelings members may be displacing onto him.
D. Move the boy to a different group so that the others can resume productive work
Removing the scapegoat leaves the underlying dynamic in place, and the group often finds a new target.
Rationale: Scapegoating occurs when a group displaces its tension onto one member. The leader blocks the attack to protect that member and then explores what the group is avoiding, which usually involves members' own anxieties or conflicts with the leader or one another.
References: Corey (Theory & Practice) — Group work: scapegoating and protecting members · ACA Code of Ethics — A.9.b Protecting Clients (group work)

315. nce-s-cou-067 · Group Counseling and Group Work · group leadership skills and therapeutic factors · easy · In review

A group leader says, "I notice that each time someone starts to talk about anger, the group quickly shifts to joking. What do you make of that?" This statement is an example of which leader focus?
A. A content focus on the topic under discussion
A content focus addresses what is being discussed, such as the anger itself, rather than how the group handles it.
B. A process focus on how the members interact ✔ KEY
The leader is commenting on the pattern of interaction in the room, which is a process observation.
C. An educational focus on the facts about anger
An educational focus would teach information about anger rather than reflect a pattern back to the group.
D. An individual focus on one member's history
The observation is about the group as a whole, not about any one member's background.
Rationale: Content is what the group talks about; process is how members relate to one another while they talk, including patterns of avoidance, alliances and shifts in mood. Process comments help members see their here-and-now behavior and are central to interpersonal group work.
References: Yalom & Leszcz (Group Psychotherapy) — Ch. 6: The here-and-now and process illumination

316. nce-s-cou-068 · Group Counseling and Group Work · group leadership skills and therapeutic factors · easy · In review

A leader says, "Amara, what you just described about feeling invisible at work sounds a lot like what Dev shared last week about his family. Dev, how does Amara's story land for you?" Which group leadership skill is the leader using?
A. Cutting off
Cutting off stops a member who is rambling or going off track; nothing is being stopped here.
B. Drawing out
Drawing out invites a quiet member to participate; the main move here is connecting two members' concerns.
C. Summarizing
Summarizing pulls together the main points of a discussion rather than connecting two members.
D. Linking ✔ KEY
Linking connects what one member says or feels to another member's concerns, promoting member-to-member interaction.
Rationale: Linking builds cohesion and universality by pointing out common themes between members and encouraging them to speak to each other rather than only to the leader. Related skills include drawing out quieter members and cutting off, or redirecting, members who stray from the group's focus.
References: Corey (Theory & Practice) — Group leadership skills: linking

317. nce-s-cou-069 · Group Counseling and Group Work · group leadership skills and therapeutic factors · hard · In review

Which statement about group cohesiveness is MOST consistent with Yalom's account of it?
A. Highly cohesive groups can express and work through more conflict and hostility. ✔ KEY
Yalom notes that members of cohesive groups feel secure enough to voice anger and disagreement, which can then be worked through.
B. Cohesiveness is mostly fixed by the end of the first session and changes little later.
Cohesion develops and fluctuates over the life of a group; it is not set in the first meeting.
C. Cohesive groups tend to have poorer attendance because members feel less accountable.
Cohesion is linked to better attendance, greater participation and lower dropout, not poorer attendance.
D. Cohesion is a distinct outcome rather than a condition for other therapeutic factors.
Yalom treats cohesiveness as a precondition for other factors, comparable to the relationship in individual therapy.
Rationale: Yalom describes cohesiveness as the group analogue of the therapeutic relationship: the members' sense of belonging and acceptance. It is associated with better attendance, more self-disclosure, greater risk-taking and better outcomes, and cohesive groups can tolerate open conflict because members trust the group to survive it.
References: Yalom & Leszcz (Group Psychotherapy) — Ch. 3: Group cohesiveness
☐ Approve☐ Revise☐ RetireNotes:

318. nce-s-cou-070 · Group Counseling and Group Work · group stages and dynamics · medium · In review

Midway through a closed 12-week counseling group, a member emails the leader that she has decided to quit the group and will not return. What is the BEST response from the leader?
A. Tell her she agreed to finish all 12 weeks and must attend until the group ends
Members have the right to leave a group; the leader can encourage but not compel continued attendance.
B. Accept her decision and avoid mentioning her absence so the others stay focused
Unexplained departures affect remaining members, who may wonder whether they caused it; silence leaves this unprocessed.
C. Respect her right to leave while encouraging her to talk it over with the group ✔ KEY
Corey advises leaders to respect members' freedom to leave while encouraging them to discuss the decision with the group rather than simply disappear.
D. Replace her at once with a new member from the waiting list to keep the size stable
Adding a member midway changes the closed format and does nothing to address the departure itself.
Rationale: Members have the right to leave a group, but an abrupt exit can leave both the departing member and the remaining members with unfinished business. Leaders explain this during orientation and, when a member wants to leave, encourage discussion of the decision in the group and process the loss with those who remain.
References: Corey (Theory & Practice) — Group work ethics: freedom to leave a group

319. nce-s-cou-071 · Group Counseling and Group Work · group stages and dynamics · hard · In review

In a training group, members persistently treat the leader as all-knowing, wait passively for direction and become resentful when the leader does not supply answers. In Bion's theory of group functioning, this pattern BEST reflects which basic assumption?
A. Fight-flight
Fight-flight is shown when the group attacks something or escapes the task, for example through hostility or changing the subject.
B. Dependency ✔ KEY
In basic assumption dependency the group acts as if a strong leader will provide for and protect it, and members become passive.
C. Pairing
Pairing is a shared hope that two members will produce a future savior or solution, not reliance on the leader.
D. Work group
The work group is Bion's term for the rational, task-focused mode of functioning, not a basic assumption.
Rationale: Wilfred Bion, working at the Tavistock Clinic, proposed that groups alternate between a work group focused on the task and three unconscious basic assumptions: dependency, fight-flight and pairing. Recognizing a basic assumption lets the leader interpret the group-as-a-whole behavior instead of simply meeting the demand.
References: Yalom & Leszcz (Group Psychotherapy) — Group-as-a-whole approaches: Bion's basic assumptions (Tavistock)
⚑ Check: Verify that the cited Yalom & Leszcz chapter (group-as-a-whole/Tavistock discussion) covers Bion's basic assumptions; Corey or Gladding may be a better reference.

320. nce-s-cou-072 · Career Development · career development theories (Holland, Super, Krumboltz) · easy · In review

Frank Parsons described wise vocational choice as resting on a clear understanding of oneself, knowledge of the requirements and conditions of different jobs, and which third element?
A. Exploration of chance events that may reshape one's path
Using chance events is central to Krumboltz's planned happenstance, not to Parsons' model.
B. Implementation of the self-concept through a series of roles
Implementing the self-concept in work roles is Super's idea, developed decades after Parsons.
C. Resolution of early parent-child needs through one's work
Linking occupational choice to early parent-child relations is Anne Roe's theory.
D. True reasoning on the relations between these two groups ✔ KEY
Parsons' third step was "true reasoning" about how the person's traits match the requirements of occupations.
Rationale: In Choosing a Vocation (1909), Parsons set out three steps: understand yourself, know the world of work and use true reasoning to match the two. This matching approach became trait-factor theory and underlies later person-environment fit models such as Holland's.
References: Sharf (Career Development Theory) — Trait and factor theory: Parsons' three-part model

321. nce-s-cou-073 · Career Development · career development theories (Holland, Super, Krumboltz) · medium · In review

A client's interest inventory shows a very high Investigative score, while her other five RIASEC scores are all low and close together. In Holland's theory, this profile is BEST described as showing a high degree of:
A. Differentiation ✔ KEY
Differentiation is the degree to which a profile has clear peaks and valleys; one high score above flat low scores is well differentiated.
B. Consistency
Consistency concerns how close a person's top types sit on the hexagon, which needs at least two high types to judge.
C. Congruence
Congruence is the fit between a person's type and the work environment, which a profile alone does not show.
D. Calculus
Calculus is Holland's assumption that hexagon distances reflect type relationships, not a property of one profile.
Rationale: Holland's secondary constructs include differentiation (how distinct the profile is), consistency (how related the top types are on the hexagon), congruence (person-environment fit), identity (clarity and stability of goals) and calculus. A flat profile is undifferentiated and is often associated with less clear career direction.
References: Sharf (Career Development Theory) — Holland's theory: differentiation, consistency and congruence

322. nce-s-cou-074 · Career Development · career development theories (Holland, Super, Krumboltz) · hard · In review

Super used career maturity to describe an adolescent's readiness to make age-appropriate career decisions. Because adults recycle through stages rather than progress in a fixed order, which term did Super propose for readiness to cope with changing work and working conditions in adulthood?
A. Career salience
Salience refers to the importance a person gives to the worker role relative to other life roles.
B. Vocational identity
Vocational identity, the clarity and stability of one's goals and interests, is associated with Holland.
C. Career adaptability ✔ KEY
Super introduced career adaptability as the adult counterpart of career maturity; Savickas later made it central to career construction theory.
D. Career crystallizing
Crystallizing is a developmental task of the exploration stage, not an index of adult readiness.
Rationale: Career maturity, measured by tools such as the Career Development Inventory, compares a young person's coping with developmental tasks to that of peers. Because adult careers involve recycling and transitions, Super suggested career adaptability for adults, a concept Savickas expanded into the adapt-abilities of concern, control, curiosity and confidence.
References: Sharf (Career Development Theory) — Super's theory: career maturity and career adaptability
⚑ Check: Confirm attribution: Super proposed career adaptability for adults (early 1980s), later developed by Savickas.

323. nce-s-cou-075 · Career Development · career development theories (Holland, Super, Krumboltz) · medium · In review

According to Anne Roe's theory, an adult who grew up in a cold, rejecting or neglectful family climate would be MOST likely to gravitate toward which kind of occupation?
A. Person-oriented fields such as service and business contact
Roe linked warm, accepting or overprotective climates, not cold ones, with orientation toward people.
B. Non-person-oriented fields such as technology and science ✔ KEY
Roe proposed that avoidant (rejecting or neglecting) climates lead toward work that does not center on people.
C. Highly creative fields chosen to win approval from parents
Roe did not tie avoidant climates to approval-seeking creative work; arts and entertainment is a field, not this mechanism.
D. Any field at all, since family climate predicts only level
In Roe's model, family climate shapes orientation toward or away from people; level depends on ability and status factors.
Rationale: Roe classified occupations into eight fields and six levels and proposed that early parent-child relations (emotional concentration, avoidance or acceptance) create needs that steer people toward or away from person-oriented work. Research gave her theory limited support, but her field-and-level classification influenced later occupational systems.
References: Sharf (Career Development Theory) — Roe's personality theory of career choice: parent-child relations

324. nce-s-cou-076 · Career Development · career development theories (Holland, Super, Krumboltz) · easy · In review

A 14-year-old who once wanted to be an astronaut now talks about whether she has the grades and abilities for engineering and whether the work fits what she values. In Ginzberg's theory, she is in which period?
A. Fantasy period
The fantasy period (to about age 11) involves play-based choices made without regard to ability or reality.
B. Realistic period
The realistic period (about 17 into early adulthood) involves exploration, crystallization and specification of a choice.
C. Crystallization
Crystallization is a substage of the later realistic period, when a clear occupational pattern emerges.
D. Tentative period ✔ KEY
The tentative period (about 11 to 17) moves through interest, capacity, values and transition substages.
Rationale: Ginzberg, Ginsburg, Axelrad and Herma described three periods: fantasy, tentative (interest, capacity, values, transition) and realistic (exploration, crystallization, specification). Ginzberg later revised the theory to view career choice as a lifelong, open-ended process rather than one fixed by early adulthood.
References: Sharf (Career Development Theory) — Ginzberg's developmental theory: fantasy, tentative and realistic periods
