325. nce-s-cou-077 · Career Development · career development theories (Holland, Super, Krumboltz) · hard · In review

A client must make a major compromise because her preferred occupations are not available locally. According to Gottfredson's theory of circumscription and compromise, which aspect of her self-concept is she likely to protect MOST when compromising?
A. Sextype, how suitable the job seems for her gender ✔ KEY
Gottfredson proposed that sextype, the earliest and most core element to be circumscribed, is the last to be given up in major compromises.
B. Interest field, how well the work matches her likes
Interests develop later and are more peripheral, so they tend to be sacrificed before prestige or sextype in major compromises.
C. Prestige, the social standing of the occupation
Prestige is protected over interests but is given up before sextype when the compromise is severe.
D. Pay level, the salary that the job typically offers
Salary is not one of the self-concept dimensions in Gottfredson's model of circumscription and compromise.
Rationale: Gottfredson described circumscription in four stages: orientation to size and power, to sex roles, to social valuation and to the internal, unique self. Because sextype is incorporated earliest, it is the most protected aspect; in major compromises people tend to give up interest fit first, then prestige, and sextype last.
References: Sharf (Career Development Theory) — Gottfredson's theory of circumscription and compromise
⚑ Check: Gottfredson's 1996/2002 revision makes the order depend on how severe the compromise is; verify the key against the current Sharf wording.

326. nce-s-cou-078 · Career Development · career development theories (Holland, Super, Krumboltz) · medium · In review

A counselor helps a client set a career goal, clarify her values, generate several alternatives and gather occupational information. In Krumboltz's social learning theory of career decision making, the counselor is building which factor?
A. Genetic endowment and special abilities
Genetic endowment covers inherited qualities such as physical traits and talents, which counseling does not build.
B. Environmental conditions and events
Environmental conditions, such as job markets and policies, are largely outside the individual's control.
C. Task-approach skills ✔ KEY
Task-approach skills are the learned skills, such as goal setting, values clarification and information seeking, used to approach career tasks.
D. Associative learning experiences
Associative learning links neutral stimuli with positive or negative ones; it is not the set of decision skills described.
Rationale: Krumboltz identified four influences on career decisions: genetic endowment and special abilities, environmental conditions and events, learning experiences (instrumental and associative) and task-approach skills. Their interaction produces self-observation generalizations, worldview generalizations and the actions people take.
References: Sharf (Career Development Theory) — Krumboltz's social learning theory: task-approach skills

327. nce-s-cou-079 · Career Development · career development theories (Holland, Super, Krumboltz) · medium · In review

A college senior knows her strengths and has researched several occupations, but each time she nears a choice she tells herself, "I always make the wrong decision," and stalls. In the cognitive information processing (CIP) approach, the counselor should focus FIRST on which domain of the pyramid?
A. Self-knowledge domain
She already knows her strengths; her difficulty is not a lack of self-knowledge.
B. Executive processing domain ✔ KEY
Executive processing covers metacognitions such as self-talk and self-monitoring; her negative self-talk is blocking the process.
C. Occupational knowledge domain
She has already researched occupations, so information about options is not the main barrier.
D. Decision-making skills domain
The CASVE cycle may need work later, but the dysfunctional self-talk at the top of the pyramid is what stalls her.
Rationale: Peterson, Sampson, Reardon and Lenz describe a pyramid with knowledge domains (self and occupations) at the base, decision-making skills (the CASVE cycle: communication, analysis, synthesis, valuing, execution) in the middle and executive processing at the top. Negative career thoughts, measured by the Career Thoughts Inventory, operate at the executive level.
References: Sharf (Career Development Theory) — Cognitive information processing: pyramid and executive processing

328. nce-s-cou-080 · Career Development · career development theories (Holland, Super, Krumboltz) · medium · In review

A first-generation college student is confident she can master the coursework for an engineering degree, but she doubts that the degree will lead to a good job for someone without family connections in the field. In social cognitive career theory, her doubt BEST reflects low:
A. Self-efficacy beliefs
Self-efficacy is belief in one's ability to perform; she is confident she can do the work.
B. Personal goals
Goals are intentions to pursue an activity or outcome; her doubt concerns expected results, not intentions.
C. Interest development
In SCCT interests grow from efficacy and outcome expectations; the stem describes a belief about results.
D. Outcome expectations ✔ KEY
Outcome expectations are beliefs about the consequences of an action, such as whether a degree will pay off.
Rationale: Lent, Brown and Hackett's social cognitive career theory builds on Bandura and centers on self-efficacy, outcome expectations and personal goals. Contextual supports and barriers, such as lack of professional networks, shape these beliefs and help explain why capable people may avoid a field.
References: Sharf (Career Development Theory) — Social cognitive career theory: self-efficacy, outcome expectations and goals

329. nce-s-cou-081 · Career Development · career development theories (Holland, Super, Krumboltz) · medium · In review

An accountant consistently receives excellent performance reviews, but he tells his counselor that the job offers none of the autonomy or variety he values and he dreads going to work. In the theory of work adjustment, his situation is BEST described as:
A. high satisfactoriness and low satisfaction ✔ KEY
His employer is pleased with his performance (satisfactoriness), but his own needs and values are not being met (satisfaction).
B. high satisfaction and low satisfactoriness
This reverses the terms; satisfaction is the worker's view of the job and satisfactoriness is the employer's view of the worker.
C. low satisfaction and low satisfactoriness
His excellent reviews show that the employer finds him satisfactory.
D. high satisfaction and high satisfactoriness
His dread and unmet values show that he is not satisfied with the job.
Rationale: Dawis and Lofquist's theory of work adjustment holds that tenure depends on correspondence between person and environment. Satisfaction reflects whether the job's reinforcers meet the worker's needs and values; satisfactoriness reflects whether the worker's abilities meet the job's requirements. Low satisfaction predicts that the worker may leave even when performance is strong.
References: Sharf (Career Development Theory) — Theory of work adjustment: satisfaction and satisfactoriness
☐ Approve☐ Revise☐ RetireNotes:

330. nce-s-cou-082 · Career Development · career development theories (Holland, Super, Krumboltz) · medium · In review

A 24-year-old has interests and options but says his parents should decide his career, and he puts off every decision he could make himself. In Savickas's career construction theory, which career adapt-ability is MOST in need of development?
A. Concern
Concern is orientation toward and planning for the future; the main problem described is lack of ownership of decisions.
B. Curiosity
Curiosity is exploring self and options; he already has interests and options to choose from.
C. Control ✔ KEY
Control is taking personal responsibility for one's career; deferring decisions and indecision signal a control deficit.
D. Confidence
Confidence is belief in one's ability to solve problems; the stem points to handing decisions to others rather than self-doubt.
Rationale: Savickas identifies four career adapt-abilities: concern (future orientation), control (decisiveness and ownership), curiosity (exploration) and confidence (self-efficacy for problem solving). Career construction counseling also uses the Career Construction Interview to identify life themes that give meaning to career choices.
References: Sharf (Career Development Theory) — Savickas's career construction theory: career adapt-abilities

331. nce-s-cou-083 · Career Development · career development theories (Holland, Super, Krumboltz) · easy · In review

A counselor wants a free online database, sponsored by the U.S. Department of Labor, that replaced the Dictionary of Occupational Titles and describes occupations by tasks, skills, knowledge, work context and interest codes. Which resource fits this description?
A. Occupational Outlook Handbook
The Bureau of Labor Statistics handbook focuses on job outlook, pay and training, and it did not replace the DOT.
B. Occupational Information Network ✔ KEY
O*NET replaced the DOT and describes occupations with detailed descriptors, including RIASEC interest profiles.
C. Standard Occupational Classification
The SOC is a federal coding system for classifying jobs in statistical data, not a descriptive career database.
D. Guide for Occupational Exploration
The GOE grouped occupations by interest area and was a DOT companion, not its replacement.
Rationale: O*NET, the Occupational Information Network, is the Department of Labor's primary source of occupational information and replaced the DOT. It links occupations to Holland codes and pairs with labor market information, such as the Occupational Outlook Handbook's projections and wage data.
References: Gladding (Counseling: A Comprehensive Profession) — Career counseling: occupational information sources (O*NET, OOH)

332. nce-s-cou-084 · Human Growth and Development · human development theories (Erikson, Piaget, Kohlberg) · easy · In review

A 9-year-old spends his energy on school, sports and friendships with same-sex peers, and sexual interests seem quiet. In Freud's psychosexual theory, he is in which stage?
A. Phallic stage
The phallic stage (about 3 to 6) centers on the Oedipal conflict and identification with the same-sex parent.
B. Genital stage
The genital stage begins at puberty, when sexual impulses reawaken and turn toward mature relationships.
C. Anal stage
The anal stage (about 1 to 3) centers on toilet training and control.
D. Latency stage ✔ KEY
In latency (about 6 to puberty) sexual drives are dormant and energy goes into skills, school and same-sex friendships.
Rationale: Freud's psychosexual stages are oral (birth to 1), anal (1 to 3), phallic (3 to 6), latency (6 to 11) and genital (adolescence). Erikson's industry vs. inferiority covers the same school-age years, with emphasis on competence rather than sexual drives.
References: Berk (Development Through the Lifespan) — Freud's psychosexual theory: latency stage
☐ Approve☐ Revise☐ RetireNotes:

333. nce-s-cou-085 · Human Growth and Development · human development theories (Erikson, Piaget, Kohlberg) · easy · In review

A 19-year-old says he will take over his family's restaurant, as his parents always planned, and that he has never seriously considered any other path. In Marcia's framework, his identity status is:
A. Identity foreclosure ✔ KEY
Foreclosure is commitment without exploration, often accepting goals and values chosen by parents.
B. Identity moratorium
Moratorium is active exploration without commitment yet; he has committed without exploring.
C. Identity achievement
Achievement is commitment that follows a period of exploration, which he has not had.
D. Identity diffusion
Diffusion is neither exploring nor committed; he holds a firm commitment.
Rationale: Marcia extended Erikson's identity stage by crossing two dimensions, exploration and commitment, to form four statuses: achievement (explored, committed), moratorium (exploring, not committed), foreclosure (committed, not explored) and diffusion (neither). Statuses can change over time, and foreclosure is not unhealthy in every cultural context.
References: Berk (Development Through the Lifespan) — Marcia's identity statuses

334. nce-s-cou-086 · Human Growth and Development · human development theories (Erikson, Piaget, Kohlberg) · medium · In review

A 42-year-old architect is questioning whether the youthful vision he built his career around is worth pursuing, reappraising his marriage and wondering what legacy he will leave. In Levinson's seasons of life, he is MOST likely in the:
A. early adult transition
The early adult transition (about 17 to 22) involves leaving adolescence and forming a first sense of the Dream.
B. midlife transition ✔ KEY
The midlife transition (about 40 to 45) involves reappraising the Dream and the life structure built in early adulthood.
C. age 30 transition
The age 30 transition (about 28 to 33) involves adjusting the first adult life structure, not midlife reappraisal.
D. late adult transition
The late adult transition (about 60 to 65) involves facing aging and moving into late adulthood.
Rationale: Levinson described adulthood as alternating periods of building a life structure and transitions that question it. Key concepts include the Dream, a vision of one's adult future, and the mentor; at the midlife transition, people reappraise the Dream and rework the polarities of young-old, destruction-creation, masculine-feminine and attachment-separateness.

335. nce-s-cou-087 · Human Growth and Development · human development theories (Erikson, Piaget, Kohlberg) · hard · In review

An 8-year-old's mother is moved to night shifts by her employer, and the child now spends evenings with less supervision and less help with homework. The child has never been to the workplace. In Bronfenbrenner's ecological systems theory, the employer's decision operates at which level?
A. Microsystem
The microsystem includes settings the child directly takes part in, such as home and school.
B. Mesosystem
The mesosystem is the connections between the child's microsystems, such as home-school links.
C. Exosystem ✔ KEY
The exosystem includes settings the child does not take part in, such as a parent's workplace, that still affect the child.
D. Macrosystem
The macrosystem is the broad cultural values, laws and customs that shape all the other levels.
Rationale: Bronfenbrenner nested the child within the microsystem, mesosystem, exosystem and macrosystem, with the chronosystem adding change over time. A parent's workplace policy is a classic exosystem influence: it shapes the child's daily life even though the child never enters that setting.
References: Berk (Development Through the Lifespan) — Bronfenbrenner's ecological systems theory: the exosystem

336. nce-s-cou-088 · Human Growth and Development · human development theories (Erikson, Piaget, Kohlberg) · easy · In review

A tutor gives a first-grader step-by-step hints on a puzzle he cannot yet solve alone, then gradually withdraws the hints as he becomes able to solve it himself. This adjustment of support is BEST described as:
A. Accommodation
Accommodation is Piaget's term for changing a mental scheme to fit new information.
B. Private speech
Private speech is a child's self-directed talk used to guide thinking, not the adult's support.
C. Centration
Centration is the preoperational tendency to focus on one aspect of a situation.
D. Scaffolding ✔ KEY
Scaffolding adjusts the level of support to the child's current performance and fades it as competence grows.
Rationale: Vygotsky's zone of proximal development is the range of tasks a child cannot yet do alone but can do with help from a more skilled partner. Scaffolding, a term introduced by Wood, Bruner and Ross and applied to Vygotsky's theory, describes how that help is fine-tuned and withdrawn as the child masters the task.
References: Berk (Development Through the Lifespan) — Vygotsky's sociocultural theory: zone of proximal development and scaffolding

337. nce-s-cou-089 · Human Growth and Development · human development theories (Erikson, Piaget, Kohlberg) · hard · In review

A woman who has long put everyone else's needs ahead of her own comes to believe that caring responsibly must include caring for herself, and that avoiding harm applies to her as well as to others. In Gilligan's ethic of care, this reasoning BEST reflects which level?
A. Morality of nonviolence ✔ KEY
The third level integrates care for self and others, with a principle of not hurting anyone, including oneself.
B. Goodness as self-sacrifice
This second level equates goodness with meeting others' needs at one's own expense, which she is moving beyond.
C. Orientation to survival
The first level focuses on the self and its survival, without concern for responsibility to others.
D. Social contract reasoning
Social contract reasoning is Kohlberg's stage 5, based on justice and rights rather than care.
Rationale: Gilligan argued that Kohlberg's justice-based stages underrepresented an ethic of care. Her levels move from orientation to individual survival, through goodness as self-sacrifice, to the morality of nonviolence, with transitions from selfishness to responsibility and from goodness to truth about one's own needs.

338. nce-s-cou-090 · Human Growth and Development · human development theories (Erikson, Piaget, Kohlberg) · medium · In review

A school counselor is planning supports for children growing up with poverty and parental substance use. Longitudinal research on resilience, such as Werner and Smith's Kauai study, MOST consistently identifies which protective factor?
A. Avoiding all stressful experiences throughout early childhood
Resilience develops through coping with manageable stress; complete avoidance of stress is neither possible nor protective.
B. A warm, stable relationship with at least one caring adult ✔ KEY
A close bond with a competent, caring adult, who need not be a parent, is among the most consistent protective factors.
C. An above-average IQ score as measured in the preschool years
Good cognitive skills can help, but a high IQ alone is not the most consistent protective factor.
D. Living in a family that has no history of mental illness
This describes the absence of a risk factor rather than a protective factor that buffers existing risk.
Rationale: Resilience research identifies protective factors at several levels: personal traits such as an easygoing temperament, a warm relationship with a caring adult, and community supports such as schools and mentors. 339. nce-s-cou-091 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · family systems theories (Bowen, structural, strategic) · medium · In review

A strategic therapist tells a client with chronic insomnia that on any night he is still awake at midnight, he must get up and wax the kitchen floor until morning. The task is deliberately designed to be more unpleasant than the symptom itself. Which intervention and originator does this illustrate?
A. Ordeal therapy, associated with Jay Haley ✔ KEY
Haley's ordeal therapy assigns a task more burdensome than the symptom, so giving up the symptom becomes the easier choice.
B. Unbalancing, associated with Salvador Minuchin
Unbalancing is a structural technique in which the therapist temporarily sides with one family member to shift the hierarchy.
C. Paradoxical intention, from Viktor Frankl
Frankl's paradoxical intention would ask the client to try to stay awake; it does not attach an aversive, effortful task to the symptom.
D. Detriangulation, associated with Murray Bowen
Detriangulation is a Bowenian process of removing oneself from a three-person emotional triangle, unrelated to symptom tasks.
Rationale: Jay Haley described ordeal therapy as a strategic directive in which the client must perform an ordeal, ideally one that is good for the person (exercise, chores), every time the symptom occurs. Because the ordeal costs more than the symptom, the client gives up the symptom. It reflects strategic therapy's focus on directives and on changing behavior rather than producing insight.
References: Gladding (Counseling: A Comprehensive Profession) — Strategic family therapy: Haley, directives and ordeals
☐ Approve☐ Revise☐ RetireNotes:

340. nce-s-cou-092 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · family systems theories (Bowen, structural, strategic) · hard · In review

A brief therapy team trained at the Mental Research Institute (MRI) in Palo Alto notices that a mother's repeated lectures to her withdrawn son lead him to withdraw further, which prompts more lectures. In the MRI model, what is the PRIMARY target of intervention?
A. The son's unresolved early childhood conflicts
MRI brief therapy deliberately avoids historical, insight-oriented exploration of early conflicts.
B. The family's level of differentiation of self
Differentiation of self is the central construct of Bowen family systems theory, not the MRI model.
C. The attempted solution maintaining the problem ✔ KEY
The MRI team holds that well-meant attempted solutions (here, lecturing) keep the problem going, so interrupting them is the target.
D. The hierarchy and boundaries between subsystems
Restructuring hierarchy and subsystem boundaries is the focus of Minuchin's structural family therapy.
Rationale: In MRI brief therapy (Watzlawick, Weakland, Fisch), problems persist because of the very solutions people repeatedly apply to them. "More of the same" produces only first-order change within the system's existing rules; the therapist aims for second-order change by blocking or reversing the attempted solution, often through reframing or paradoxical directives.
References: Gladding (Counseling: A Comprehensive Profession) — Strategic family therapy: MRI brief therapy, attempted solutions, first- and second-order change

341. nce-s-cou-093 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · family systems theories (Bowen, structural, strategic) · easy · In review

During a family session about a teenager's failing grades, her father answers every emotional comment with charts, rules and calm, logical analysis, showing little feeling for himself or for others. Which Satir communication stance is he displaying?
A. Blaming
The blamer finds fault and attacks others to protect the self, disregarding others' feelings rather than all feelings.
B. Placating
The placater pleases and appeases others at the expense of the self; the father is not deferring to anyone.
C. Irrelevant
The irrelevant (distracting) stance changes the subject or clowns around, ignoring self, others and context.
D. Super-reasonable ✔ KEY
The super-reasonable (computer) stance relies on cool logic and data while discounting the feelings of self and others.
Rationale: Virginia Satir described four incongruent survival stances people adopt under stress: placating, blaming, super-reasonable and irrelevant. Her goal was congruent communication, in which words, feelings and body language match and self, other and context are all honored.
References: Gladding (Counseling: A Comprehensive Profession) — Experiential family therapy: Satir communication stances

342. nce-s-cou-094 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · family systems theories (Bowen, structural, strategic) · medium · In review

A family therapist insists early on that he decides who attends and how sessions are run, but then leaves it to the family to take responsibility for what will change. He relies on spontaneity, playfulness and co-therapy, and calls these struggles the "battle for structure" and the "battle for initiative." Which approach is this?
A. Symbolic-experiential (Whitaker) ✔ KEY
Carl Whitaker described the battle for structure and the battle for initiative and emphasized the therapist's spontaneous, often absurd use of self.
B. Structural therapy (Minuchin)
Minuchin focused on joining, mapping and restructuring subsystems and boundaries rather than these two "battles."
C. Bowen systems therapy (Bowen)
Bowen's approach is calm, coaching and theory-driven, aimed at differentiation, not playful or provocative.
D. Validation model (Satir)
Satir was experiential too, but her model centers on self-esteem, communication stances and congruence.
Rationale: Carl Whitaker's symbolic-experiential family therapy values growth and creativity over technique. The therapist wins the battle for structure (control over the conditions of therapy) but must lose the battle for initiative, so that the family owns its decisions about change. Whitaker often worked with a co-therapist and used humor and "craziness" to unsettle rigid patterns.
References: Gladding (Counseling: A Comprehensive Profession) — Experiential family therapy: Whitaker, symbolic-experiential approach

343. nce-s-cou-095 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · psychodynamic and psychoanalytic theory · medium · In review

A man who prides himself on generosity becomes intensely angry at a coworker he calls "greedy and selfish," although his friends say he can be quite stingy himself. A Jungian counselor would MOST likely view his reaction as a projection of which archetype?
A. The persona
The persona is the public mask a person presents; his generous image is persona, but what he projects is the disowned side.
B. The shadow ✔ KEY
The shadow holds the traits a person rejects in himself; they are often projected onto others and met with strong emotion.
C. The anima
The anima is the feminine side of a man's psyche, not the store of disowned negative traits.
D. The Self
The Self is the archetype of wholeness and integration toward which individuation moves.
Rationale: Carl Jung proposed a collective unconscious containing archetypes shared across humanity, including the persona, shadow, anima/animus and Self. Disowned qualities in the shadow are commonly projected onto others, and a strong emotional charge toward someone is a clue. Jungian work aims at individuation, partly by recognizing and integrating the shadow.
References: Corey (Theory & Practice) — Psychoanalytic therapy: Jung's analytical psychology, archetypes and the shadow

344. nce-s-cou-096 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · psychodynamic and psychoanalytic theory · hard · In review

A client raised by emotionally unavailable parents eagerly reports each accomplishment to the counselor, seems to need admiring responses, and feels deflated and empty when the counselor responds neutrally. In Kohut's self psychology, the client is MOST likely seeking which kind of response?
A. An idealizing selfobject response
Idealizing needs involve merging with a calm, admired figure; the client wants to be admired, not to admire.
B. A twinship selfobject response
Twinship (alter ego) needs involve feeling essentially alike with another person, not being admired.
C. A mirroring selfobject response ✔ KEY
Mirroring needs are met by others who reflect back and admire the person's worth and achievements.
D. Splitting of good and bad objects
Splitting is an object relations concept (Klein, Kernberg) about keeping all-good and all-bad images apart, not a need for admiration.
Rationale: Heinz Kohut argued that a cohesive self develops when caregivers serve as selfobjects meeting three needs: mirroring (being admired), idealizing (merging with a strong, calm figure) and twinship (feeling alike). Unmet mirroring needs can leave adults hungry for admiration and fragile in self-esteem. Kohut's self psychology grew out of, and is often grouped with, object relations theory.
References: Corey (Theory & Practice) — Psychoanalytic therapy: contemporary trends, self psychology and object relations

345. nce-s-cou-097 · Human Growth and Development · human development theories (Erikson, Piaget, Kohlberg) · easy · In review

A counseling student is comparing Freud's psychosexual theory with Erikson's psychosocial theory. Which statement MOST accurately describes a key difference between them?
A. Erikson's stages extend across the entire life span ✔ KEY
Erikson's eight stages run from infancy through late adulthood, whereas Freud's stages end with the genital stage at puberty.
B. Erikson made sexual drives the core of every stage
Erikson shifted the emphasis from sexual drives to social and cultural tasks and the growth of identity.
C. Freud proposed eight stages ending in late adulthood
Freud described five psychosexual stages; the eight life-span stages belong to Erikson.
D. Freud stressed social crises more than instinctual drives
This reverses the theories; Freud stressed instinctual drives, and Erikson stressed psychosocial crises.
Rationale: Freud's five psychosexual stages (oral, anal, phallic, latency, genital) center on instinctual drives and end at adolescence. Erikson kept a stage framework but emphasized ego development, social relationships and culture, and he extended development across the whole life span with eight psychosocial crises from trust vs. mistrust to integrity vs. despair.

346. nce-s-cou-098 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · psychodynamic and psychoanalytic theory · easy · In review

Describing a mistake she made at work, a client says aloud to herself, in a scolding tone that echoes her mother, "You should have known better. How careless can you be?" In Eric Berne's transactional analysis, which ego state is she speaking from?
A. Adult
The Adult ego state processes information objectively in the here and now; it does not scold.
B. Critical Parent ✔ KEY
The Critical (Controlling) Parent holds the rules, judgments and criticisms absorbed from caregivers.
C. Adapted Child
The Adapted Child complies with or rebels against parental messages; it receives the scolding rather than delivering it.
D. Free Child
The Free (Natural) Child is spontaneous, playful and expressive, not judgmental.
Rationale: Berne's transactional analysis describes three ego states: Parent (Critical and Nurturing), Adult and Child (Free and Adapted). Self-critical messages copied from caregivers come from the Critical Parent. Arnold Lazarus's multimodal therapy assesses clients across seven modalities summarized by the acronym BASIC I.D. What do the final letters "I.D." represent?
A. Insight and defense mechanisms
Insight and defenses are psychodynamic concepts and are not BASIC I.D. modalities.
B. Identity and dream content
Neither identity nor dreams is one of Lazarus's seven modalities.
C. Interpersonal and drugs/biology ✔ KEY
The last two modalities are Interpersonal relationships and Drugs/biological functions.
D. Imagery and developmental history
Imagery is the earlier "I" in BASIC; developmental history is not a modality.
Rationale: Lazarus's technically eclectic multimodal therapy assesses Behavior, Affect, Sensation, Imagery, Cognition, Interpersonal relationships and Drugs/biology. 348. nce-s-cou-100 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · cognitive behavioral therapy · medium · In review

A counselor prepares a newly hired paramedic for high-stress calls in three phases: first building an understanding of how stress works, then learning and rehearsing coping skills such as coping self-statements and relaxation, and finally applying them under graded, increasingly realistic stressors. Which approach is the counselor using?
A. Systematic desensitization
Wolpe's systematic desensitization pairs relaxation with an imagined fear hierarchy; it lacks the conceptualization and coping-skills phases.
B. Rational emotive imagery
Rational emotive imagery is an REBT technique for changing emotions by imagining a feared event while feeling a healthier emotion.
C. Cognitive processing therapy
Cognitive processing therapy treats PTSD by challenging stuck points about a past trauma, not by inoculating against future stress.
D. Stress inoculation training ✔ KEY
Meichenbaum's stress inoculation training moves through conceptualization, skills acquisition and rehearsal, and application and follow-through.
Rationale: Donald Meichenbaum's stress inoculation training "inoculates" clients against stress by exposing them to manageable doses after they have learned coping skills. Its three phases are conceptualization, skills acquisition and rehearsal, and application and follow-through. It builds on his self-instructional training, which teaches clients to replace self-defeating inner speech with coping self-statements.
References: Corey (Theory & Practice) — Cognitive behavior therapy: Meichenbaum's cognitive behavior modification and stress inoculation training

349. nce-s-cou-101 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · behavior therapy · medium · In review

A 46-year-old client returning to the workforce doubts she can learn the spreadsheet software her new job requires. According to Bandura, which source of self-efficacy is generally the MOST powerful way to strengthen her belief that she can succeed?
A. Watching a peer of similar age master the software
Vicarious experience (modeling) raises self-efficacy, especially with similar models, but less strongly than one's own success.
B. Encouragement from her counselor that she can do it
Verbal persuasion helps but is weaker and easily undone by later failure.
C. Succeeding herself at small, graded spreadsheet tasks ✔ KEY
Mastery (performance) experiences are the most powerful source of self-efficacy because they provide direct evidence of capability.
D. Feeling physically calmer when she sits down to work on it
Physiological and emotional states shape self-efficacy judgments, but they are the least direct of the four sources.
Rationale: Albert Bandura identified four sources of self-efficacy: mastery experiences, vicarious experiences (modeling), verbal persuasion, and physiological and emotional states. Mastery experiences are the strongest. Counselors therefore break goals into graded steps the client can succeed at, often after modeling and with encouragement.
References: Corey (Theory & Practice) — Behavior therapy: Bandura's social learning theory, modeling and self-efficacy

350. nce-s-cou-102 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · behavior therapy · easy · In review

A slot machine pays out after an unpredictable number of plays, averaging about one win every 20 pulls. Which reinforcement schedule is this, and how does it typically affect extinction?
A. Fixed interval; quick to extinguish once payouts stop
A fixed-interval schedule reinforces the first response after a set time; payouts here depend on the number of plays.
B. Variable ratio; highly resistant to extinction ✔ KEY
Reinforcement after an unpredictable number of responses is a variable-ratio schedule, which produces high, steady responding that is very hard to extinguish.
C. Fixed ratio; very resistant because it is predictable
A fixed ratio pays after a set number of responses; its predictability makes it less resistant to extinction than a variable ratio.
D. Variable interval; extinguishes faster than continuous
Payouts here depend on responses, not time, and intermittent schedules resist extinction more than continuous reinforcement.
Rationale: Skinner's intermittent schedules are fixed ratio, variable ratio, fixed interval and variable interval. Variable-ratio reinforcement produces the highest, steadiest response rates and the greatest resistance to extinction, which is why gambling is so persistent. Continuous reinforcement speeds learning but extinguishes fastest.
References: Corey (Theory & Practice) — Behavior therapy: operant conditioning and schedules of reinforcement

351. nce-s-cou-103 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · behavior therapy · medium · In review

After being bitten by a neighbor's German shepherd, a 7-year-old now becomes frightened around other large dogs, yet he stays relaxed around small dogs and cats. In classical conditioning terms, his fear of other large dogs and his calm around small dogs reflect, respectively, which two processes?
A. Spontaneous recovery; stimulus generalization
Spontaneous recovery is the return of an extinguished response after a rest period; nothing here was extinguished.
B. Stimulus discrimination; second-order conditioning
This reverses the first process, and second-order conditioning pairs a new neutral stimulus with an existing conditioned stimulus.
C. Stimulus generalization; stimulus discrimination ✔ KEY
Fear spreading to similar stimuli is generalization; responding differently to dissimilar stimuli is discrimination.
D. Higher-order conditioning; response extinction
His calm around small dogs is not extinction, since that fear was never conditioned and then weakened.
Rationale: In Pavlov's model the bite is the unconditioned stimulus, fear the unconditioned response, and the German shepherd becomes a conditioned stimulus. Stimulus generalization occurs when stimuli similar to the conditioned stimulus also elicit the response; stimulus discrimination occurs when the person responds only to stimuli close enough to the original.
References: Corey (Theory & Practice) — Behavior therapy: classical conditioning, generalization and discrimination

352. nce-s-cou-104 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · behavior therapy · easy · In review

To teach a 4-year-old with a language delay to say "water," a therapist first reinforces any vocal sound, then only "wa," then "wa-ter," and finally only the complete word. Which behavioral technique is the therapist using?
A. Chaining
Chaining links a series of already-learned behaviors into a sequence, such as the steps of handwashing.
B. Token economy
A token economy delivers conditioned reinforcers (tokens) that are later exchanged for backup rewards; it is not defined by successive approximations.
C. Premack principle
The Premack principle uses a high-frequency activity to reinforce a lower-frequency one.
D. Shaping ✔ KEY
Shaping reinforces successive approximations of a target behavior, raising the criterion step by step.
Rationale: Shaping builds a new behavior by reinforcing closer and closer approximations to the goal while no longer reinforcing earlier, cruder forms. It is often combined with other operant tools, such as token economies, in which tokens act as generalized conditioned reinforcers exchanged later for backup reinforcers.
References: Corey (Theory & Practice) — Behavior therapy: operant techniques, shaping and token economies

353. nce-s-cou-105 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · cognitive behavioral therapy · medium · In review

A client keeps telling herself, "I'm a failure." Rather than disputing the thought, her counselor asks her to restate it as "I'm having the thought that I'm a failure" and to notice the words without arguing with them. Which acceptance and commitment therapy (ACT) process is the counselor targeting?
A. Cognitive restructuring
Cognitive restructuring is a traditional CBT strategy that tests and replaces the thought; ACT changes the client's relationship to the thought instead.
B. Cognitive defusion ✔ KEY
Defusion helps clients see thoughts as passing words and images rather than literal truths, loosening their grip on behavior.
C. Committed action
Committed action is taking concrete steps in line with chosen values, not changing how one relates to a thought.
D. Values clarification
Values work identifies what matters most to the client; this exercise targets her relationship to a thought.
Rationale: Steven Hayes's ACT targets psychological flexibility through six hexaflex processes: acceptance, cognitive defusion, contact with the present moment, self-as-context, values and committed action. Defusion techniques, such as adding "I'm having the thought that," create distance from thoughts without trying to change their content.
References: Corey (Theory & Practice) — Third-wave behavior therapies: acceptance and commitment therapy, cognitive defusion
☐ Approve☐ Revise☐ RetireNotes:

354. nce-s-cou-106 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · cognitive behavioral therapy · easy · In review

Mindfulness-based cognitive therapy (MBCT), developed by Segal, Williams and Teasdale, was designed primarily for which purpose?
A. Preventing relapse in people with recurrent depression ✔ KEY
MBCT was built to reduce relapse in people who have had repeated major depressive episodes and are currently in remission.
B. Treating acute mania during inpatient hospitalization
MBCT is a group program for people who are currently well, not an acute treatment for mania.
C. Reducing self-harm in clients with borderline traits
This describes dialectical behavior therapy, which also uses mindfulness but was developed by Linehan.
D. Replacing exposure as the core treatment for specific phobias
Exposure remains the core treatment for specific phobias; MBCT was not developed for them.
Rationale: MBCT combines mindfulness practice, adapted from Kabat-Zinn's MBSR, with cognitive therapy in an 8-week group format. It teaches people with recurrent depression to notice early negative thinking and relate to it from a decentered stance, interrupting the rumination that can trigger relapse. Its strongest evidence is for people with three or more prior episodes.
References: NICE guidelines — Depression in adults (NG222): relapse prevention with mindfulness-based cognitive therapy

355. nce-s-cou-107 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · reality therapy and choice theory · easy · In review

In William Glasser's choice theory, all behavior is an attempt to satisfy five basic needs. Which list names them?
A. Safety, esteem, belonging, purpose, and self-actualization
This resembles Maslow's hierarchy of needs, not Glasser's five basic needs.
B. Survival, attachment, mastery, autonomy, and meaning
This mixes concepts from attachment and self-determination theories; Glasser's list includes power, freedom and fun.
C. Love, work, play, spirituality, and self-direction
These echo Adlerian life tasks and wellness models rather than choice theory.
D. Survival, love and belonging, power, freedom, and fun ✔ KEY
Glasser named survival, love and belonging, power (achievement), freedom and fun as the five basic needs.
Rationale: Choice theory holds that people choose behaviors to meet five genetically based needs: survival, love and belonging, power or achievement, freedom, and fun. Glasser saw love and belonging as central. Reality therapy helps clients evaluate whether what they are doing is meeting these needs and plan more effective choices.
References: Corey (Theory & Practice) — Reality therapy: choice theory and the five basic needs

356. nce-s-cou-108 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · psychodynamic and psychoanalytic theory · hard · In review

A counselor uses a time-limited psychodynamic approach (such as those of Mann or Strupp and Binder) with a client who has recurring conflicts with authority figures. How does this approach MOST differ from traditional long-term psychoanalysis?
A. Transference is avoided so the work stays short-term
Brief dynamic therapists actively use transference, especially as it relates to the focal pattern.
B. Free association becomes the counselor's main tool
Unstructured free association is typical of classical analysis; brief therapy is more focused and active.
C. Work centers on one focal conflict or relational pattern ✔ KEY
Time-limited dynamic therapies select a single central issue or cyclical maladaptive pattern and keep the work on it.
D. The ending date is left open until the client feels ready
A set time limit, often agreed at the start (Mann used 12 sessions), is a defining feature, and the ending itself is used therapeutically.
Rationale: Brief psychodynamic therapies keep psychoanalytic ideas such as transference and unconscious conflict but limit the number of sessions, have the counselor take a more active role, and select a circumscribed focus, such as Mann's central issue or Strupp and Binder's cyclical maladaptive pattern. The fixed ending is used to work through issues of separation and loss.
References: Corey (Theory & Practice) — Psychoanalytic therapy: brief psychodynamic therapy and time-limited dynamic psychotherapy
☐ Approve☐ Revise☐ RetireNotes:

357. nce-s-cou-109 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · basic attending and listening skills · easy · In review

A reluctant 16-year-old says only that last night "was bad" because of an argument with his mother. The counselor wants to invite him to tell the story in his own words. Which counselor question BEST serves that goal?
A. Were you upset by the argument?
A closed question invites a yes or no and limits how much he says.
B. Why did you yell at her?
"Why" questions can sound accusatory and prompt defensiveness; it also assumes he yelled.
C. Was it about curfew or grades?
An either/or question is closed and imposes the counselor's guesses about the topic.
D. What happened during the argument? ✔ KEY
An open "what" question invites him to describe events in his own words and at his own depth.
Rationale: Open questions, often starting with "what" or "how," encourage clients to elaborate and lead the content. Closed questions are useful for specific facts but narrow the conversation, and "why" questions often provoke defensiveness. Once a client is talking, minimal encouragers such as "mm-hm" help keep the story going.
References: Gladding (Counseling: A Comprehensive Profession) — Counseling skills: open and closed questions

358. nce-s-cou-110 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · basic attending and listening skills · easy · In review

A grieving client finishes describing her mother's final days in hospice, then stops speaking and looks down with tears in her eyes. What is the MOST helpful counselor response in that moment?
A. Allow a silence so she can stay with what she feels ✔ KEY
A respectful, attentive silence gives her room to feel and reflect and signals that the counselor can stay present with her pain.
B. Ask a new question so the pause does not grow awkward
Filling the pause usually serves the counselor's discomfort and pulls her away from her feelings.
C. Offer reassurance that her mother is no longer suffering
Premature reassurance can minimize her grief and close down the emotion she is experiencing.
D. Summarize the session so far and move to coping planning
Shifting to planning at a moment of deep feeling is mistimed and can feel dismissive.
Rationale: Purposeful silence is an attending skill. It allows clients to experience emotion, gather thoughts and decide what to say next, and it communicates acceptance. Beginning counselors often rush to fill silences; the skill lies in reading whether a silence is productive (reflective) or a sign the client is stuck or confused.
References: Gladding (Counseling: A Comprehensive Profession) — Counseling skills: use of silence and attending
☐ Approve☐ Revise☐ RetireNotes:

359. nce-s-cou-111 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · confrontation and immediacy · medium · In review

A client in early recovery asks his counselor, "Have you ever struggled with addiction yourself?" The counselor has been in recovery for many years. Which guideline BEST governs whether and how she self-discloses?
A. Share her own recovery story in full detail, since shared experience builds credibility
Lengthy disclosure shifts the focus to the counselor and can burden the client, whatever its intent.
B. Disclose only if it serves the client, keep it brief, and return the focus to him ✔ KEY
Helpful self-disclosure is purposeful, brief, relevant to the client's goals, and followed by a quick return of the focus to the client.
C. Decline every personal question, because any disclosure is a boundary violation
Self-disclosure is a recognized skill; used sparingly and for the client's benefit, it is not a boundary violation.
D. Disclose only after he asks a second time, to confirm that his interest is sincere
Making him ask twice is not a recognized guideline; the test is whether disclosure serves his welfare.
Rationale: Counselor self-disclosure should be infrequent, brief, relevant and offered for the client's benefit rather than the counselor's needs. After disclosing, the counselor returns the focus to the client, for example by asking how the disclosure lands. Counselors should consider the client's culture, timing and whether the disclosure could blur roles.
References: Gladding (Counseling: A Comprehensive Profession) — Counseling skills: counselor self-disclosure

360. nce-s-cou-112 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · family systems theories (Bowen, structural, strategic) · hard · In review

A father calls his 15-year-old "stubborn," and the counselor replies, "He sounds determined to stand by what he believes." Later, the counselor suggests the son's school refusal may be his way of keeping an eye on a parent he worries about. Which statement BEST distinguishes the two techniques?
A. The first is reframing, which recasts a whole situation; the second is relabeling a single trait
This reverses the terms; swapping one adjective is relabeling, and recasting the meaning of a situation is reframing.
B. The first is interpretation of a defense; the second is a paradoxical prescription
Neither statement interprets a defense or tells anyone to continue the symptom.
C. Both are confrontations of discrepancies between the family's words and actions
Confrontation points out a discrepancy; these statements offer new, more positive meanings instead.
D. The first relabels a negative trait; the second reframes what the behavior means ✔ KEY
Relabeling replaces a negative descriptor with a positive one, while reframing places a behavior in a new context that changes its meaning.
Rationale: Both techniques change perception without changing facts. Relabeling attaches a more positive label to a person or trait ("stubborn" becomes "determined"). Reframing offers a different frame for a whole pattern, often giving a symptom a protective or caring function, which can open new options for the family. Reframing is especially associated with strategic and MRI approaches.
References: Gladding (Counseling: A Comprehensive Profession) — Family counseling techniques: reframing and relabeling
⚑ Check: Verify the reframing vs. relabeling distinction against the Gladding glossary, since some texts treat relabeling as a subtype of reframing.

361. nce-s-cou-113 · Social and Cultural Diversity · multicultural counseling competencies · medium · In review

Pamela Hays's ADDRESSING framework helps counselors consider the many cultural influences shaping a client's identity. In this acronym, what does the first "D" stand for?
A. Developmental disabilities ✔ KEY
The first D is developmental disabilities; the second D is disabilities acquired later in life.
B. Dominant-group status
Privilege runs through the whole framework, but dominant-group status is not a letter in the acronym.
C. Diagnostic history
Diagnostic history is a clinical, not cultural, factor and is not part of ADDRESSING.
D. Dialect and language
Language is considered under national origin and ethnicity; it is not one of the letters.
Rationale: ADDRESSING stands for Age and generational influences, Developmental disabilities, Disabilities acquired later in life, Religion and spiritual orientation, Ethnic and racial identity, Socioeconomic status, Sexual orientation, Indigenous heritage, National origin and Gender. Hays designed it to help counselors see clients' intersecting identities and to examine where the counselor holds privilege.
References: Sue & Sue (Counseling the Culturally Diverse) — Multidimensional identity: Hays's ADDRESSING framework

362. nce-s-cou-114 · Social and Cultural Diversity · multicultural counseling competencies · hard · In review

After years of being passed over at work despite strong reviews, a client says he has little control over his career because of systemic discrimination, and he places responsibility for his situation on that system rather than on himself. In Derald Wing Sue's worldview model, which quadrant BEST fits?
A. Internal control, internal responsibility
IC-IR reflects the mainstream U.S. belief that people control their outcomes and are to blame for failures.
B. External control, external responsibility ✔ KEY
EC-ER describes someone who sees outcomes as controlled by others and attributes problems to systemic forces such as discrimination.
C. External control, internal responsibility
EC-IR describes someone who has little control yet blames himself, often linked to internalized oppression.
D. Internal control, external responsibility
IC-ER describes someone who feels able to shape his life while recognizing systemic barriers, not one who feels little control.
Rationale: Sue crossed locus of control (internal or external) with locus of responsibility (internal or person-centered vs. external or system-centered) to form four worldviews. Counselors who assume an IC-IR view may misread an EC-ER client as resistant or blaming. Understanding the client's worldview, and the real barriers behind it, guides culturally responsive goals such as advocacy.
References: Sue & Sue (Counseling the Culturally Diverse) — Worldview: locus of control and locus of responsibility quadrants

363. nce-s-cou-115 · Social and Cultural Diversity · multicultural counseling competencies · hard · In review

At intake, a counselor briefly asks a Latina client whether she minds working with a counselor of a different ethnicity. He never returns to culture, even when she later describes ethnic bias from her supervisor. In Day-Vines and colleagues' broaching continuum, which style does this MOST reflect?
A. Avoidant
An avoidant counselor does not raise race or culture at all; this counselor did raise it once.
B. Infusing
An infusing counselor builds attention to culture and social justice into all of their work, well beyond the session.
C. Isolating ✔ KEY
An isolating counselor broaches culture in a superficial, one-time way to satisfy an obligation, then drops it.
D. Integrated/congruent
An integrated/congruent counselor broaches routinely and links the client's cultural experiences to the presenting concerns.
Rationale: Broaching is the counselor's consistent, open invitation to explore how race, ethnicity and culture bear on the client's concerns. Day-Vines and colleagues described a continuum of broaching styles: avoidant, isolating, continuing/incongruent, integrated/congruent and infusing. Raising culture once and never connecting it to the client's experience is the isolating style.
References: Sue & Sue (Counseling the Culturally Diverse) — Broaching race and culture in counseling: Day-Vines broaching continuum
⚑ Check: Verify the broaching style names and order against Day-Vines et al. (2007) and confirm Sue & Sue covers them.

364. nce-s-cou-116 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · crisis intervention models · medium · In review

A counselor deployed to a family assistance center after a mass shooting will use Psychological First Aid (PFA) as developed by the National Child Traumatic Stress Network and National Center for PTSD. Which practice is NOT one of the PFA core actions?
A. Linking survivors with needed collaborative services
Linkage with collaborative services is one of the eight PFA core actions.
B. Gathering information on current needs and worries
Information gathering about current needs and concerns is a PFA core action.
C. Offering information on coping with stress reactions
Providing information on coping is a PFA core action.
D. Asking survivors to recount the event in detail ✔ KEY
PFA does not require survivors to describe the event in detail; pressing for a detailed account can be distressing and resembles debriefing, which PFA avoids.
Rationale: PFA's eight core actions are contact and engagement, safety and comfort, stabilization, information gathering on current needs and concerns, practical assistance, connection with social supports, information on coping, and linkage with collaborative services. It is evidence-informed, flexible and non-intrusive, and it does not push survivors to retell or process their trauma.
References: Psychological First Aid Field Operations Guide (NCTSN/NCPTSD) — The eight PFA core actions

365. nce-s-cou-117 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · trauma-informed interventions · medium · In review

A counselor using trauma-focused cognitive behavioral therapy (TF-CBT) with a 9-year-old has completed psychoeducation and parenting skills, relaxation, affective modulation and cognitive coping. Following the PRACTICE sequence, which component comes NEXT?
A. In vivo mastery of trauma reminders
In vivo mastery follows the trauma narrative and is used only when the child avoids safe reminders.
B. Conjoint child-parent sessions
Conjoint sessions, often used to share the narrative with the caregiver, come after the narrative is developed.
C. Enhancing future safety
Enhancing safety is the final component of the PRACTICE sequence.
D. Trauma narrative and processing ✔ KEY
After the stabilization skills, the child gradually develops and processes a narrative of the trauma.
Rationale: TF-CBT, developed by Cohen, Mannarino and Deblinger, follows the PRACTICE components: Psychoeducation and Parenting skills, Relaxation, Affective modulation, Cognitive coping, Trauma narrative and processing, In vivo mastery, Conjoint child-parent sessions and Enhancing safety. Coping skills are built first so the child can tolerate the gradual exposure of the trauma narrative.
References: NICE guidelines — PTSD (NG116): trauma-focused CBT for children and young people
⚑ Check: The PRACTICE order is from Cohen, Mannarino and Deblinger; confirm NICE NG116 is an acceptable citation or swap to a source that describes the model.

366. nce-s-cou-118 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · trauma-informed interventions · medium · In review

After a year at a child advocacy center, a counselor notices lasting changes in her own beliefs: she now sees the world as unsafe, distrusts adults around her own children, and has intrusive images drawn from clients' accounts. Which term BEST describes her experience?
A. Burnout
Burnout is exhaustion, cynicism and reduced effectiveness from workload and work conditions; it need not involve trauma-related changes in beliefs.
B. Vicarious trauma ✔ KEY
Vicarious trauma is a cumulative change in the helper's inner world, such as beliefs about safety and trust, from empathic engagement with clients' trauma.
C. Compassion satisfaction
Compassion satisfaction is the positive fulfillment helpers draw from their work, the opposite of her experience.
D. Countertransference
Countertransference is the counselor's reaction to a particular client rooted in her own history, not a cumulative shift in worldview.
Rationale: Vicarious trauma (McCann and Pearlman) is a lasting shift in a helper's beliefs about safety, trust and control that results from repeated exposure to clients' trauma. Compassion fatigue (Figley) emphasizes the emotional cost of caring, including secondary traumatic stress, while burnout stems from work conditions. Remedies include supervision, caseload balance, peer support and personal self-care.
References: Gladding (Counseling: A Comprehensive Profession) — Counselor wellness: burnout, compassion fatigue and vicarious trauma · ACA Code of Ethics — C.2.g Impairment
☐ Approve☐ Revise☐ RetireNotes:

367. nce-s-cou-119 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · trauma-informed interventions · easy · In review

While talking about an assault, a client begins to dissociate: she stares blankly, speaks in a monotone, and says the room feels "far away." What should the counselor do FIRST?
A. Guide her to name things she can see and feel her feet on the floor ✔ KEY
Grounding techniques that engage the senses and body bring her back into the present moment and her window of tolerance.
B. Encourage her to keep going so the memory can be fully processed today
Continuing while she is dissociated is unlikely to be therapeutic and can overwhelm her further.
C. Ask her to close her eyes and picture the memory in greater detail
Closing her eyes and deepening the memory increases the risk of further dissociation.
D. Interpret her dissociation as resistance to the work of the session
Dissociation is a protective response to overwhelming arousal, not resistance to be interpreted.
Rationale: When a client dissociates or becomes flooded, trauma-informed counselors first re-establish safety and present-moment awareness through grounding, such as the 5-4-3-2-1 senses exercise, feeling the feet on the floor, holding an object or naming the date and place. Trauma processing resumes only when the client is back within her window of tolerance.
References: Gladding (Counseling: A Comprehensive Profession) — Crisis and trauma counseling: grounding and stabilization

368. nce-s-cou-120 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · trauma-informed interventions · medium · In review

In eye movement desensitization and reprocessing (EMDR), the client holds the disturbing image, negative belief and body sensations in mind while the counselor leads sets of eye movements, and distress ratings are taken until they fall toward zero. In Shapiro's eight-phase protocol, which phase is this?
A. Preparation
Preparation builds the relationship, explains EMDR and teaches calming skills before memories are processed.
B. Assessment
Assessment identifies the target image, negative and positive cognitions and baseline ratings without bilateral stimulation.
C. Desensitization ✔ KEY
Desensitization uses sets of bilateral stimulation while the client attends to the target, until the SUD rating approaches 0.
D. Reevaluation
Reevaluation happens at the start of the next session to check whether earlier gains have held.
Rationale: Francine Shapiro's EMDR protocol has eight phases: history taking, preparation, assessment, desensitization, installation, body scan, closure and reevaluation. Bilateral stimulation (eye movements, taps or tones) is used during desensitization, installation of the positive cognition and the body scan. EMDR is recommended by several PTSD practice guidelines.
References: APA CPG — PTSD guideline (2017): EMDR as a conditionally recommended psychotherapy

369. nce-s-cor-001 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · empathy and its levels · medium · In review

A 34-year-old client says, "My boss praised my project in front of everyone, but then gave the promotion to someone else. I don't know what to think." Which counselor response is an interchangeable (Level 3) response on Carkhuff's empathy scale?
A. You should ask your boss directly why you were passed over this time.
Advice-giving ignores the client's feelings entirely; it is subtractive (Level 1 or 2), not interchangeable.
B. You feel confused because the praise and the promotion decision don't match. ✔ KEY
This captures the feeling (confusion) and its stated cause at the same depth the client expressed, which is the definition of a Level 3 interchangeable response.
C. Underneath the confusion, you may fear that your hard work goes unrecognized.
This goes beyond what the client expressed to an underlying feeling and meaning, making it additive (Level 4), not interchangeable.
D. Lots of people get passed over at work, and it usually works out fine in the end.
Reassurance minimizes the experience and shifts away from the client's feelings, a subtractive response.
Rationale: Carkhuff's scale rates counselor responses from 1 to 5. Level 3 is the minimally facilitative, interchangeable level: the counselor reflects essentially the same feeling and meaning the client expressed, adding nothing and taking nothing away. Levels 1 and 2 subtract from the client's message, and Levels 4 and 5 add depth beyond it.
References: Gladding (Counseling: A Comprehensive Profession) — Carkhuff's scale of empathic understanding, Levels 1-5
☐ Approve☐ Revise☐ RetireNotes:

370. nce-s-cor-002 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · empathy and its levels · hard · In review

In a sixth session, a graduate student says, "I keep telling everyone I'm fine with moving home after graduation. My parents are happy, so I guess I should be too." Which counselor response BEST illustrates additive (Level 4) empathy?
A. So you're moving home after graduation, and your parents are pleased with it.
This paraphrases the content but omits feeling; it is at best Level 2, slightly subtractive.
B. You feel fine about moving home, since your parents are happy with the plan.
This repeats the client's surface statement and misses the doubt in "I guess I should be"; it does not add depth.
C. Why do you think your parents' happiness matters so much in this decision?
A why-question invites intellectualizing and does not communicate understanding of the feeling, so it is not an empathic response.
D. You sound unsure, as if your own wishes got set aside to keep your parents happy. ✔ KEY
This names the implicit ambivalence and the meaning beneath it (setting aside one's own wishes), adding accurately to what the client said: Level 4.
Rationale: Additive empathy (Carkhuff Levels 4-5) goes beyond the client's explicit words to reflect feelings and meanings the client implied but did not fully state, which helps the client explore at a deeper level. To be additive rather than inaccurate, the reflection must stay grounded in cues the client gave, such as the hedged "I guess I should be."
References: Gladding (Counseling: A Comprehensive Profession) — Advanced (additive) empathy, Carkhuff Levels 4-5
⚑ Check: Option A is rated Level 2 in its rationale; raters may disagree on the exact level (it is clearly not additive).
☐ Approve☐ Revise☐ RetireNotes:

371. nce-s-cor-003 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · empathy and its levels · easy · In review

On Carkhuff's five-level scale of empathic understanding, a counselor response rated at Level 1 or Level 2 is described as which of the following?
A. Subtractive, detracting from what the client expressed ✔ KEY
Levels 1 and 2 are subtractive: they ignore or only partly capture the client's feelings and meaning.
B. Interchangeable, matching the client's expressed feeling
Interchangeable, minimally facilitative responses are Level 3.
C. Additive, reflecting feelings below the client's words
Additive responses that reach underlying feelings are Levels 4 and 5.
D. Facilitative, the baseline goal for beginning counselors
Level 3 is the minimally facilitative baseline counselors aim to reach consistently; Levels 1-2 fall below it.
Rationale: Carkhuff's scale runs from Level 1 (the response ignores or contradicts the client's feelings) to Level 5 (the response adds significantly to the client's awareness). Levels 1-2 are subtractive, Level 3 is interchangeable and minimally facilitative, and Levels 4-5 are additive.
References: Gladding (Counseling: A Comprehensive Profession) — Carkhuff's scale of empathic understanding
☐ Approve☐ Revise☐ RetireNotes:

372. nce-s-cor-004 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · empathy and its levels · easy · In review

A client tearfully describes the recent death of her dog. Which counselor response reflects empathy rather than sympathy?
A. I'm so sorry; I lost my own dog last year and I still cry about it sometimes too.
This shifts the focus to the counselor's own loss and expresses pity or shared distress, which is sympathy and self-disclosure, not empathy.
B. That is such a sad thing to go through, and I feel terrible for you today.
Feeling sorry for the client is sympathy; it conveys the counselor's feeling rather than understanding of the client's.
C. Losing her has left a real emptiness in your days, and the grief feels heavy. ✔ KEY
This communicates accurate understanding of the client's own experience from her frame of reference, which is empathy.
D. At least she had a long life with you, and you gave her a very good home.
"At least" reassurance minimizes the loss and moves away from the client's feelings.
Rationale: Empathy is understanding the client's world as if it were one's own, without losing the "as if" quality, and communicating that understanding back. Sympathy centers on the helper's own feelings of sorrow or pity for the client, which can shift the focus away from the client.
References: Corey (Theory & Practice) — Person-centered therapy: accurate empathic understanding
☐ Approve☐ Revise☐ RetireNotes:

373. nce-s-cor-005 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · unconditional positive regard · medium · In review

A 52-year-old client says, "I yelled at my teenage son last night and called him useless. I'm ashamed to even tell you." Which counselor response BEST demonstrates unconditional positive regard?
A. That was harsh, but I'm sure you are a good parent most of the time anyway.
Reassurance about being a "good parent" is conditional praise; it evaluates the client rather than accepting the client.
B. Calling him useless can do real damage, so let's plan what to say instead.
This jumps to evaluation and correction before the client feels accepted; it is not an expression of UPR.
C. It took courage to share that, and I'm glad you trusted me with it here. ✔ KEY
This accepts the client as a person, without approving or condemning the behavior, and values the client's openness, which is UPR.
D. Don't be ashamed, since all parents lose their temper and say things they regret.
Telling the client not to feel shame dismisses the feeling and normalizes too quickly; it is reassurance, not acceptance.
Rationale: Rogers described unconditional positive regard as a warm acceptance of the client as a person, without conditions of worth. UPR does not mean approving of every behavior; it means the client's worth is not contingent on what is disclosed, which lowers defensiveness and supports self-exploration.
References: Corey (Theory & Practice) — Person-centered therapy: unconditional positive regard and acceptance
☐ Approve☐ Revise☐ RetireNotes:

374. nce-s-cor-006 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · congruence and genuineness · medium · In review

Midway through a session, a client asks, "You look distracted. Are you even listening to me?" The counselor realizes her attention had drifted. Which response BEST demonstrates congruence?
A. You're right, my mind wandered for a moment. I'm with you now; please go on. ✔ KEY
The counselor's words match her inner experience, she owns it briefly, and she returns the focus to the client, which is congruence.
B. Of course I'm listening, and I have been following everything you've said.
Denying a lapse the counselor knows occurred is incongruent; outward words do not match inner experience.
C. It sounds like you worry people don't listen to you. Tell me more about that.
Reflecting the client's concern here deflects from the counselor's actual lapse and avoids genuineness.
D. I've had a long, stressful day with other clients, so I'm tired and distracted.
This is honest but over-discloses and shifts the focus to the counselor's needs rather than the relationship.
Rationale: Congruence (genuineness) means the counselor's outward behavior is consistent with inner experience, without a professional facade. Rogers viewed it as the most basic of the core conditions. Being genuine does not require disclosing every feeling; the counselor shares what is relevant to the relationship and keeps the focus on the client.
References: Corey (Theory & Practice) — Person-centered therapy: congruence or genuineness
☐ Approve☐ Revise☐ RetireNotes:

375. nce-s-cor-007 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · therapeutic alliance · easy · In review

Bordin's pantheoretical model of the working alliance consists of which three components?
A. Empathy, congruence, and positive regard
These are Rogers' core conditions, not Bordin's alliance components.
B. Rapport, insight, and corrective feedback
These are general therapy processes, not the components Bordin named.
C. Trust, transference, and client motivation
Transference is a psychodynamic concept; Bordin's model was explicitly pantheoretical.
D. Agreement on goals, tasks, and the bond ✔ KEY
Bordin defined the working alliance as agreement on goals, agreement on tasks, and the emotional bond between counselor and client.
Rationale: Bordin (1979) proposed that the working alliance has three parts: agreement on the goals of counseling, agreement on the tasks used to reach them, and the affective bond between counselor and client. Because it is pantheoretical, the model applies across approaches, and alliance strength is one of the most consistent predictors of outcome.
References: Gladding (Counseling: A Comprehensive Profession) — The working alliance: goals, tasks, and bond

376. nce-s-cor-008 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · therapeutic alliance · hard · In review

In the fourth session, a client who has been completing CBT thought records says flatly, "I did the homework again, I guess. Whatever helps you." The client has arrived late twice. What should the counselor do FIRST?
A. Review the thought record in detail and praise the client for completing it
Proceeding with the task ignores the withdrawal cues and leaves a possible rupture unaddressed.
B. Invite the client to talk about how the work together is feeling so far ✔ KEY
Openly exploring the relationship addresses a likely withdrawal rupture and checks agreement on tasks and goals before continuing.
C. Set a lateness policy so that attendance does not continue to slip further
Focusing on rules treats the lateness as a compliance issue and can deepen the rupture.
D. Switch to a different intervention, since thought records are not working
Changing tasks unilaterally skips collaboration; the counselor does not yet know what the client wants or is reacting to.
Rationale: Flat compliance ("whatever helps you") and lateness suggest a withdrawal rupture in the alliance, often reflecting disagreement about tasks or goals or strain in the bond. Research on rupture and repair supports raising the relationship directly and nondefensively, which lets the counselor renegotiate tasks and goals collaboratively. Successfully repaired ruptures are associated with better outcomes.
References: Gladding (Counseling: A Comprehensive Profession) — Working alliance: goals, tasks, bond; addressing alliance ruptures
⚑ Check: Rupture-repair evidence (Safran et al.) is cited to a general Gladding alliance reference.

377. nce-s-cor-009 · Social and Cultural Diversity · cultural humility · medium · In review

A counselor who has completed many multicultural trainings begins work with a Hmong American client. According to Hook and colleagues' model of cultural humility, which stance BEST reflects cultural humility?
A. Ask the client what their culture means to them and stay open to being corrected ✔ KEY
Cultural humility is an other-oriented, curious stance that treats the client as the expert on their own cultural identity.
B. Draw on prior training about Hmong customs to anticipate the client's values
Applying general group knowledge as a template risks stereotyping; it reflects a knowledge-based, not humble, stance.
C. Set cultural issues aside unless the client raises them first in the session
Waiting for the client to raise culture places the burden on the client and can signal that culture is unwelcome.
D. Treat the training already completed as sufficient competence for this client
Viewing competence as a finished achievement contradicts the ongoing, lifelong nature of humility.
Rationale: Hook et al. (2013) described cultural humility as an interpersonal stance that is other-oriented rather than self-focused, marked by respect, openness, and lack of superiority regarding the client's cultural background. It is an ongoing process of self-reflection rather than a fixed body of knowledge, and clients' perceptions of it are associated with a stronger alliance.
References: Sue & Sue (Counseling the Culturally Diverse) — Cultural humility as an other-oriented stance
⚑ Check: Confirm the cited Sue & Sue edition discusses Hook et al.'s cultural humility model.
☐ Approve☐ Revise☐ RetireNotes:

378. nce-s-cor-010 · Social and Cultural Diversity · counselor self-awareness and bias · medium · In review

A counselor notices that she schedules a client who lives in public housing for shorter sessions and assigns fewer homework tasks, assuming the client "won't follow through." What is the MOST appropriate next step?
A. Refer the client to a counselor who has more experience with low-income clients
Referral based on the counselor's bias, rather than competence limits, abandons the client and avoids the counselor's own work.
B. Continue as planned, since shorter sessions may reduce the client's burden
This rationalizes the bias instead of examining it and keeps treating the client differently.
C. Examine the assumption in supervision and restore an equitable treatment plan ✔ KEY
Recognizing an implicit bias, exploring it in supervision, and correcting its effect on care reflects counselor self-awareness.
D. Tell the client about the bias so the client can decide whether to continue
Disclosing the bias burdens the client with the counselor's issue; the counselor should first address it through supervision.
Rationale: Counselor self-awareness of attitudes, beliefs, and biases is the first dimension of multicultural competence. Biases about social class can shape clinical decisions without conscious intent. When a counselor notices differential treatment, the ethical response is to examine it, often through supervision or consultation, and correct its effect on services.
References: Sue & Sue (Counseling the Culturally Diverse) — Counselor awareness of own assumptions, values, and biases · ACA Code of Ethics — C.5 Nondiscrimination

379. nce-s-cor-011 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · countertransference · easy · In review

A counselor notices he feels unusually irritated and impatient with an older male client who reminds him of his own critical father. This reaction is BEST described as
A. transference by the client
Transference is the client's projection of past relationships onto the counselor; here the reaction is the counselor's.
B. countertransference ✔ KEY
The counselor's emotional reaction to the client, shaped by his own unresolved history, is countertransference.
C. resistance in the client
Resistance refers to client behavior that impedes change, not the counselor's feelings.
D. compassion fatigue
Compassion fatigue is cumulative exhaustion from trauma exposure, not a reaction tied to a specific personal history.
Rationale: Countertransference refers to the counselor's emotional reactions to a client, particularly those rooted in the counselor's own unresolved conflicts or past relationships. It is not inherently harmful, but unexamined countertransference can distort the counselor's perceptions and responses, so recognizing it is the first step to managing it.
References: Corey (Theory & Practice) — Psychoanalytic therapy: transference and countertransference
