380. nce-s-cor-012 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · countertransference · medium · In review

A counselor whose sister died by overdose finds herself giving extra session time and her personal cell number to a young adult client in early recovery. What is the BEST course of action?
A. Keep the extra contact, since it clearly helps the client stay engaged
This keeps a boundary crossing driven by the counselor's needs rather than the client's welfare.
B. Transfer the client right away to avoid any further personal reactions
Abrupt transfer is premature; countertransference can often be managed and abrupt referral may harm the client.
C. Disclose the sister's death so the client understands her strong concern
This self-disclosure serves the counselor's needs and burdens the client.
D. Seek supervision on the reaction and restore usual session boundaries ✔ KEY
Recognizing the countertransference, processing it in supervision, and restoring boundaries protects the client and the work.
Rationale: Countertransference can show up as overinvolvement, rescuing, or boundary crossings that meet the counselor's needs. Effective management involves self-awareness, supervision or personal counseling, and returning to boundaries that serve the client. Referral is reserved for cases where the counselor cannot remain objective after these steps.
References: Corey (Theory & Practice) — Countertransference and the counselor as a person · ACA Code of Ethics — A.6.b Extending Conventional Boundaries

381. nce-s-cor-013 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · countertransference · hard · In review

A supervisor tells a trainee, "Every feeling you have toward a client, not just the conflicts from your own past, can be useful information about the client and the relationship." This statement reflects which view of countertransference?
A. Classical view, treating it as an obstacle to be eliminated
The classical (Freudian) view limits countertransference to the counselor's unresolved conflicts and treats it as interference.
B. Behavioral view, treating it as a learned response to extinguish
Countertransference is not framed as a conditioned response in behavioral theory; this distractor misattributes the concept.
C. Complementary view, limited to the client's projected roles
The complementary conception frames countertransference as the counselor's reaction to the interpersonal pull of the client's style, a narrower idea than all feelings toward the client.
D. Totalistic view, including all counselor reactions to clients ✔ KEY
The totalistic view defines countertransference as all of the counselor's emotional reactions and sees them as potentially useful data.
Rationale: The classical view, rooted in Freud, defines countertransference narrowly as the counselor's unconscious, conflict-based reactions to the client's transference and treats it as an impediment. The totalistic view broadens it to all of the counselor's emotional responses and treats them as a source of understanding. Contemporary integrative views keep the focus on conflict-based reactions while stressing that, once understood, they can inform the work.
References: Corey (Theory & Practice) — Psychoanalytic therapy: countertransference and its therapeutic use
⚑ Check: Classical/totalistic/complementary views come from Gelso & Hayes; confirm Corey covers the totalistic view or swap the source.

382. nce-s-cor-014 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · nonjudgmental stance · medium · In review

A college student says, "I've been hooking up with different people most weekends, and my roommate thinks I'm a mess." Which counselor response BEST reflects a nonjudgmental stance?
A. Your roommate may have a point, so let's look at the risks you're taking.
Siding with the roommate conveys the counselor's judgment before understanding the client's view.
B. Your roommate sees it one way. I'm curious how you feel about these weekends. ✔ KEY
This neither condemns nor endorses the behavior and invites the client's own perspective, which is nonjudgmental.
C. There's nothing wrong with that at all, and your roommate should not judge you.
Approval is also an evaluation; it imposes the counselor's values in the opposite direction.
D. What do you think is missing in your life that leads you to keep doing this?
This presumes the behavior signals a deficit, a judgment embedded in the question.
Rationale: A nonjudgmental stance means the counselor suspends both disapproval and approval and seeks to understand the client's experience from the client's frame of reference. Counselors avoid imposing their own values on clients, which allows clients to explore their choices without becoming defensive.
References: ACA Code of Ethics — A.4.b Personal Values

383. nce-s-cor-015 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · warmth and respect · easy · In review

A 70-year-old widower arrives early for his first session and seems nervous. Which counselor behavior BEST conveys warmth and respect?
A. Greet him by name, ask how he prefers to be addressed, and offer him a seat ✔ KEY
This shows genuine interest and respects his preferences and dignity, communicating warmth and respect.
B. Call him "sweetie" and speak slowly and loudly so he can follow along easily
Endearments and exaggerated speech are elderspeak; they are patronizing and disrespectful.
C. Hand him the intake forms and ask him to wait until his appointment time
This is procedural and distant, and it ignores his visible nervousness.
D. Tell him there is no need to be nervous because most clients feel fine later
This dismisses his feelings and offers reassurance in place of attunement.
Rationale: Warmth is conveyed through friendly, caring verbal and nonverbal behavior, and respect is shown by treating the client as a capable person with preferences and dignity. Asking how a client wishes to be addressed is a simple, culturally responsive way to communicate both, and avoiding ageist communication such as elderspeak is part of respect.
References: Gladding (Counseling: A Comprehensive Profession) — Building a counseling relationship: warmth and respect

384. nce-s-cor-016 · Social and Cultural Diversity · awareness of power and privilege in the counseling relationship · medium · In review

A White counselor working with a Black client hears the client say, "I wasn't sure I could talk about racism at work with you." Which counselor response BEST shows awareness of power and privilege in the relationship?
A. I don't really see color, so you can talk to me about anything you need to here.
Color-blind statements deny the relevance of race and the client's concern, which is a microinvalidation.
B. I've had many Black clients, so I understand a lot about what you face at work.
Claiming understanding based on experience with other clients overstates the counselor's knowledge and dismisses the concern.
C. As a White counselor I may miss things, and I'd like to hear it if you're willing. ✔ KEY
This names the racial difference and the limits of the counselor's perspective, and invites the client's experience without pressure.
D. Let's focus on what you can control at work, since we can't change the people there.
Redirecting away from racism minimizes a systemic experience and avoids the topic the client raised.
Rationale: Counselors from privileged groups hold both professional and social power. Broaching racial and cultural differences directly and nondefensively, and acknowledging the limits of one's own perspective, signals that the client's experiences of oppression are welcome. Color-blind or minimizing responses are common microaggressions that weaken trust.
References: Sue & Sue (Counseling the Culturally Diverse) — Power, privilege, and microaggressions in counseling; color-blindness

385. nce-s-cor-017 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · empathy and its levels · medium · In review

A 41-year-old man ordered to counseling after a road-rage incident sits down and says, "This is a joke. The other guy cut me off and I'm the one stuck here wasting my Thursday." Which counselor response BEST conveys empathy?
A. The court decided you need to be here, so let's make good use of the hour today.
This sides with the court and moves to the agenda without acknowledging the client's feelings, a subtractive response.
B. Getting angry on the road is risky, and that is what we need to look at together.
This confronts the behavior before the client feels heard, which tends to increase defensiveness in a mandated client.
C. You're frustrated, because it feels unfair that you're the one paying the price. ✔ KEY
This accurately reflects the feeling (frustration) and its meaning (unfairness) from the client's frame of reference without agreeing or arguing.
D. I hear that, but many people find these sessions useful once they give them a try.
"I hear that, but" discounts the feeling and shifts to persuasion, which is reassurance rather than empathy.
Rationale: Empathy with an angry or mandated client means communicating understanding of the client's feelings and perspective, not defending the referral source or confronting the behavior first. An accurate reflection lowers defensiveness and builds the alliance needed for later work on the behavior that led to the referral.
References: Gladding (Counseling: A Comprehensive Profession) — Empathy and working with reluctant or resistant clients

386. nce-s-cor-018 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · empathy and its levels · hard · In review

A client says, "My sister borrowed money again and didn't pay it back. She's completely selfish, and anyone in my place would cut her off for good." Which counselor response communicates empathy WITHOUT signaling agreement with the client's judgment?
A. You feel hurt and fed up, and cutting her off seems like the only way to stop it. ✔ KEY
This reflects the client's feelings and how the situation looks to the client, conveying understanding without endorsing the conclusion that the sister is selfish.
B. She does sound selfish, and you'd be right to protect yourself from her by now.
This agrees with the client's evaluation of the sister, confusing empathy with agreement and taking sides.
C. Maybe your sister is struggling in ways that you might not know about yet, though.
Offering the sister's side challenges the client prematurely and communicates that the counselor does not understand the client.
D. Cutting off family is a big step, so let's think carefully before you decide.
This shifts to cautionary advice and skips reflecting the client's feelings, so it is not an empathic response.
Rationale: Empathy is understanding the client's world "as if" it were one's own while keeping the "as if" quality. It reflects the client's feelings and perceptions as the client experiences them; it neither agrees with nor disputes the client's judgments. Distinguishing empathy from agreement lets the counselor stay with the client without becoming an ally against third parties.
References: Corey (Theory & Practice) — Person-centered therapy: accurate empathic understanding and the "as if" quality

387. nce-s-cor-019 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · unconditional positive regard · medium · In review

A 29-year-old client who left a stable nursing job to work as a touring musician says she is happy with the choice, though money is tight. The counselor privately thinks the decision is unwise. Which approach BEST reflects unconditional positive regard and respect for client autonomy?
A. Point out the financial risks so the client can reconsider before it is too late
Steering the client toward the counselor's preferred choice imposes the counselor's values on a decision the client has not asked to revisit.
B. Accept her choice as hers to make and explore the goals she brings to counseling ✔ KEY
Valuing the client without conditions and following her own goals honors UPR and self-determination, even when the counselor would choose differently.
C. Praise the decision warmly so the client feels supported in following her dream
Praise is an evaluation too; UPR is acceptance of the person, not approval of choices, and the praise would be incongruent with the counselor's view.
D. Share the honest concern about the career change since genuineness matters most
Genuineness does not require voicing every personal opinion; volunteering disapproval of a lawful life choice imposes values.
Rationale: Unconditional positive regard means prizing the client as a person regardless of whether the counselor agrees with the client's choices. Combined with respect for self-determination, it means counselors do not impose personal values on clients' lawful life decisions and instead work on the goals clients bring.
References: Corey (Theory & Practice) — Person-centered therapy: unconditional positive regard and acceptance · ACA Code of Ethics — A.4.b Personal Values

388. nce-s-cor-020 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · unconditional positive regard · easy · In review

Rogers held that when a counselor provides unconditional positive regard, empathy, and congruence, clients move toward growth because of an innate drive to develop their capacities. He called this drive the
A. striving for superiority
Striving for superiority (or significance) is Adler's concept of the movement toward overcoming felt inferiority.
B. will to meaning
The will to meaning is Frankl's existential concept, central to logotherapy.
C. hierarchy of needs
The hierarchy of needs is Maslow's model of motivation, not Rogers' term for the growth drive.
D. actualizing tendency ✔ KEY
Rogers described the actualizing tendency as the organism's inherent tendency to develop its capacities in ways that maintain and enhance it.
Rationale: Person-centered theory rests on trust in the actualizing tendency, an inborn movement toward growth and fulfillment. The counselor does not direct change; instead, the core conditions create a climate in which the client's own actualizing tendency can operate and conditions of worth lose their hold.
References: Corey (Theory & Practice) — Person-centered therapy: the actualizing tendency

389. nce-s-cor-021 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · congruence and genuineness · medium · In review

A client describes years of failed fertility treatments. The counselor went through a similar loss and feels a wave of sadness. Which response BEST reflects genuineness and intentional use of self without inappropriate self-disclosure?
A. I'm moved hearing how much hope and heartbreak these years have held for you. ✔ KEY
The counselor shares an authentic, here-and-now reaction that is relevant to the client and keeps the focus on the client's experience.
B. I went through fertility treatments too, so I know exactly how painful this is.
This disclosure shifts focus to the counselor and claims identical experience, which can silence the client's own version of the loss.
C. Let's look at the coping strategies that have helped you through these years.
Moving straight to coping sidesteps the emotion in the room; the counselor masks her reaction rather than using it.
D. Many couples face this, and there are other paths to parenthood worth exploring.
Normalizing and problem-solving prematurely minimizes the grief and does not reflect a genuine, attuned response.
Rationale: Congruence means the counselor's outward expression matches inner experience, without a professional facade. Genuineness does not require telling one's personal history; intentional use of self means sharing reactions only when they serve the client. Disclosures of the counselor's own life should be brief, purposeful, and returned quickly to the client.
References: Corey (Theory & Practice) — Person-centered therapy: congruence; therapist self-disclosure

390. nce-s-cor-022 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · congruence and genuineness · easy · In review

During a video session, a client says, "On this screen it's hard to tell whether what I say really lands with you." Which counselor adjustment BEST supports genuineness in telehealth?
A. Ask more questions so the client can see the counselor is paying close attention
More questions do not convey the counselor's reactions and may feel like an interview rather than a genuine relationship.
B. Reassure the client that the counselor is listening carefully even if it is hard to see
Reassurance asks the client to trust without changing anything about how the counselor's presence comes across.
C. Put more reactions into words, since the camera filters out many nonverbal cues ✔ KEY
Verbalizing reactions compensates for the reduced nonverbal channel so the client can experience the counselor as present and authentic.
D. Suggest switching to phone sessions so that video issues stop being a distraction
Phone sessions remove even more nonverbal information and avoid the client's concern rather than addressing it.
Rationale: Video sessions reduce the nonverbal cues, such as posture, small facial changes, and eye contact, that usually convey a counselor's presence. To remain genuine and transparent, counselors can name their reactions more explicitly, look toward the camera, and check in about how the connection feels to the client.

391. nce-s-cor-023 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · therapeutic alliance · medium · In review

A 15-year-old brought to counseling by his mother crosses his arms and says, "She's the one who wanted this. I don't have anything to talk about." Which counselor response is MOST likely to begin building a working alliance?
A. Your mom is really worried about you, so it would help to tell me what has been going on.
This aligns the counselor with the parent's agenda and pressures the teen, weakening the bond.
B. Coming here wasn't your idea. I'm curious if anything would make this worth it to you. ✔ KEY
This acknowledges the teen's lack of choice and invites his own goals, building agreement on goals and the bond.
C. That's fine. We can just sit quietly until you decide you're ready to start talking.
Passive waiting leaves the teen without a reason to engage and does nothing to establish shared goals.
D. Most teens feel that way at first, but they usually end up liking counseling a lot.
Generalized reassurance dismisses his stated position and can sound like the counselor is selling something.
Rationale: Adolescents brought by parents often arrive reluctant. The alliance grows when the counselor acknowledges the teen's autonomy, separates from the parent's agenda, and invites the teen to name goals of his own. In Bordin's terms, this begins building agreement on goals and the bond.
References: Gladding (Counseling: A Comprehensive Profession) — Working alliance with reluctant clients and adolescents

392. nce-s-cor-024 · Social and Cultural Diversity · awareness of power and privilege in the counseling relationship · hard · In review

A court-mandated client on probation asks at intake, "So whatever I say in here goes straight to my probation officer, right?" The client has signed a release limited to attendance and progress reports. What is the BEST response?
A. Reassure the client that sessions are confidential so that he will speak more freely
Promising full confidentiality is inaccurate given the signed release and would undermine trust when reports are sent.
B. Tell the client that the probation officer will decide what is shared from sessions
The scope of disclosure is set by the release and law, not by the officer; this also cedes the client's informed role.
C. Suggest that the client avoid sharing anything that could get him into legal trouble
This advises concealment and undermines the purpose of counseling instead of clarifying the actual limits.
D. Explain exactly what the release permits to be shared and invite questions about it ✔ KEY
Transparent, specific information about the limits of confidentiality acknowledges the power imbalance and supports informed participation.
Rationale: Mandated clients face a real power imbalance: the counselor reports to a system that holds consequences over them. Counselors address this by explaining clearly, at the start and as needed, what will and will not be disclosed and to whom. Honest transparency about the counselor's dual accountability is the foundation for trust in mandated work.
References: ACA Code of Ethics — B.1.d Explanation of Limitations

393. nce-s-cor-025 · Social and Cultural Diversity · cultural humility · medium · In review

In a first session, a counselor told a U.S.-born Korean American client, "Your English is excellent." The client became quiet for the rest of the hour. At the next session, what should the counselor do FIRST?
A. Wait to see whether the client brings up the comment before addressing it in session
Waiting puts the burden of raising the harm on the client and lets the rupture persist.
B. Explain that the remark was meant as a compliment and was not intended to offend
Defending intent centers the counselor and minimizes the impact on the client.
C. Name the comment, take responsibility for its impact, and invite the client's view ✔ KEY
Nondefensively acknowledging a microaggression and inviting the client's experience is the core of rupture repair with cultural humility.
D. Focus on the presenting problem so the session is not sidetracked by the earlier slip
Moving on avoids the rupture; unaddressed microaggressions are linked to weaker alliances and dropout.
Rationale: Remarks that assume an Asian American is a foreigner ("alien in one's own land") are a recognized microaggression. When a counselor commits one, cultural humility calls for owning the impact rather than defending intent, and openly inviting the client's reaction. Repairing such ruptures can strengthen the relationship.
References: Sue & Sue (Counseling the Culturally Diverse) — Racial microaggressions: alien in one's own land

394. nce-s-cor-026 · Social and Cultural Diversity · cultural humility · hard · In review

A second-generation Nigerian American counselor begins working with a 24-year-old woman who immigrated from Nigeria two years ago. The counselor thinks, "I grew up with this, so I already know what her parents expect of her." Which stance BEST reflects cultural humility?
A. Draw on shared heritage to explain the family expectations the client is facing
Using one's own background as a template assumes sameness and replaces the client's account with the counselor's.
B. Ask how she sees her family's expectations and stay open to differences from her own ✔ KEY
This treats the client as the expert on her experience and stays alert to differences in generation, region, ethnicity, and immigration history.
C. Refer her to a counselor of another culture to avoid any bias from the shared heritage
A shared background is not grounds for referral; the task is to examine assumptions, not avoid the client.
D. Disclose the shared background early so the client knows she will be understood
Promising understanding on the basis of shared heritage reinforces the assumption of sameness.
Rationale: Cultural humility applies even when counselor and client share a cultural background. Within-group differences in generation, acculturation, region, language, religion, and personal values can be large, and assuming sameness can lead to blind spots or overidentification. A humble, curious stance invites the client to define what her culture means to her.
References: Sue & Sue (Counseling the Culturally Diverse) — Within-group differences and the dangers of assumed similarity

395. nce-s-cor-027 · Social and Cultural Diversity · counselor self-awareness and bias · medium · In review

A counselor with strong religious convictions works with a client who wants help deciding whether to leave her faith community. The counselor notices a pull to encourage the client to stay. What is the MOST appropriate action?
A. Refer the client elsewhere, because the counselor's beliefs conflict with the goal
The ACA Code bars referral based solely on the counselor's personal values; the counselor must first work to bracket them.
B. Share the counselor's own beliefs openly so the client can weigh them in deciding
Offering personal religious views on the client's decision imposes the counselor's values.
C. Gently point out what the client may lose by leaving her supportive community
Emphasizing losses of only one option steers the client toward the counselor's preference.
D. Seek consultation, set aside personal views, and follow the client's own process ✔ KEY
Recognizing the pull, getting consultation, and keeping the focus on the client's goals reflect self-awareness and A.4.b.
Rationale: Self-awareness includes noticing when personal religious or political values press on clinical work. The ACA Code directs counselors to be aware of their own values and avoid imposing them, seeking training where they are at risk of doing so, and it prohibits referral based solely on the counselor's personal values.

396. nce-s-cor-028 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · countertransference · easy · In review

A counselor whose parents had a bitter divorce works with a 16-year-old caught in a custody dispute. She finds herself angry at the client's father and urging the teen to "stand up to him." This pattern is BEST described as
A. overidentification ✔ KEY
The counselor is merging her own history with the client's, losing objectivity and taking sides, a form of countertransference.
B. parallel process
Parallel process refers to dynamics in counseling being replicated in supervision, or the reverse.
C. advocacy
Advocacy addresses systemic barriers on behalf of a client; it is not driven by the counselor's unresolved personal history.
D. client transference
Transference is the client's projection onto the counselor; here the reaction originates in the counselor.
Rationale: Overidentification occurs when a counselor sees the client's situation through the lens of his or her own experience, losing the "as if" boundary of empathy. It is a common form of countertransference that can lead the counselor to take sides or push the client toward choices the counselor wished for, so it calls for self-reflection and supervision.

397. nce-s-cor-029 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · nonjudgmental stance · easy · In review

A client says, "I still smoke weed most nights to fall asleep. Go ahead and give me the lecture." Which counselor response BEST reflects a nonjudgmental stance?
A. No lecture. I'd like to understand what it does for you and how it's working. ✔ KEY
This sets aside evaluation and invites the client's own view of benefits and costs, a nonjudgmental and collaborative stance.
B. I won't lecture, but you know nightly use can make your sleep worse over time.
The "but" delivers the lecture anyway, conveying judgment and likely increasing defensiveness.
C. It sounds like you already know it's a problem, or you wouldn't expect a lecture.
This interprets the comment as an admission of a problem, imposing the counselor's conclusion.
D. Lots of people use cannabis for sleep now, so it isn't a big concern for me.
Dismissing the topic is approval, another kind of judgment, and closes off exploration.
Rationale: A nonjudgmental stance suspends both disapproval and approval in favor of curiosity about the client's experience. With substance use, exploring what the substance does for the client and how it is working invites honest self-evaluation and is consistent with motivational interviewing's spirit of acceptance.
References: Miller & Rollnick (MI) — Spirit of MI: acceptance (absolute worth, autonomy support)

398. nce-s-cor-030 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · nonjudgmental stance · medium · In review

A 33-year-old single mother mentions in passing that she does sex work to pay her bills, then says she came to counseling because of anxiety about her son's problems at school. What should the counselor do?
A. Shift the focus to her work, since it is likely the underlying source of her anxiety
Assuming the work is the real problem overrides the client's stated goal and reflects the counselor's judgment.
B. Follow her stated concern while leaving room to discuss work or safety if she wishes ✔ KEY
Respecting the client's agenda without ignoring possible safety needs reflects a nonjudgmental, client-centered stance.
C. Give her referrals for job training so she has other options to support her family
Unrequested job referrals imply her work must change, imposing values she did not ask the counselor to apply.
D. Avoid any mention of her work so that she does not feel judged in this session
Treating the topic as unspeakable can also signal judgment and may miss safety concerns she wants to raise.
Rationale: A nonjudgmental stance means the counselor does not treat a stigmatized behavior as the presumed problem. The client sets the agenda, and the counselor stays open to discussing work, health, or safety when the client chooses, without pathologizing or avoiding the topic.
References: ACA Code of Ethics — A.4.b Personal Values

399. nce-s-cor-031 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · warmth and respect · easy · In review

A client arrives sobbing and breathing rapidly after learning she is being evicted at the end of the week. Which counselor response BEST conveys warmth in this moment?
A. Hand her a list of housing resources so she can start making calls right away
Resources may help later, but moving to tasks while she is flooded ignores her emotional state.
B. Ask her to describe the eviction notice in detail to see what her options are
Detailed fact-gathering before she is calmer is task-focused and can add to her distress.
C. Tell her firmly that panicking will not help and that she needs to calm down
A directive to stop panicking is cold and dismissive, and it rarely reduces distress.
D. Sit with her, speak in a calm, steady voice, and acknowledge how scared she is ✔ KEY
A calm, caring presence and acknowledgment of her fear communicate warmth and help her regulate before problem-solving.
Rationale: Warmth is communicated largely through nonverbal behavior, including tone of voice, pacing, and attentive presence, together with acknowledgment of the client's feelings. In acute distress, a warm and steady presence helps the client regulate enough to engage in the practical problem-solving that follows.

400. nce-s-cor-032 · Social and Cultural Diversity · awareness of power and privilege in the counseling relationship · medium · In review

A counselor is meeting with a Spanish-speaking client through a trained professional interpreter. Which practice BEST preserves the client's voice and respects the counseling relationship?
A. Ask the interpreter to summarize what the client says to keep sessions brief
Summaries filter the client's words through the interpreter and lose emotional content.
B. Ask the client's bilingual teenage son to interpret, since the family trusts him
Family members, especially children, compromise accuracy and confidentiality and shift family power roles.
C. Speak directly to the client in the first person rather than to the interpreter ✔ KEY
Addressing the client directly keeps her at the center of the relationship and the interpreter in a conduit role.
D. Speak with the interpreter after sessions to learn the cultural meaning of her words
Relying on the interpreter as cultural expert behind the client's back can sideline her own account of her experience.
Rationale: Interpreted sessions add a third person and can shift power away from the client. Best practice is to use a trained interpreter rather than family, brief the interpreter beforehand, and speak directly to the client in the first person so that the relationship remains between counselor and client.
References: ACA Code of Ethics — A.2.c Developmental and Cultural Sensitivity · Sue & Sue (Counseling the Culturally Diverse) — Language barriers and use of interpreters
